                          Supplementary Information for
                    DISCOVERYAGENT: A Framework for
                        Scientist-centric Agentic AI

Supplementary Note 1: Execution agent implementation.
      The execution agent is an autonomous coding agent built on the claude_agent_sdk. Run
configurations assign it separately from the director and other orchestration components; its model
and turn cap therefore vary by campaign (Supplementary Note 41).
Tools and sandbox. To reduce accidental access to files and benchmark results outside the re-
search workspace, we implemented layered application-level containment around the execution
agent. Each session runs from a dedicated working directory; pre-tool hooks restrict direct file
writes and edits using both literal and symlink-resolved paths, authorize reads and searches through
sandbox-relative paths or configured read roots, and screen shell commands for common external
read and write patterns. A post-tool guard rechecks completed direct writes and edits, terminates
the session after a detected escape, and removes newly created external targets when their prior state
is known, while a separate URL hook blocks requests to known ProteinGym result sources.
Fresh sessions. By default the execution agent does not persist conversation state across queries;
each query rebuilds a fresh session, so independent queries do not accumulate one another’s context.
Continuity across sessions is provided by the procedural memory (Supplementary Note 3) rather
than by a shared conversation.
Summary generation. Internally the agent retains its full message and tool-call trace, but only a
concise summary is returned to the director. The summary is produced by having the agent write
a short Markdown report of what it did and found to a report file, which is then read back and
returned; the full trace is not surfaced to the director. A bounded number of turns per session caps
the work done within a single query.




                                                  2
Supplementary Note 2: Empirical memory pipeline.
      At the end of each research round, the director produces a report from that round’s queries
and saves it as round_report.md. The first round report initializes the empirical memory un-
changed. For every later round, the memory manager passes the previous accumulated report and
the new round report through three sequential agents.
Overall flow of the memory. Denote the report generated at the end of Round i as Ri , and denote
the cumulative report of the previous round as R[:i−1] . After Round 0, we assign the R0 report
that is passed to Round 1. After this, at Round i, we use the memory aggregation function M,
which takes the R[:i−1] and Ri and produces R[:i] , e.g., M(R[:i−1] , Ri ) = R[:i] . R[:i] serves as the
cumulative empirical memory from round-to-round, persisting findings over the research process.
Memory aggregation function. Our memory mechanism is adopted from the ACE context-
engineering architecture [1]. First, the integrator combines the two reports into a single document
organized as background, data characteristics, experiments and analyses, and discussion. It carries
forward prior material, inserts the new experiments, and retains both versions when related findings
differ. Second, the analyzer reads this integrated report and returns a structured list of redundant
passages, assigning each a risk level and listing the information that must survive consolidation.
Third, the refiner removes low-risk repetition and trims the report while preserving the specified
details. The refiner’s output replaces the previous accumulated report and is saved in the round
checkpoint as memory_report.md.
       Before the next round begins, DISCOVERYAGENT resets the round-local workflows and injects
the updated memory report as their background_report. The hypothesis-generation agent and
director each receive this background when beginning the new round, while the execution agents
receive only the specific queries dispatched by the director; this limits the execution agent’s context
to the queries dispatched by the director, which manages the project and tests hypotheses. When a
run is resumed, the latest completed memory report seeds the same memory manager before new
rounds are executed. The raw round reports, per-query checkpoints, and computational workspace
remain stored separately from empirical memory (Supplementary Note 44).




                                                   3
Supplementary Note 3: Procedural memory.
      Each execution query starts a fresh coding-agent session, but all sessions operate on the same
persistent research workspace. The codebase for each project can drift into hundreds of thousands of
lines of code for some long-running experiments. Procedural memory moves information between
these sessions through two files in that workspace: CLAUDE.md, a concise map of the project,
and DOCUMENTATION.md, a detailed catalog of the implementation. These two files can be seen
as active and passive memory, respectively. These files help each fresh execution-agent session
navigate the codebase and reduce redundant work.
      At the start of a query, the execution agent uses CLAUDE.md to identify the project layout,
principal entry points, import patterns, and reusable components. When it needs an exact interface
or implementation detail, it searches DOCUMENTATION.md by file path, symbol, parameter, or
domain term, then reads the referenced source code [2]. The agent can therefore move from a short
project map, to targeted technical documentation, to the implementation itself without carrying over
the preceding conversation.
      During the query, the execution worker reuses or extends the codebase and writes analy-
sis outputs to the workspace. The orchestration agent then delegates two documentation updates
in the same session. A claude_md_agent revises CLAUDE.md to reflect the current high-
level structure and commonly reused components, while a documentation_agent updates
DOCUMENTATION.md with detailed module, function, class, dependency, and usage information.
Both documentation agents read the shared workspace but can edit only their assigned document.
Their revisions persist with the code and are available to the next fresh execution session. The
source code remains the authoritative procedural state; the two memory files provide progressively
more detailed routes into it.




                                                 4
Supplementary Note 4: StarEmbed baseline task setup and inputs.
Prediction task and data released to baselines. The StarEmbed case study asks a baseline agent
to develop a seven-class variable-star classifier from irregular multi-band light curves. Baseline
agents received two Apache Arrow training shards containing 29,047 labeled rows and one Arrow
validation file containing 4,150 labeled rows. The top-level fields are sourceid, bands_data,
item_id, start, freq, period, objid, csdr1_id, and the target class_str. The tar-
get takes the seven string-valued codes {1, 2, 4, 5, 6, 8, 13}. The training counts are strongly imbal-
anced: 19,633, 2,984, 1,451, 3,368, 308, 982, and 321 rows, respectively, in that class order.
       Each row’s bands_data field contains up to three photometric time series, one each for
g, i, and r. For every band, target stores magnitude measurements, mjd stores observation
times, past_feat_dynamic_real stores one-sigma magnitude errors, feat_dynamic_
real stores inter-observation time gaps, and length stores the observation count. The bands are
sparse and sampled at irregular, non-aligned times. The accompanying dataset description spec-
ified this schema and identified the catalog period and identifiers, but did not prescribe a feature
representation or classifier.
Held-out data. We withheld an 8,301-row test split from all baseline agents. The final common
comparison uses the 7,955 test rows having more than 10 observations in both r and g. These rows
represent 7,944 unique source identifiers; we retain the original multiplicity of the 11 repeated
identifiers. Test labels were used only by our evaluation pipeline after model and representation
selection.
Baseline prompts, tools, and budgets. We supplied Kosmos and AutoDiscovery with the same
task text and dataset description. The Coding agent loop baseline received the task once and then
received the exact continuation “Continue the research. Investigate a new path related to the orig-
inal task description and dataset.” four times. It used Sonnet 4.5, ran for 625 turns, and had no
verifier or human intervention. We configured AutoDiscovery for 40 experiments (10 initial experi-
ments and 30 refinements) and retained all other user-visible defaults. We submitted one prompt to
Kosmos and used its platform-managed defaults; the interface did not expose a user-configurable
compute budget. For AI-Scientist-v2, the run harness constructed the realized topic from the same
StarEmbed dataset and task descriptions.
       Kosmos and AutoDiscovery were accessed through hosted, minimally configurable interfaces:
each accepted a prompt and task data, but neither exposed nor permitted selection of the underly-
ing model. We report the observable interface inputs, execution record, returned artifacts, and
the downstream procedure used to select and evaluate a predictive representation. Supplementary
Table 4 summarizes each baseline’s harness, budget, and model.
AI-Scientist-v2 run and candidate selection. AI-Scientist-v2 uses agentic tree search to imple-
ment and refine multiple generated research ideas [3]. We configured the StarEmbed run to gener-
ate four finalized ideas with three reflection steps and to execute each idea through a four-stage tree
search capped at 3, 3, 2, and 2 iterations. The run used one independent Stage-1 draft, one best-
node seed, four workers, a 3,600-s timeout per experiment node, and an 8-h scheduler allocation
with one GPU and 128 GB of memory. Sonnet 4.5 served as both the ideation and code-generation
model, while GPT-4o-2024-11-20 (as was default in the codebase) supplied feedback during tree


                                                  5
search. The four candidates investigated physics-informed multiband uncertainty, cross-band self-
supervision, causal interpretable attention, and phase-folded prototype networks.
      The run harness intentionally omitted AI-Scientist-v2’s paper-writing and review stages. On
the following day, we assembled a report for each candidate from its proposal and stage summaries,
then scored each candidate with AI-Scientist-v2’s own GPT-4o-2024-11-20 reviewer. The reviewer
assigned an overall score of 4/10 to the causal interpretable-attention candidate and 3/10 to each
of the other candidates; all four received a reject decision. We selected the highest-scoring causal
interpretable-attention architecture for a subsequent transfer implementation.
AI-Scientist-v2 itself ran only synthetic five-class experiments and saved no model weights; its re-
ported StarEmbed prediction vector was instead produced by the later transfer implementation on
the real dataset. We document these distinct provenance and model-selection decisions in Supple-
mentary Note 5.
      We retained all user-visible defaults for Kosmos and AutoDiscovery except for the stated
AutoDiscovery experiment count.




                                                 6
Supplementary Note 5: StarEmbed baseline models, selection, and implementation.
Selection rule. We traced each baseline candidate to an agent report and its available computational
artifacts, then fixed one model and representation for each comparator before scoring the held-out
test set. We followed explicit run-level selections where available and otherwise required a com-
plete, task-aligned configuration supported by validation evidence from the run. Supplementary
Table 2 provides the selected model, representation, and performance for each comparator. For
Kosmos and AutoDiscovery, the named predictive model denotes the model developed from the
returned research artifacts, not the unexposed backend model that operated the hosted service.
Coding agent loop. The Coding agent loop explicitly identified its hybrid ensemble as best. We
regenerated its inputs with the unmodified run extractors, reproduced the reported validation met-
rics, and loaded the saved scaler, encoder, and fitted XGBoost and LightGBM classifiers without
retraining. The saved predictor standardizes the cleaned input and averages the two classifiers’
probabilities with validation-selected weights of 0.8 and 0.2.
Coding agent@1. The model-selection section chose the Random Forest as the best single model
based on validation accuracy and deployment simplicity, and the run saved it as its best model. A
separate summary table called a hard-voting Random Forest–XGBoost–LightGBM ensemble best
overall, while the text recommended that ensemble only when minority-class recall outweighed
inference cost. We follow the explicit selection and score the stored Random Forest predictions
without retraining.
Kosmos. We selected the 175-feature LightGBM because it was the report’s principal controlled
in-survey configuration and showed a reproducible gain from phase-resolved morphology: across
ten seeds, adding 84 phase-folded features to 91 period, per-band, and colour statistics increased
validation macro-F1 from 0.8173 ± 0.0016 to 0.8597 ± 0.0042 during Kosmos’s research process.
The selected representation contains four Fourier amplitudes, four Fourier phases, and the mean and
standard deviation within ten phase bins for each of r, interpolated g, and g − r, in addition to the
91 baseline features. We reproduced the reported recipe using inverse-frequency sample weights
and a 500-tree LightGBM with learning rate 0.05, 31 leaves, row and feature subsampling of 0.8,
and native missing-value handling, then used the fixed predictor for held-out scoring.
       The Kosmos StarEmbed run comprised 50 analysis trajectories. We inspected every note-
book for labeled data from outside the released training and validation splits. In its 19th trajectory,
Kosmos tested whether its classifier generalized beyond the StarEmbed sample, which pairs Zwicky
Transient Facility (ZTF) photometry with Catalina catalog labels. Following its own research objec-
tive, which directed it to search public archives for an external validation set, it assembled a labeled
sample from the ZTF catalog of periodic variable stars [4]. After mapping ZTF variability types
onto the seven StarEmbed classes, the sample contained 348 sources (48 semiregular and Mira vari-
ables and 50 sources for each remaining class) with g- and r-band light curves. The catalog and
light curves remained in the workspace, and 26 later trajectories loaded them.
       Many of these later trajectories built hierarchical classifiers that route each source by its
period: fixed period thresholds assign some classes directly, and a general classifier trained on
StarEmbed data handles the remaining sources. In at least two trajectories, Kosmos selected these
thresholds by scoring candidate values against the ZTF labels. In the clearest instance, it swept


                                                   7
the threshold that assigns semiregular and Mira variables from 5 to 70 days and the threshold that
assigns Cepheids from 5 to 15 days, and retained the combination with the highest ZTF macro F1
(0.396, at 50 and 5 days). Other trajectories inspected ZTF period distributions and per-class rout-
ing purity while revising their rules. Because the same labels guided threshold selection and served
as the evaluation target, performance on the additional sample from these analyses is optimistically
biased, and we do not treat it as an independent test of generalization. We provided Kosmos only
the StarEmbed training and validation splits and evaluated every system after its run on the withheld
StarEmbed test split. The selected Kosmos classifier was developed before the additional-sample
analysis and did not use that sample. We therefore retain Kosmos in the StarEmbed comparison.
AutoDiscovery. We selected refinement experiment r28 because it defines a complete, determinis-
tic representation for the full seven-class task, retains every source, and received direct support from
the run’s controlled validation experiment. Using a fixed 100-tree Random Forest, r28’s ten-feature
representation attained 89.16% validation accuracy, and AutoDiscovery assigned it posterior sup-
port 0.9375. The representation comprises the catalog period and the mean, standard deviation,
and full peak-to-peak amplitude of the raw observations in each of g, i, and r, with three zeros for a
missing band. For held-out scoring, we selected XGBoost by validation balanced accuracy and fit
500 trees with learning rate 0.05, depth 6, row and feature subsampling of 0.9, and balanced sample
weights.
Expert HCF. For the expert hand-crafted feature (HCF) comparator, we fit Random Forest, linear
and radial-basis-function support vector machine, 15-nearest-neighbour, XGBoost, and LightGBM
classifiers on 28,001 training objects and assessed them on 3,968 validation objects. Each model
used the same 138-dimensional representation: 69 FATS and light_curve features for each of
the g and r bands, spanning magnitude-distribution, variability, period, cadence, and harmonic de-
scriptors but no cross-band colour features. We fit median imputation on the training split for the
Random Forest, support vector machines, and nearest-neighbour classifier, and additionally stan-
dardized the inputs to the scale-dependent support vector machines and nearest-neighbour classifier.
To preserve the published expert comparator rather than define a new HCF–classifier combination,
we report its Random Forest: 500 trees with balanced class weights, square-root feature sampling,
minimum leaf size one, and seed 0.
AI-Scientist-v2. Supplementary Note 4 records the reviewer-based architecture selection. We use
a fixed prediction vector from a subsequent real-data transfer training because AI-Scientist-v2 itself
produced neither a real-data model nor predictions.
Common evaluation and uncertainty. Using the held-out subset defined in Supplementary Note 4,
we aligned predictions by sourceid and report balanced accuracy, the mean of the seven class
recalls. One-standard-error intervals come from 2,000 bootstrap resamples of fixed row-level pre-
dictions. We compare DISCOVERYAGENT with all six baselines using 100,000 within-row predic-
tion swaps and apply Holm correction across the six comparisons (Supplementary Table 1). All
seven headline comparators are scored on the same 7,955 rows (7,944 unique sources), retaining
the original repeated-source multiplicities. The 1.11-percentage-point difference between DISCOV-
ERYAGENT and Kosmos is not statistically significant in this paired comparison (Holm-adjusted
p = 0.194).




                                                   8
Supplementary Note 6: The DISCOVERYAGENT StarEmbed classifier and its held-out evalua-
tion.
      This note specifies DISCOVERYAGENT’s selected StarEmbed model and held-out scoring.
Comparator details are in Supplementary Note 5, and the research trajectory is summarized in
Supplementary Note 46.
Selected model. The evaluated model is the flat seven-class LightGBM classifier over the full 46-
feature representation: seventeen observed-magnitude and phase-folded Fourier features in each of
the r and g bands, the base-ten logarithm of the catalog period, and eleven phase-resolved colour
features (Supplementary Table 3). It is flat in the sense that all seven classes are predicted by one
model, rather than through the hierarchical routing variants the agent also tested and rejected. We
generated these features with the agent’s own single-band and colour-curve extraction procedures.
When the model was fixed. The 46-feature configuration was named the best overall model in a
Round 2, query 9 report before the test set was touched; the held-out evaluation was implemented
months later. The trace also contains a separate production recommendation of an 18-feature r-band
model, on the grounds of simplicity at a cost of roughly 0.6 percentage points of cross-validated bal-
anced accuracy, and a third document naming a 17-feature classifier. We report the model the agent
identified as best overall rather than the one it recommended for deployment, which is the selection
rule applied to the comparators; the one exception, Coding agent@1, is explained in Supplementary
Note 5.
Training for the held-out evaluation. We trained the DISCOVERYAGENT-selected LightGBM using
the agent’s Model C parameters: depth 6, learning rate 0.1, minimum child size 20, L1 regulariza-
tion 0.1, L2 regularization 1.0, balanced class weights, and seed 42. This training was done post
hoc because DISCOVERYAGENT did not retain a saved model during its research trace. Its original
comparison fit 500 trees without early stopping; a Round 2 report listed validation-fold early stop-
ping among proposed next steps, which we implemented with a 500-tree cap. Median imputation
and standardization were fitted on the 27,999 eligible training objects, and multiclass log loss on
the 3,967 eligible validation objects stopped training at 155 trees with patience 50 (205 rounds eval-
uated). We evaluated this early-stopped model directly, without refitting on the validation objects.
Test split and metrics. The 8,301-object test split was not exposed during research or tree-count
selection. We retained 7,955 objects with more than ten observations in both r and g, representing
7,944 unique sources. The classifier attains 86.23% balanced accuracy (Supplementary Table 2).
Uncertainty is one standard deviation over 2,000 bootstrap resamples of fixed row-level predictions
with seed 0. Paired comparisons use 100,000 within-row swaps with Holm correction (Supplemen-
tary Table 1).




                                                  9
Supplementary Note 7: Exclusion of Kosmos from the ProteinGym comparison.
ProteinGym. The Kosmos ProteinGym run comprised 62 analysis trajectories. We searched every
trajectory notebook for network requests.
       What was retrieved. In the 28th trajectory by completion time, Kosmos set out to test whether
MSA Transformer scores added signal to its variant-effect model. It attempted to download Pro-
teinGym’s published zero-shot score archive (version 1.1) from the benchmark’s distribution site,
marks.hms.harvard.edu. When TLS certificate verification failed, it disabled verification
and streamed individual members of the remote archive, extracting the published MSA Transformer
and ESM-2 650M scores for all 217 assays. The archive contains the precomputed predictions of the
published baselines for every variant in each assay, together with the experimental DMS_score
and DMS_score_bin labels. We matched the archive against our held-out split and found all
140,810 test variants, with their labels, across all 217 assays.
       Subsequent use. Retrieval was not an isolated event. Of the 34 trajectories that followed, 20
issued requests for the same archive, and 15 loaded published score files into their analyses, using
them as input features, as comparison baselines, or both. Three trajectory workspaces retained
a local copy of the full 1.9 GB archive, which includes the scores of all published ProteinGym
baselines. The supplied instructions stated that the held-out test set was not accessible but did not
explicitly prohibit internet access.
       Why the run was excluded. Once this archive entered the workspace, the test labels were
available to every later analysis, and the run’s accumulated state could no longer be certified as blind
to the evaluation. For DISCOVERYAGENT, we recovered from the analogous event by restoring a pre-
leak checkpoint and resuming (Supplementary Note 17). The hosted Kosmos platform provides no
rollback to an earlier research stage, so we could not remove the information and continue from a
clean state. We therefore exclude Kosmos from all ProteinGym figures and per-assay comparisons.




                                                  10
Supplementary Note 8: ProteinGym benchmark, baseline agents, and evaluation.
Benchmark split and input controls. All systems received the same research bundle, exposed
through symlinks in their workspaces. We used ProteinGym’s precomputed contiguous-region five-
fold assignment, holding out fold 0 and using folds 1–4 for research. The evaluation therefore tests
within-assay generalization to non-overlapping residue positions; no common validation split was
provided, and each system selected internal validation data from the research variants. We omitted
test files and labels from the released bundle. After a superseded DA-Auto round and the Kosmos
run independently retrieved published ProteinGym archives, we restored DA-Auto to the clean end-
of-Round-5 checkpoint and excluded Kosmos (Supplementary Notes 17 and 7). We were unable to
resume Kosmos because the implementation does not allow mid-round resumption.
Prompt delivery. Prompt delivery differed across systems. Coding agent@1 received the task in
a single-session coding interface, AI-Scientist-v2 received it as an ideation topic, and the hosted
systems received interface-specific variants of the same task and dataset description. Run configu-
rations, realized budgets, and hardware allocations are reported once in Supplementary Table 36.
Baseline predictors. We evaluated the final deployed predictor produced by each coding-agent run.
The Sonnet loop is represented by per-variant predictions. The Opus loop rank-normalizes 103 fea-
tures within each assay, stacks a Ridge prediction into three-seed LightGBM and XGBoost ensem-
bles, and averages the boosted-tree outputs. We corrected four train–inference feature mismatches
before evaluation; this changes mean Spearman from 0.4954 to 0.4961 and median Spearman from
0.5298 to 0.5302. Coding agent@1’s 30-feature Ridge predictor has median Spearman −0.096.
       AutoDiscovery produced separate hypothesis tests rather than one integrated model, so its
comparator combines the substitution, conservation, and residue-property signals that the run
reported as supported. It produces scorable predictions for 215 of 217 assays. The conservation
signal requires each variant to map to a column of the assay’s multiple-sequence alignment. The
released bundle contains no alignment for LYAM1_HUMAN_Elazar_2016, and our imple-
mentation indexes alignment columns by residue position after removing insert columns, so all
UBE4B_MOUSE_Starita_2013 variants (residues 1072–1173) fall outside the 897 retained
columns. Both assays are therefore unscored on the training and held-out splits, and we report
AutoDiscovery on the remaining 215. Kosmos is not assigned a predictive result because its run
retrieved published benchmark scores and labels (Supplementary Note 7).
AI-Scientist-v2 selection and reconstruction. AI-Scientist-v2 finalized ten ideas and passed each
through its four-stage tree search; Supplementary Table 36 reports its stage caps, node timeout,
hardware, and wall-clock allocation. The remaining configuration used three reflection steps per
idea, up to 32 generation attempts, five search steps, one independent Stage-1 draft, one best-node
seed, four workers, debug probability 0.5, and maximum debug depth three. The code agent used
a 12,000-token limit and temperature 1.0; GPT-4o-2024-11-20 supplied feedback with an 8,192-
token limit at temperature 0.5 and generated reports at temperature 1.0. The run used one-fold
validation and one synthetic dataset, disabled data preview and prediction exposure, and linked the
read-only ProteinGym inputs into each workspace.
      We selected structural-residual boosting because it achieved the highest internal best-node
validation metric among the ten candidates (ρ = 0.9483). This metric was the sole model-selection


                                                11
criterion; we did not select the model using held-out test performance or the later reviewer scores.
Because the released training tables lack the pretrained protein-language-model score required by
the proposed method, we implemented the candidate’s documented fallback: a global LightGBM
regressor on mutation-level amino-acid-property changes, absolute changes, wild-type and mutant
properties, polarity, relative position, distance to the nearest terminus, and mutation count. We
standardize these inputs, pool training variants across assays, and fit 200 boosting rounds with 63
leaves, learning rate 0.05, row and column subsampling 0.8, and seed 42. This reconstruction yields
mean Spearman −0.0590 and median Spearman −0.0564, with negative correlations on 137 assays
and no sign inversion.
       The selected candidate’s internal metric does not estimate real-data generalization because
its synthetic experiment constructed the protein-language-model feature as the target plus residual
noise. We therefore use the leakage-free held-out result from the reconstructed selected model in
all benchmark comparisons.
Scoring and paired comparisons. For each system, we compute Spearman correlation between
predicted and measured effects within each assay on held-out variants, then summarize the per-assay
correlations by their median and mean. We compute full-list normalized discounted cumulative gain
(NDCG) with sklearn.metrics.ndcg_score, shifting measured scores within each assay
by their minimum when needed to obtain nonnegative relevance values and averaging assays with
equal weight. We compare agent systems with two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank tests over paired
per-assay correlations and Holm correction. Supplementary Tables 5 and 6 contain the results.
Reference comparison. The 106 ProteinGym references comprise 95 zero-shot and 11 supervised
predictors. We score every reference on the same 140,810 fold-0 variants used to evaluate the agents.
For zero-shot references, we compute per-assay Spearman correlation from ProteinGym’s published
per-variant predictions restricted to fold 0. For supervised references, we use ProteinGym’s out-of-
fold predictions under the contiguous five-fold split; restricted to fold 0, these come from models
trained on folds 1–4, the same partition available to the agents. We matched every fold-0 variant
and its label to ProteinGym’s split files. Fold 0 is harder than average for the references: Kermut’s
median ρ is 0.585 on fold 0 versus 0.644 in ProteinGym’s five-fold summary. We use one held-
out fold because cross-validation would expose each fold to the agents during research on the other
folds, allowing them to optimize against previously observed evaluation data. A fixed holdout keeps
the test set isolated.
       Supplementary Table 7 reports the assays on which DA-Steer exceeds the strongest reference
by more than 5%.




                                                12
Supplementary Note 9: DA-Steer model architecture.
      DA-Steer maps each amino-acid substitution to a scalar variant-effect score with a 98-feature
LightGBM regressor. The representation combines sequence-model, substitution, evolutionary,
and structural information, while the training target is the DMS score standardized within each as-
say. The final model was trained on 555,501 variants from 217 assays; evaluation uses 140,810
held-out variants at non-overlapping residue positions from the same assays, so its predictions are
interpreted as within-assay rankings rather than calibrated cross-assay fitness values.
Sequence and evolutionary features. The first 57 inputs comprise one ESM-2 650M wild-type-
marginal log-likelihood ratio [5], 20-dimensional one-hot encodings for each of the wild-type
and mutant amino acids, changes in hydrophobicity, molecular weight, charge, and side-chain
volume, a BLOSUM62 substitution score, four local AlphaFold2 structure descriptors, five
position or protein-level descriptors, and two fixed intercept slots. Nine additional multiple-
sequence-alignment (MSA) inputs describe conservation, Shannon entropy, wild-type and mutant
residue frequencies, gap fraction, whether the query residue aligns to an insertion, and the
wild-type, mutant, and difference position-specific scoring-matrix values. MSA frequencies use a
pseudocount of 0.5.
Graph-attention structural representation. For each AlphaFold2 structure, residues form nodes
in a contact graph and two residues are connected when their Cα atoms are less than 10 Å apart.
Each node contains a 20-dimensional amino-acid encoding, normalized pLDDT, relative sequence
position, and backbone ϕ and ψ angles; edges contain Euclidean and normalized sequence distances.
A three-layer graph attention network (GAT) [6] uses four attention heads in its first two layers, one
head in its final layer, 64 hidden dimensions, 0.1 dropout, and 12,417 trainable parameters. The
GAT produces a 32-dimensional embedding at the mutated residue, completing the 57+9+32 = 98
feature vector. We trained the GAT only on the training split using per-assay standardized DMS
targets and early stopping.
LightGBM predictor. The final regressor uses 63 leaves, learning rate 0.03, maximum depth 10,
minimum child count 10, row and feature subsampling rates of 0.7, ℓ1 = ℓ2 = 0.05, 1,847 trees,
and random seed 42. Missing MSA or structure-derived values remain missing and are handled
natively by LightGBM.




                                                 13
Supplementary Note 10: DA-Auto model architecture.
       DA-Auto’s evaluated model combines six zero-shot model families with a further Trancep-
tion blend for six designated proteins. It combines sequence, structure-conditioned, and MSA-
conditioned mutation scores, with per-category blend coeﬀicients fitted on internal validation data
that the agent selected from the training variants rather than a supervised mutation-level regressor.
Sequence-conditioned scores. ESM-2 650M [5] and an ensemble of five ESM-1v 650M check-
points [7] use masked-marginal scoring. For a substitution at position i, the wild-type residue is
masked and the model produces

                             sseq = log p(xmut
                                           i                    i | x\i ),
                                               | x\i ) − log p(xwt

so positive values favor the mutant residue in the surrounding sequence context. The remaining
components are ESM-IF1 [8], ProteinMPNN, MSA Transformer, and SaProt. ESM-IF1 and Pro-
teinMPNN condition on AlphaFold2 backbones; MSA Transformer conditions on an A2M align-
ment; and SaProt combines sequence information with Foldseek 3Di structural tokens.
Ensembling. The first stage blends ESM-2, ESM-1v, ESM-IF1, ProteinMPNN, and MSA
Transformer using separate Nelder–Mead-optimized weights for each assay category. It then
rank-normalizes the base score and SaProt score within protein and combines them with category-
specific SaProt coeﬀicients. Finally, it rank-normalizes again and blends Tranception for six
designated proteins, with weights 0.125 or 0.200. Score availability determines the available
components and their renormalized weights. On the 217-assay held-out-position test set, the
evaluated cache attained mean per-assay Spearman 0.4806, median 0.5312, and mean NDCG
0.9479.




                                                  14
Supplementary Note 11: Prediction-level comparison with existing variant-effect predictors.
      We examined whether DA-Steer’s performance could be explained by reproducing the be-
havior of an existing variant-effect predictor. All variant-effect predictors are applied to the same
mutations and seek to recover the same underlying assay signal, so substantial correlation is ex-
pected even among methods with distinct implementations. Moreover, assays differ in how readily
that shared signal can be predicted. A raw Spearman correlation with a reference variant-effect
predictor (VEP) therefore cannot distinguish reproduction of that VEP from common recovery of
mutation-effect structure. Instead, we asked whether DA-Steer was unusually similar to any one
existing predictor after calibrating similarity against other predictors on the same assay.
Assays and prediction alignment. We analyzed all 217 ProteinGym substitution assays, represent-
ing 186 proteins and 140,810 held-out variants. Within each assay, we aligned DA-Steer predictions
by mutation with all 106 ProteinGym v1.3 reference predictors: 95 zero-shot predictors and 11 su-
pervised predictors evaluated under ProteinGym’s contiguous five-fold split. We retained only the
supervised predictions for the same held-out fold used to evaluate DA-Steer and computed all pair-
wise Spearman correlations on those held-out variants. All 106 reference predictors had usable
scores in every assay. The primary analysis retained every assay and required at least five aligned
variants per correlation; the smallest held-out assay contained seven variants. As a sensitivity analy-
sis, we required at least 30 variants, which excluded six assays and retained 211 assays representing
183 proteins.
Assay-normalized pseudo-predictor comparison. To account for differences in assay and protein
predictability, we treated each reference predictor as a pseudo-DA model and calculated two within-
assay statistics: 1) its median correlation to the other references, which measures overall correlation
centrality, and 2) its maximum correlation to any reference, which measures nearest-neighbor sim-
ilarity. We converted DA-Steer’s statistics to percentiles of the 106 pseudo-predictor values within
each assay. For the model-level empirical null, we preserved predictor identity by averaging each
named reference predictor’s percentiles across all assays and comparing DA-Steer with these 106
fixed-predictor averages. We also averaged assay percentiles within protein before averaging across
proteins, preventing proteins represented by multiple assays from receiving greater weight.
       We separately measured whether one named reference repeatedly served as the nearest neigh-
bor. For DA-Steer and for each pseudo-DA predictor, we identified the closest reference within
every assay and calculated the fraction of assays assigned to its most frequent closest neighbor.
This concentration statistic tests whether a model repeatedly tracks one particular VEP, rather than
whether it shares signal with the predictor set generally.
Results. DA-Steer’s overall correlation centrality remained within the reference distribution (mean
within-assay percentile 44.7; model-level empirical p = 0.36). Protein-balanced aggregation pro-
duced a mean percentile of 43.5 (p = 0.32), consistent with the predictors capturing shared biologi-
cal and assay-level structure. DA-Steer’s nearest-neighbor similarity was lower: its mean percentile
was 22.2, its median percentile was 20.8, and 144 of 217 assays placed it at or below the 25th per-
centile. The fixed-predictor model-level comparison was suggestive but did not cross the conven-
tional significance threshold (p = 0.11); protein-balanced aggregation gave a mean percentile of
21.9 (p = 0.10).
      The identity of DA-Steer’s nearest reference was dispersed across 52 of the 106 predictors.

                                                  15
The supervised embeddings-augmented MSA Transformer and zero-shot ESM2-650M predictors
were tied as the most frequent, each closest on 23 of 217 assays (10.6%). This maximum share was
at the 6.6th percentile of the corresponding pseudo-VEP concentration distribution (model-level
empirical p = 0.075). The 30-variant sensitivity analysis was nearly unchanged: mean nearest-
neighbor percentile 21.7 (p = 0.10), 142 of 211 assays at or below the 25th percentile, and a
maximum closest-reference share of 10.9% (p = 0.075).
Performance beyond individual references. When all 106 ProteinGym reference predictors are
scored on the same held-out variants as the agents, DA-Steer exceeds all 106 ProteinGym estab-
lished predictors in Spearman ρ on 21 of 217 assays (Supplementary Table 7). These assays are
concentrated in organismal fitness readouts (12 of 21), seven of which measure viral replication,
while the remaining wins span stability (6), activity (2), and expression (1) assays. DA-Steer out-
performs every reference on the human tumor suppressor p53 (+5.1%) [9] and HIV-1’s envelope
protein Env, which enables viral entry into host cells (+7.2%) [10].
Interpretation. DA-Steer remains correlated with the reference set, as expected for predictors that
recover the same mutation-effect signal, but it does not exhibit an unusually close or persistent
match to any one reference predictor. The low nearest-neighbor percentiles and dispersed neighbor
identities therefore argue against simple reproduction of a single existing VEP.




                                                16
Supplementary Note 12: The audit interface.
      Participants in the human study’s “Audit” cohort reviewed completed research runs through
a browser-based interface rather than a terminal. The interface exposed the underlying evidence
while preventing participants from modifying the work under review.
Role-based access. A single application provided both the hypothesis-steering workflow and the
audit workspace. At login, the project credential determined which role the user received; users
could not switch roles within the interface, and audit credentials redirected all non-audit routes to
the audit workspace. The application rejected requests from audit users to generate, regenerate,
select, or edit hypotheses and reports.
Evidence available to auditors. The audit workspace combined a conversational pane with links
to the dataset description, agent-written research paper, research workspace, checkpoint browser,
and interface guide, each of which opened in a separate tab. The guide distinguished checkpoints,
which record results and decisions during the run, from the research workspace, which contains
the corresponding methods and computational artifacts. It also encouraged provenance-specific
questions, such as asking the auditor to identify the round and query in which a decision was made.
The checkpoint browser presented a read-only file tree, blocked path traversal, displayed only text-
like file types, and truncated long logs and tables.
Agent grounding. The audit agent could read both the research workspace and the checkpoint
tree. At the first turn, the application supplied the run’s accumulated memory report and, when
available, its research paper as initial context. The agent was instructed to use these documents for
orientation and to substantiate its answers with evidence from the implementation and checkpoints.
The application synchronized checkpoints from object storage to the serving host using the project
credential as the key.
Read-only operation. The auditor’s tool allow-list was limited to file reading, content search,
globbing, and shell access within a read-only environment; it provided no file-writing, editing, or
web-fetch tools. The study used a single general read-only auditor.




                                                 17
Supplementary Note 13: Corrupted-claim identification experiment.
       This note records the design of the experiment in which an auditor is asked to identify ar-
tificially corrupted claims in a completed research trace, for DISCOVERYAGENT, Kosmos, and the
Coding agent loop baseline on the StarEmbed task.
Claim extraction and corruption. For each of the three systems we extracted exactly 50 claims
from the report the system itself produced at the end of its research. For each system we then
generated five independent corruption cases; in every case exactly 25 of the 50 claims were cor-
rupted, selected by a seeded random sample so that the corrupted subset differs between cases but
the count does not. Corruptions were drawn from a fixed catalog of eight categories — numeric
alteration, reframing, effect-size inflation, limitation removal, method misstatement, significance
misstatement, validation misstatement, and sample-size alteration — with numeric and reframing
corruptions the two most common. We did not grade or balance corruption severity at construc-
tion, so this experiment controls the number and category catalog of corruptions rather than their
strength.
Auditor. Within each auditor-model comparison, every trace-source arm used the same read-only
coding-agent auditor. The auditor was run at a fixed model and reasoning effort in a read-only
sandbox with no network access and no memory or plugin state, and was asked to return one of three
verdicts per claim (supported, misrepresented, uncertain); only misrepresented
counts as a positive detection. Scoring is mechanical claim-identifier matching against the ground-
truth corruption list, with no LLM judge in the loop. Each of the 5 cases per system was audited in
3 replicates, giving 15 scored runs per trace source, 45 runs, and 2,250 scored verdicts per auditor
model and trace-access condition. We expanded the GPT-5.6-Sol evaluation to GPT-5.6-Terra and
GPT-5.6-Luna using the same reports, corruptions, prompts, response schemas, medium reasoning
effort, and Codex harness. Each additional model completed 45 validator-accepted trace-grounded
runs and 45 matched report-only runs; invalid outputs were retried, so reported F1 is conditional
on validator acceptance.
Report-only negative control. A matched control repeats the experiment with the trace mount
removed, so the auditor sees only the corrupted report. Everything else — the same 15 reports,
the same 25-of-50 corruptions, the same auditor model, reasoning effort, and response schema
— is unchanged, isolating access to the trace as the single manipulated variable (Supplementary
Figure 2).
Results. For GPT-5.6-Sol, mean run-level F1 is 0.965 ±0.008 for DISCOVERYAGENT, 0.918 ±0.013 for
Kosmos, and 0.900 ±0.006 for Coding agent loop. Averaging the three replicates within each corrup-
tion case (n = 5 per system; two-sided Welch’s t-test, Holm-adjusted across the three comparisons),
DISCOVERYAGENT exceeds Kosmos (p = 0.0022) and Coding agent loop (p = 3.0 × 10−5 ); Kosmos
and Coding agent loop do not differ detectably (p = 0.069). Because all cases draw corruptions
from the same 50 claims, these tests are conditional on each system’s report. In the report-only
control, mean F1 decreases to 0.268, 0.135, and 0.315 respectively (pooled over all 45 runs: F1
0.244, precision 0.563, recall 0.156). Supplementary Table 8 reports precision, recall, and F1 for
all three auditor models under both conditions; Figure 3d compares all three auditor models.



                                                18
Supplementary Note 14: Provenance exposed in the corrupted-claim audit.
       For each corrupted-claim audit, we exposed the corrupted report and the complete native re-
search trace as separate read-only filesystem roots. We did not provide the honest report, corruption
key, outputs from other auditors, external memory, or network access, and we did not reorganize
the traces into a shared representation. The auditor therefore evaluated three provenance structures
produced directly by the respective research systems.
DISCOVERYAGENT. The DISCOVERYAGENT trace contained two linked provenance trees. The first
organized agent state by round and query: each query directory contained a serialized workflow
checkpoint and a human-readable query–result transcript, while each round contained its configu-
ration, execution log, round report, and accumulated memory report. The audit report corresponded
to the end of Round 3, the last completed round. The second contained the version-controlled re-
search workspace, including its git history, source and analysis scripts, notebooks, intermediate and
processed data, model artifacts, results, reports, and figures. Together, the checkpoint tree recorded
what the agents observed and concluded at each step, while the research workspace recorded the
corresponding implementation and outputs.
Kosmos. The Kosmos export organized provenance as 50 identifier-named trajectory capsules.
Each capsule contained an executed notebook and a memory transcript, together with any figures,
tabular outputs, serialized models, or other artifacts produced by that trajectory. The mounted
export also contained the project-level agent–user dialog, used to recover the overall research se-
quence, and the project files. Auditors were instructed to account for all 50 capsules and to verify
claims against executed notebook outputs or standalone artifacts rather than relying on memory text
alone.
Coding agent loop. The Coding agent loop trace preserved one cumulative working directory span-
ning the initial research session and four continuation sessions. Its provenance included the raw and
cleaned chronological interaction logs, message and tool-call records, cumulative summaries and
reports, executable analysis and training scripts, data directories, primary JSON and CSV results,
saved predictions and model objects, and generated figures. Auditors reconstructed the five phases
from the chronological log, treated the narrative summaries only as navigation aids, and used direct
tool outputs and generated artifacts to determine whether an analysis was executed and which result
version supported a claim.
      Across all three arms, the auditor could read and search the entire mounted trace and perform
read-only calculations, but could not modify it. No separately curated evidence ledger or cross-
system synthesis was supplied; the navigation instructions only required the auditor to follow each
trace’s native organization and verify proposed discrepancies in primary artifacts.




                                                 19
Supplementary Note 15: Verification effort for the inserted corruptions.
      Corruptions in the corrupted-claim experiment (Supplementary Note 13) were authored
against each system’s own report and were not balanced for verification effort. We therefore
graded every inserted corruption post hoc and checked whether the audit comparison depends on
the effort required to verify it. We did not rebalance or reselect corruptions.
Grading. Nine independent, trace-blind language-model graders (three per shuffled batch of 48)
graded all 144 distinct corrupted claims (46 for DISCOVERYAGENT, 49 for Kosmos, 49 for Coding
agent loop). Graders saw only the honest claim, the corrupted claim, and the anonymized 50-claim
list, with system identities masked. Each item received an integer verification-effort score from 1 to
5, a label for the evidence needed to refute it, and an indicator of whether the corruption contradicted
another claim in the same report. A score of 1 denotes a large change to a headline result visible
in a summary artifact; 2 denotes a directly reported but modest or secondary change; 3 requires a
specific intermediate artifact or small calculation; 4 requires reconstructing a procedure from logs
or code; and 5 requires reasoning across several artifacts or establishing that claimed work was never
performed. The four evidence categories are direct artifact comparison, calculation from artifacts,
establishing that claimed work is absent, and interpretive assessment. Agreement was high (mean
pairwise Spearman 0.85 for the score; 92% agreement on the evidence category), and consensus is
the per-item mean score and majority categorical label.
Results. Mean verification-effort scores were similar across systems (2.49, 2.50, and 2.59; Supple-
mentary Table 9), but the Coding agent loop catalog had a larger complex-to-verify tail (18% of
variants scored ≥ 3.5) and more variants requiring the auditor to establish that claimed work was
absent (37%). This tail reflects the content of that report, a project log whose procedural admis-
sions corrupt naturally into claims that a procedure was performed. DISCOVERYAGENT recall was
higher within the shared directly verifiable and intermediate strata, and a logistic model of detec-
tion on system and verification-effort score gave odds ratios of 0.29 for both comparators relative
to DISCOVERYAGENT (Supplementary Table 10). The graders were language models rather than
domain experts, and the ordinal score is partly anchored in how the truth would be recovered from
a trace. We do not consider this an indication of improper experiment design as by design, DISCOV-
ERYAGENT is built to be more auditable, meaning that these grades are partially conflated with the
construction of the provenance for each agent.




                                                  20
Supplementary Note 16: Human audit study protocol and participant instructions.
Recruitment and participants. We recruited advanced-undergraduate and PhD research students
through personal invitations by email or Slack. All participants had experience in machine learning
and its applications in biomedicine. We excluded students who had worked on DISCOVERYAGENT
or had detailed knowledge of the ProteinGym experiment. No participant had previously seen the
study paper, research trace, codebase, or inserted-error catalog, and none had participated in the
original research or the error-insertion process. Participants were not members of the authors’ lab-
oratories, collaborators, or coauthors. They knew that they would audit research generated by an
AI system and were generally aware of the structure of DISCOVERYAGENT. To preserve anonymity
in this small cohort, we report expertise only in aggregate and do not report demographic character-
istics. Participants were not compensated. Before participation, we informed participants that their
responses would be collected and reported and gave them the opportunity to withdraw.
Allocation and participant flow. The study included eight participants, four in the Audit cohort
and four in the Code cohort. All eight completed the study and were included in the analysis. We
recruited this small initial cohort because its members had relevant experience in machine learning
and biology.
Task and study conditions. All participants reviewed the same corrupted DA-Auto ProteinGym
paper and its research codebase in a single session. They were asked to identify inconsistencies or
errors as in a conference review and to record a short summary, location, and explanation for each
finding. Code participants received a form containing the instructions, a private GitHub instance
of the codebase, and an individual link to the paper. Audit participants received the corresponding
form and individual credentials for the read-only audit interface, which exposed the paper, code-
base, agent checkpoints, and audit agents. Participants used their own computers and software
environments, with study materials delivered through GitHub or the audit interface. They received
no training, demonstration, or practice task, but could ask questions before beginning; when asked,
we clarified only the expected task and response format. Both cohorts were prohibited from using
external AI tools, with the supplied audit interface as the sole exception for the Audit cohort. The
interface is described in Supplementary Note 12.
Timing and technical limitations. The one-hour limit covered reading, investigation, and record-
ing findings and was self-tracked and self-reported. All eight participants reported completing the
task within one hour. The only reported technical issue was intermittent high latency in the audit
interface caused by a third-party cloud-provider problem; this may have reduced the number of
findings that Audit participants could report within the time limit.
Outcomes and analysis population. We evaluated two primary outcomes: the total number of
findings reported by each participant and whether those findings matched the inserted corruptions.
Participants were not told these outcomes or the corresponding study hypothesis before completing
the task. The participant was the unit of analysis. The eight included responses contained 54 finding
blocks after empty templates, time summaries, and appended non-finding text were removed: 33
findings from Audit participants and 21 from Code participants. We aggregate matches as the
number of unique inserted-error identifiers recovered per participant, deduplicated against the 36
active errors. Supplementary Note 49 defines the error catalog, matching rubric, blinded repeated
judging, adjudication, and all scoring units.
                                                21
Supplementary Note 17: Sandbox enforcement, the ProteinGym leak, and leakage auditing.
      Predictive-modeling tasks are only meaningful if the agent never sees the held-out test labels.
This note records how the sandbox is enforced, how one run nonetheless obtained test data, and
what was changed in response.
Sandbox enforcement. The execution agent applies pre-tool hooks to direct file reads, writes, ed-
its, file globbing, content search, and shell commands. Direct write and edit targets must remain
within the sandbox root in both their literal absolute form and their symlink-resolved form, pre-
venting these tools from writing through a symbolic link to an external target. Direct reads and
searches are authorized when their literal or resolved paths fall within the sandbox or an explicit
read allow-list; accepting sandbox-relative paths supports the read-only data directories symlinked
into the workspace. For shell commands, relative paths are resolved from the sandbox root, and
the hook performs best-effort static screening of output redirection, tee, in-place edits, selected
file-modifying commands, and common file-reading commands before execution. Denials from the
file hooks are returned through both the SDK’s generic block response and its nested pre-tool per-
mission decision. A separate process-protection hook screens a narrow set of broad process-killing
and destructive command patterns. The system prompt additionally instructs the agent to treat the
sandbox as the project root and to access shared data only through the paths exposed within it.
How the leak occurred. The original sandbox hooks constrained file locations but did not inspect
shell commands for network addresses. In the automated ProteinGym run, the agent used shell
curl calls to retrieve the published ProteinGym baseline score archives directly from the bench-
mark’s distribution site into its working directory. The download occurred during Round 7. We
selected the clean end-of-Round-5 checkpoint for rollback and resumed execution at Round 6.
URL blocking. We added pre-tool hooks for shell and web-fetch requests. For shell commands, the
hook extracts literal HTTP and HTTPS addresses and also scans the complete command text; for
web-fetch requests, it parses the requested address directly. Host rules match a configured hostname
and all of its subdomains, while case-insensitive substring rules match URL paths and repository
identifiers that do not include a literal address, such as a snapshot_download call. The default
ProteinGym rules cover marks.hms.harvard.edu; the ProteinGym dataset paths and reposi-
tory identifiers on Hugging Face; ProteinGym records 13932633, 13936340, 14997691, and
15293562 on Zenodo; and the ProteinGym release and raw-content paths on GitHub. A match
returns an SDK-recognized denial before the tool executes. The execution-agent configuration can
replace either rule set or disable the URL hook explicitly; when enabled, session initialization
records the active configuration in the action log, and denied requests are logged separately. The
action logs record URL-blocker initialization in later DA-Auto, extragalactic astronomy, and func-
tional genomics sessions, but no URL-blocker denials in any campaign.
Filesystem hardening. We strengthened containment for direct write and edit tool calls at both pre-
tool and post-tool stages. The pre-tool hook records whether an out-of-sandbox target existed before
the attempted operation and returns an authoritative denial using the SDK permission schema. In
parallel, a post-tool guard rechecks every completed direct write and edit call. When the corre-
sponding pre-tool record establishes that an external target was newly created, the guard removes
the created file or directory; a pre-existing target, or one whose prior state cannot be established, is


                                                  22
retained to avoid deleting unrelated data. In either case, detection of a completed out-of-sandbox
direct write or edit terminates the execution session and records the event. The post-tool guard does
not observe filesystem changes performed inside a shell process; those calls instead rely on the best-
effort pre-execution screening described above. The shell-path checks resolve relative targets from
the sandbox working directory while preserving ordinary file-descriptor duplication and process-
substitution syntax. For the resumed ProteinGym run, the quarantined leaked directory was placed
outside the read allow-list, and an intermediate resume attempt that rediscovered this directory was
denied by the sandbox hook.
Rollback and recovery. Before resuming, we restored the tracked working directory to the final
pre-leak commit, removed the downloaded caches and interim files, and pruned the version-control
history so that unreachable objects did not retain the downloaded data. We restored agent state
independently by pointing the resume procedure to the clean end-of-Round-5 checkpoint. The
outer loop identifies the highest completed round from its integrated memory report and restarts
from the following round, while the query checkpoints also support resuming within a partially
completed round.
Kosmos. A comparable test-set download occurred in the Kosmos ProteinGym run and could not
be repaired; Supplementary Note 7 describes that run and the resulting exclusion.
Leakage auditing. The artifacts establish that sandbox enforcement was active in the reported
campaigns through recorded denials, and that URL-blocker initialization occurred in later DA-Auto,
extragalactic astronomy, and functional genomics sessions. After remediation, we used audit agents
to perform post hoc leakage checks on the models evaluated on held-out test sets, including the later
DA-Auto rounds and DA-Steer, and found no further test-data access. A train–test variant-overlap
test for the DA-Steer graph-attention branch additionally found no overlapping variants.




                                                 23
Supplementary Note 18: Extragalactic astronomy dataset and evaluation splits.
Source observations and initial sample. We construct the photometric-redshift dataset from galax-
ies with spectroscopic redshifts in DESI Data Release 1 [11]. The initial sample comprises three
groups of 83,333 galaxies: 1) dwarf galaxies from the DESI extragalactic dwarf catalog, excluding
objects with a nonzero DWARF_MASKBIT or observations from the emission-line galaxy program;
2) nearby candidates satisfying the surface-brightness and color selection of Darragh–Ford et al. at
z < 0.03 (Equations 1–2 of [12]); and 3) a random comparison sample representing the broader
galaxy population. All groups require a reliable spectroscopic redshift (ZWARN= 0), clean r-band
imaging (ALLMASK_R= 0), positive r-band flux, and z < 1.5. The dwarf and nearby groups
additionally require a confident spectral fit (∆χ2 > 40), while the nearby and comparison groups
apply the photometric quality cuts from Section 3.1 of [12]. The original dataset contains 242,614
objects. Each galaxy is paired with catalog photometry and a 128 × 128-pixel Legacy Survey DR9
image cutout in the g, r, and z bands at 0.262 arcsec pixel−1 .
Catalog photometry and Galactic extinction. The catalog photometry comes from the DESI
Legacy Imaging Surveys DR9 Tractor model-fit fluxes in the g, r, and z bands [13]. These fluxes,
fb for band b ∈ {g, r, z}, are in nanomaggies on the AB magnitude system. The original cam-
paign’s MAG_G, MAG_R, and MAG_Z inputs are mb = 22.5 − 2.5 log10 fb and are not corrected
for Galactic extinction. In contrast, the balanced external sample described below uses the cata-
log’s extinction-corrected magnitudes, mb,0 = 22.5 − 2.5 log10 (fb /Tb ), where Tb is the correspond-
ing MW_TRANSMISSION_G, MW_TRANSMISSION_R, or MW_TRANSMISSION_Z value. The
transmission factors encode foreground Milky Way dust attenuation, following the DR9 catalog
prescription1 .
Balanced external sample. We additionally construct a balanced sample of 199,294 DESI DR1
galaxies. We apply the same standard cleaning, including the photometric quality cuts from Section
3.1 of [12] and ZWARN= 0, followed by the continuous surface-brightness and color relations from
Equations 3–4 of [12] evaluated at z = 0.05. The sample contains all qualifying DESI galaxies at
0 < z < 0.05 and an equally sized random sample of galaxies at 0.05 ≤ z < 1.5 that pass the same
cuts. Thus, the original campaign evaluations define low redshift as z < 0.03, whereas the external
evaluation uses z < 0.05.
       The final test split contains 39,859 galaxies: 19,929 at z < 0.05 and 19,930 at higher redshift.
Because purity depends on class prevalence, PF-EffB3’s 88.4% purity at 95% completeness is spe-
cific to this selected, approximately balanced evaluation sample. Completeness is measured among
labeled low-redshift galaxies satisfying the evaluation-sample cuts; it does not establish complete-
ness for the full parent imaging population. Supplementary Table 12 distinguishes the samples,
target definitions, and uses.
Research and evaluation splits. During the scientist-steered campaign, the original dataset pro-
vides 181,959 usable training galaxies and 12,130 usable validation galaxies after removing two
and one negative-redshift records, respectively; some earlier validation analyses retained all 12,131
rows. The reported post-purge trajectory uses the validation split for hypothesis development. We
select candidate model recipes using the original validation split, then retrain them on the external
   1
       https://www.legacysurvey.org/dr9/catalogs/


                                                    24
training data with validation-based checkpoint selection before evaluating them on held-out test
data. The balanced external sample provides 151,463 training, 7,972 validation, and 39,859 held-
out test galaxies, with z < 0.05 as both its classification target and selection threshold; its test
set also forms the population analyzed in Supplementary Note 24. The original split also has a
48,522-galaxy held-out test set (3,054 at z < 0.03), which is excluded from the reported trajectory.
Because the external sample changes both the sample construction and the low-redshift threshold,
and its test split is balanced (19,929 of 39,859 galaxies at z < 0.05, versus ∼6% low-redshift in the
original test split), we do not compare raw metrics between the two datasets.
