                                               26
Supplementary Note 19: Scientist-steered extragalactic astronomy research trace.
      This note summarizes the 16-round research trajectory for the extragalactic astronomy study
(Rounds 0–15). Results below are in-loop validation findings for the z < 0.03 target, for which
P@95C denotes purity at 95% completeness. Supplementary Notes 18 and 22 describe the separate
held-out evaluation.
Round 0 — exploratory characterization. The agent characterized the image dimensions, photo-
metric distributions, class imbalance, and low-redshift target, then prepared the preprocessing and
evaluation pipeline used throughout the campaign. It also trained initial convolutional neural net-
work (CNN) models that jointly classified low-redshift galaxies and predicted continuous redshift.
Round 1 — classification objective. The agent tested whether focal classification and a small
robust-regression auxiliary loss would improve purity near the z = 0.03 boundary. The loss variants
performed similarly, suggesting that subsequent gains would require better representations or more
data rather than a different objective alone.
Round 2 — image backbone. The agent replaced ResNet18 with the larger EﬀicientNet-B3 to
test whether image-model capacity was limiting performance. The larger backbone shifted some
subgroup metrics but did not improve full-sample P@95C, so model scale alone did not resolve the
performance ceiling.
Round 3 — training-set scale. The agent tested whether the ceiling instead reflected the reduced
training subset used in earlier rounds. Expanding to all 181,959 training galaxies raised P@95C
from 9.50% to 11.69%, the largest single improvement observed during the campaign.
Round 4 — magnitude specialists. The agent trained separate classifiers and decision thresholds
for bright, intermediate, and faint galaxies, where image quality and class balance differ. These
specialists underperformed a shared model, so later rounds retained a unified predictor while con-
tinuing to examine performance across magnitude regimes.
Round 5 — self-supervised image features. The agent tested domain-specific self-supervised
pretraining as a way to improve image representations, particularly for faint galaxies. Full fine-
tuning rapidly overfit and worsened redshift regression, providing the first of several negative results
showing that stronger image encoders did not automatically improve the task.
Round 6 — photometry and image residuals. The agent asked whether images could refine a
coarse redshift estimate derived from five photometric features. Instead, the photometry-only mul-
tilayer perceptron (MLP) proved more robust than the tested image models and reached 12.79%
P@95C, motivating its use as the principal single-modality baseline in the main text. Its valida-
tion area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) reached 0.888 and remained
stable, whereas the image-based residual models peaked earlier at 0.868–0.876 and then declined
(Figure 4c).
Round 7 — conditional image information. To test whether images contained information beyond
photometry, the agent trained residual models on frozen image embeddings after accounting for the
MLP prediction. Real embeddings provided essentially no validation gain over shuffled controls,
strengthening the conclusion that photometry captured most of the accessible signal in the tested
representations.


                                                  27
Round 8 — alternative frozen features. The agent repeated this test using intermediate features
from self-supervised, ImageNet-pretrained, and random image backbones. None reliably improved
on the photometry-only MLP, focusing the next round on learning a stronger multimodal fusion
architecture rather than adding frozen image features.
Round 9 — multimodal fusion. Asked to construct the strongest image-using model from the
accumulated evidence, DISCOVERYAGENT introduced PhotoFusion, which strengthened the photo-
metric branch before combining it with learned image features. PhotoFusion became the first mul-
timodal model to exceed the MLP (13.11% versus 12.79% P@95C), establishing the architecture
highlighted in the main text.
Round 10 — boundary-aware contrastive learning. The agent tested whether photometry-
matched examples near the low-redshift boundary could improve the image representation through
contrastive learning. No contrastive setting improved the full sample, although one helped the
dwarf subgroup at a cost to other galaxies, so the method was not retained as a general solution.
Round 11 — checkpoint selection. The agent next tested checkpoint criteria that jointly reflected
classification and regression quality rather than selecting exclusively for low-redshift purity. Later
checkpoints reduced regression errors and catastrophic outliers with only a small loss in P@95C,
clarifying the trade-off between the two formulations emphasized in the main text.
Round 12 — probabilistic prediction. The agent replaced point regression with a 100-bin red-
shift distribution to represent uncertainty and broaden the predicted redshift range. The probabilis-
tic head reduced outliers and supported confidence estimates, but it slightly reduced P@95C and
required further calibration.
       In the analysis shown in Figure 4e, the agent computed the probability integral transform
(PIT) by evaluating each predicted cumulative redshift distribution at the spectroscopic redshift. It
reconstructed interior bin edges from adjacent bin-center midpoints and interpolated linearly within
each bin, assigning PIT values of zero or one to observations outside the predicted range. The
density histograms used 20 equal-width PIT bins and separated the 12,130 validation galaxies by
spectroscopic redshift: 763 at z < 0.03 and 11,367 at z ≥ 0.03. Because these groups condition on
the observed redshift, a calibrated model need not produce uniform PIT values within each group;
the uniform reference and subgroup Kolmogorov–Smirnov distances are descriptive and do not by
themselves establish miscalibration.
Round 13 — augmentation. The agent compared geometric, noise, and brightness augmentations
for the image branch. Flips and 90◦ rotations helped in one training configuration, whereas pho-
tometric perturbations hurt and the geometric gain did not persist across image sizes and training
lengths.
Round 14 — architecture scaling. The agent compared 12 configurations using ResNet, Con-
vNeXt, and vision-transformer backbones, varying the learning rate and photometric branch (Fig-
ure 4f). All models combined images with catalog photometry, using either a small auxiliary
magnitude MLP or the stronger PhotoFusion branch. None of the larger architectures surpassed
PhotoFusion-128 on full-sample P@95C, although some achieved higher dwarf-subgroup P@95C;
learning-rate changes produced larger shifts than backbone choice in the tested configurations.
Round 15 — consolidation. The agent extended the architecture comparison to a model matrix in-
cluding EﬀicientNet-B3, ResNet, and ConvNeXt backbones with classification, regression, or joint

                                                 28
objectives. The final round standardized predictions from 125 models after training 190 model con-
figurations across the research run and compared them under common metrics. The leading models
were closely separated, confirming PhotoFusion as the main architectural advance while showing
that the many tested losses, pretrained representations, augmentations, and larger backbones did
not break the approximately 13% validation ceiling. This consolidation provided the model recipes
subsequently retrained for the external evaluation.




                                               29
Supplementary Note 20: Single-modality baselines for the extragalactic astronomy evalua-
tion.
      The external evaluation compares the two DISCOVERYAGENT fusion recipes against a
photometry-only MLP and an image-only CNN. The recipes differ in architecture, training, and
preprocessing as well as input modality, so their comparison does not isolate the contribution
of fusion or morphological information. Supplementary Note 22 gives the shared retraining and
scoring protocol.
Photometry-only MLP. This is the model DISCOVERYAGENT built in Rounds 6–8 (Supplementary
Note 19) and used unchanged as the photometry baseline. It is the photometric-prior branch of
a four-condition prior-residual experiment, run with its image backbone disabled, so the redshift
and low-z logit are read directly off the photometry branch. The network takes five features—the
three normalized g, r, z magnitudes plus the derived g − r and r − z colors—through an MLP
of width 5 → 64 → 64 → 2, for 4,674 parameters in total. It is trained for 25 epochs at batch
size 256 with AdamW (learning rate 10−3 , cosine annealing to 10−6 ), gradient-norm clipping at
1.0, mixed precision, the standard low-redshift oversampling sampler, and no augmentation, under
a focal binary cross-entropy loss (γ = 2, α = 0.25) plus 0.1× Huber loss (δ = 0.02) on the
normalized redshift error (zpred − ztrue )/(1 + ztrue ). The checkpoint is the epoch with the highest
validation purity at 95% completeness, which was epoch 5 of 25.
Image-only CNN. The image baseline is a 12,588,834-parameter convolutional neural network that
takes only 128 × 128 three-band (g, r, z) image cutouts as input. It contains six convolution–batch-
normalization–ReLU blocks with channel widths 3 → 32 → 64 → 128 → 256 → 512 → 512, a
fully connected trunk of 8,192 → 1,024 → 256 with dropout 0.3, and two linear heads for redshift
regression and the low-z logit. We train the model with channel-normalized images and random
horizontal flips, vertical flips, and 90◦ rotations, using AdamW (learning rate 10−3 , weight decay
10−4 ), batch size 128, and a loss that combines normalized Huber loss (δ = 0.02) with binary cross-
entropy at unit weight. We select checkpoints and apply early stopping using validation σNMAD
with patience 15. Training stopped after 46 of a maximum 100 epochs, with the best checkpoint at
epoch 31 (validation σNMAD = 0.0203). The g, r, z magnitudes are retained only as metadata for
the magnitude- and color-stratified analyses and never enter the network.




                                                 30
Supplementary Note 21: DiscoveryAgent-developed photometric-redshift models.
      PF-EffB3 and V3-Binned are model architectures and training recipes developed by DISCOV-
ERYAGENT during the extragalactic astronomy campaign, rather than external architectures intro-
duced for the held-out evaluation. This note records their original implementations on the research
dataset, where low redshift was defined as z < 0.03. Supplementary Note 19 describes the ex-
periments that produced these models, Supplementary Table 13 summarizes their configurations,
and Supplementary Note 22 describes how we subsequently transferred the implementations to the
external dataset.
PF-EffB3. PF-EffB3 is the joint classification–regression member of the PhotoFusion model matrix
with an ImageNet-pretrained EﬀicientNet-B3 image backbone. The image branch produces a 1,536-
dimensional representation of the 128 × 128 three-band (g, r, z) image, while a separate batch-
normalized multilayer perceptron (MLP) maps five photometric features—the normalized g, r, z
magnitudes and the g − r and r − z colors—through widths 5 → 64 → 128 → 128. After
concatenation, separate heads map the 1,664-dimensional fused representation through a 256-unit
ReLU layer and dropout of 0.3 to either a low-redshift logit or a point redshift estimate; the fused
heads operate in FP32 during mixed-precision training. Random horizontal flips, vertical flips, and
90◦ rotations augment the images. The training objective is focal binary cross-entropy (γ = 2, α =
0.25), with a positive-class weight computed from the training-set prevalence, plus 0.1 times Huber
loss (δ = 0.02) on the normalized redshift residual. The model uses AdamW with learning rate
10−3 and weight decay 10−4 , batch size 128, gradient-norm clipping at 1.0, and cosine annealing
over 15 epochs; the checkpoint with the highest validation purity at 95% completeness is retained.
V3-Binned. V3-Binned is a probabilistic PhotoFusion variant with an ImageNet-pretrained
ResNet18 image backbone and the same 5 → 64 → 128 → 128 photometry branch. The
512-dimensional image representation and 128-dimensional photometry representation are
concatenated, then passed through batch-normalized fusion layers of widths 640 → 256 → 128
with dropout of 0.3 and 0.2. One head predicts the low-redshift logit, while the second predicts
a distribution over 100 nonuniform redshift bins: 30 fine bins span 0 ≤ z < 0.05 and 70 coarser
bins span 0.05 ≤ z ≤ 0.6; the reported point estimate is the probability-weighted mean of the
bin centers. Training uses MixUp with α = 0.2, stochastic depth increasing to 0.15 across the
ResNet blocks, and an exponential moving average (EMA) of the parameters with decay 0.999.
Its objective adds focal binary cross-entropy for the soft MixUp classification targets to soft
cross-entropy over the mixed bin targets at unit weight. The model uses AdamW with learning rate
10−3 and weight decay 10−4 , batch size 256, gradient-norm clipping at 1.0, and cosine annealing
to 10−6 over 30 epochs. Validation and checkpoint selection use the EMA parameters; the retained
checkpoint maximizes AUROC(1 − fout ) subject to guards against early, degenerate, or poorly
calibrated solutions.
Shared sampling and inputs. Both original implementations use the same redshift-weighted sam-
pler: 5× for z < 0.03, 3× for 0.03 ≤ z < 0.05, 1.5× for 0.05 ≤ z < 0.10, and 1× other-
wise. Thus, the architectures, modality-specific encoders, fusion layers, objectives, augmentations,
optimization schedules, sampling rule, and checkpoint-selection criteria described above were all
established by DISCOVERYAGENT on the research dataset before the external evaluation.


                                                31
Supplementary Note 22: External evaluation of the photometric-redshift models.
      This note describes how we applied the DISCOVERYAGENT-developed PF-EffB3 and V3-
Binned implementations (Supplementary Note 21) to the external dataset and how we retrained
and scored all comparison models. Split construction is in Supplementary Note 18, and the
single-modality architectures are in Supplementary Note 20.
Transfer of the DISCOVERYAGENT implementations. For both fusion models, we carried over
the original source implementations, including the image and photometry encoders, fusion lay-
ers and prediction heads, loss functions and weights, augmentations and regularization, optimizer
and learning-rate schedules, batch sizes and epoch counts, redshift-weighted sampler, and model-
specific checkpoint-selection criteria. The expected scientific changes were the dataset paths and
the low-redshift classification boundary, which changed from z < 0.03 in the campaign to z < 0.05
for the external evaluation. We did not alter the sampler boundaries or weights when changing this
target: the external runs retained weights of 5× below z = 0.03, 3× from 0.03 to 0.05, 1.5× from
0.05 to 0.10, and 1× above 0.10.
      PF-EffB3 retained the fixed image-channel means and standard deviations from its original
implementation, while its photometric-feature medians and median absolute deviations were re-
computed from the external training split using the original procedure. Its focal-loss positive-class
weight was likewise recomputed from training prevalence; because the external training split is
balanced at the z < 0.05 boundary, this induced a weight of approximately 1, rather than the
larger weight induced by the original imbalanced research split. V3-Binned reused its original
normalization-statistics file unchanged. Neither model reused a checkpoint from the research cam-
paign: both began new optimization runs with the original ImageNet initialization recipe. The
remaining code changes were engineering changes for the larger evaluation package—lazy access
to materialized FP16 image arrays and multi-worker data loading replaced eager image loading,
and the PF model-matrix script gained filters for running only the selected architecture and task—
without changing either model definition or training objective.
Common retraining protocol. Every model in the comparison, including the baselines, was re-
trained from scratch on the balanced external training split (151,463 galaxies) and scored once on the
external test split (39,859). We selected checkpoints on the external validation split (7,972), except
for Darragh–Ford, which used the final epoch-10 checkpoint. All runs use the same low-redshift
target, z < 0.05, for classification. The recipes retain their prespecified checkpoint-selection cri-
teria: P@95C for PF-EffB3 and the photometry-only MLP, a guarded AUROC–outlier criterion
for V3-Binned, and validation σNMAD for the image-only CNN. No model in the comparison reuses
weights trained on the original research split, so the comparison uses common data splits and target
while preserving the selection rule associated with each recipe.
Comparators. Supplementary Note 20 specifies the image-only CNN and the campaign-built
photometry-only MLP, including their architectures, training configurations, and checkpoint se-
lection. The Darragh–Ford comparator is a from-scratch reimplementation of the published hybrid
xResNet-34 low-redshift classifier architecture [12], rather than a released set of weights, retrained
on the external training split against the z < 0.05 target. It contributes AUROC and purity but no
redshift estimate.
      We compute classification metrics from each model’s predicted probability of z < 0.05,

                                                 32
using its classification output rather than its redshift predictions. For Darragh–Ford, we use the
uncalibrated probabilities.
Metrics and uncertainty. AUROC is computed for the low-redshift class; σNMAD is the normalized
median absolute deviation of (zpred − ztrue )/(1 + ztrue ), reported over the full test split and over its
low-redshift (n = 19,929) and dwarf (n = 16,753) subsets; purity at 95% completeness (P@95C)
is the maximum low-redshift purity among thresholds with at least 95% completeness. Dwarfs
are the galaxies carrying the catalog dwarf flag, corresponding to a stellar mass below 109.25 M⊙ ;
the catalog supplies a stellar-mass estimate only for these objects, which is why the stellar-mass
stratification in the main text is restricted to the dwarf sample. Half-light radius is the Legacy
Survey shape_r column. Standard errors are paired bootstrap estimates over the test rows with
a fixed seed, using 150 resamples for AUROC and P@95C and 300 for σNMAD ; all models are
resampled on the same indices, so the errors are comparable across rows. Supplementary Table 14
reports the full set.
Stellar-mass and size stratification. For Figure 4h and Supplementary Table 15, we compute the
mean normalized absolute error |zpred −ztrue |/(1+ztrue ) over galaxies with ztrue < 0.05 for PF-EffB3,
the image-only CNN, and the photometry-only MLP. Before bin-range cuts, the stellar-mass analysis
includes only dwarfs with catalog masses, whereas the half-light-radius analysis includes all low-
redshift galaxies with measured radii. For each property, we use 12 equal-width, half-open bins
spanning its 2nd to 98th percentiles among low-redshift galaxies, set the lower half-light-radius edge
to zero, and exclude galaxies outside this range; no end bin falls below the prespecified minimum
of 30 galaxies. The stellar-mass analysis contains 14,575 of 15,183 low-redshift dwarfs, and the
half-light-radius analysis contains 19,530 of 19,929 low-redshift galaxies. The table abbreviates
the three models as PF, Img, and Phot, respectively.
Color–magnitude stratification. For Figure 4i and Supplementary Table 16, we divide the 39,859
test galaxies into three spectroscopic-redshift ranges and bin them on a 20 × 20 grid in r magnitude
(16.21–20.86) and g − r color (0.151–0.789). Equal-width edges span the 1st to 99th percentiles
of each quantity over the full test split, with observations outside this range assigned to the nearest
edge cell. Within each cell and model, we calculate σNMAD = 1.4826 × median |∆ − median(∆)|
for the signed residual ∆ = (zpred − ztrue )/(1 + ztrue ), retaining cells with at least eight galaxies. The
table reports the number of retained cells and galaxies, the unweighted median σNMAD across cells,
and the percentage of cells in which each model has the lowest σNMAD ; no ties occurred. PF, Img,
and Phot denote PF-EffB3, the image-only CNN, and the photometry-only MLP, respectively.
Prediction sources. All analyses use predictions from the image-only CNN, PF-EffB3, V3-Binned,
the campaign-built photometry-only MLP, and the Darragh–Ford comparator. Outlier fractions
were explored under both |∆z| > 0.05 and |∆z| > 0.15; the table reports the former.
      Supplementary Table 18 compares the PF-EffB3 joint model against both single-modality
models for each test galaxy using normalized absolute error. “Best single model” is the lower of
the image-only and photometry-only errors for that galaxy, ties count as not worse, and the final row
therefore measures the gap to an oracle that selects the better modality per galaxy. Median (mean)
normalized absolute error is 0.0186 (0.0343) for photometry-only, 0.0156 (0.0264) for image-only,
and 0.0106 (0.0207) for the joint model.




                                                    33
Supplementary Note 23: Overlap between research and external evaluation galaxies.
      The research and external evaluation samples share galaxies because both were drawn from
DESI DR1. Matching by DESI TARGETID, we identified 26.7% of external test galaxies in the
original development set (research training or validation splits). To assess robustness to this overlap,
we excluded these galaxies and repeated the Figure 4g,h analyses on the remaining 29,228 galaxies
using fixed predictions, without retraining models or reselecting checkpoints. The external splits
remain mutually disjoint; this check addresses overlap with the data used to develop the model
recipes.
      Excluding development-set galaxies preserves the AUROC ranking, with PF-EffB3 perform-
ing best, followed by V3-Binned. Both models retain lower redshift scatter than the single-modality
baselines for low-redshift galaxies and dwarfs (Supplementary Figure 3a–c). The mass- and size-
dependent error patterns also persist, including PF-EffB3’s largest relative advantage for compact,
low-mass galaxies (Supplementary Figure 3d,e). Although PF-EffB3’s full-sample redshift scatter
and outlier fraction increase slightly, the main comparisons remain stable after removing galaxies
shared with the original development set.




                                                  34
Supplementary Note 24: Joint-blind validation of modality following in the extragalactic as-
tronomy fusion model.
      We assess whether the joint prediction lies closer to the more accurate single-modality pre-
diction across the full held-out test set, then repeat the comparison in cohorts with a prespecified
difference in single-modality error.
Full-test-set prediction alignment. For each of the 39,859 test galaxies, we identify the single-
modality model with lower normalized absolute error (|zpred − ztrue |/(1 + ztrue )) and compare the
joint prediction’s absolute distance to the two single-modality predictions. The joint prediction is
closer to the more accurate single-modality prediction for 28,580 galaxies (71.7%).
Oracle comparison. We define an oracle that uses the spectroscopic redshift to select the lower-
error single-modality prediction separately for each galaxy. Its mean normalized absolute error
across the full test set is 0.019093, compared with 0.020695 for the joint model. The relative differ-
ence, calculated from unrounded values as the joint mean divided by the oracle mean minus one,
is 8.4%. The oracle is a retrospective reference that requires the true redshift at prediction time.
Together with the alignment analysis, this comparison is consistent with complementary predictive
information in images and photometry, although differences in architecture and training prevent us
from isolating the contribution of fusion (Supplementary Note 20).
Cohort definition. For each of the 39,859 held-out test galaxies we computed the difference in
normalized absolute error between the photometry-only MLP and the image-only CNN, |∆z|photo −
|∆z|image . Galaxies with a difference of at least +0.01 were labeled image-favored (n = 14,061) and
those with a difference of at most −0.01 were labeled photometry-favored (n = 8,350); galaxies
within 0.01 of parity were excluded. The joint model’s error plays no role in this assignment. For
each galaxy we then recorded a binary outcome: whether the joint prediction is closer to the image-
only prediction than to the photometry-only prediction.
Association test. Cross-tabulating cohort against outcome, the joint prediction sided with the
image-only prediction for 79.2% of image-favored galaxies (exact 95% CI 78.6–79.9%) and with
the photometry-only prediction for 71.2% of photometry-favored galaxies (70.2–72.1%). The odds
of the joint prediction siding with the image-only prediction were 9.41 times higher in the image-
favored cohort (95% CI 8.84–10.02; Fisher’s exact p < 10−300 ; ϕ = 0.50). A one-sided Mann–
Whitney test on the continuous alignment score, defined as the signed difference between the joint
prediction’s distances to the photometry-only and image-only predictions normalized by their sep-
aration, agreed (rank-biserial 0.56, p < 10−300 ).
Stratified test. Because the favored modality is itself correlated with redshift, apparent magnitude,
and angular resolution, we repeated the test within strata defined jointly by redshift quintile, r-band
magnitude quintile, and size-to-PSF ratio bin (edges at 1.15, 1.6, and 3), pooling the resulting 92
non-degenerate 2 × 2 tables with a Cochran–Mantel–Haenszel estimator. The common odds ratio
was 6.89 (p < 10−300 ). The attenuation from 9.41 to 6.89 indicates that part of the crude association
is carried by these covariates, but the remaining effect shows that among galaxies of similar redshift,
magnitude, and resolution the joint model still leans toward whichever single modality is more
accurate.
Low-redshift subset. Restricting to z < 0.05, where the target dwarf population lies, the joint

                                                  35
prediction sided with the favored modality for 77.4% of image-favored (n = 4,198) and 88.8% of
photometry-favored (n = 3,005) galaxies (odds ratio 27.1, 95% CI 23.7–30.9; Cochran–Mantel–
Haenszel odds ratio 37.3 across 87 strata; both p < 10−300 ).




                                             36
Supplementary Note 25: Census data access for the economics experiment.
      The economics experiment is conducted on a broad collection of public-use U.S. Census
and federal survey data reached exclusively through the Census data agent. This specialized data-
delivery agent is registered in the director’s tool catalog only when enabled for a run, accepts natural-
language requests for particular subjects, geographies, and years, and forwards them to a wrapped
data client. Each run begins with an empty data directory: every dataset ultimately analyzed is
one the agent requested and downloaded on demand. The agent is restricted to retrieval rather than
analysis; it places the requested files on disk for the execution agent and returns a report describing
the delivered data. This note catalogs the datasets documented as accessible through that interface;
the realized subset for our run is far smaller and is summarized at the end.
Access mechanism. The wrapped client exposes three public-use backends through a common
search-then-download interface: published American Community Survey (ACS) summary tables,
confidentiality-protected ACS Public Use Microdata Sample (PUMS) records, and harmonized
IPUMS microdata from the USA, Current Population Survey (CPS), National Health Interview
Survey (NHIS), and Medical Expenditure Panel Survey (MEPS) collections. The agent searches
for a table or variable by topic keyword, inspects its data dictionary, and downloads it for a specified
geography and year. Each ACS download writes one or more CSV files of estimates—with optional
margins of error (MOE)—together with a JSON data dictionary that explains every variable code;
IPUMS extracts are instead returned as compressed record files with an XML codebook. The three
backends differ in latency: the ACS summary-table and PUMS APIs respond in seconds, whereas
an IPUMS extract is built server-side and takes minutes.
Backend 1 — ACS summary tables (Census Bureau API). Pre-tabulated, published statistics
from the American Community Survey, covering 2005–2019 and 2021–2024 for standard 1-year
estimates and 2009–2024 release end years for 5-year estimates [14,15]. Standard 2020 ACS 1-year
estimates were not released, and the experimental replacement tables are not available through the
Census API [16]. The 5-year estimates pool 60 months of data, providing greater precision and
coverage of small geographies, including census tracts and block groups [17]. In recent survey
years, the ACS sampled approximately 3.5 million housing-unit addresses annually, of which ap-
proximately 1.9–2.0 million yielded final housing-unit interviews [18]. Geographies range from the
nation down to the state, county, place, metropolitan/micropolitan statistical area (CBSA), census
tract, and block group, and also include congressional districts, school districts, and ZIP-code tab-
ulation areas (ZCTAs); a wildcard requests all units of a level (e.g., all counties in a state). Tables
span demographic, economic, educational, housing, transportation, social, and language topics, in-
dexed by the Census “B”-series detailed-table codes. Thousands of tables exist; the series most
relevant to the economics study are catalogued in Supplementary Table 20.
Backend 2 — PUMS microdata (Census Bureau API). Confidentiality-protected person- and
housing-unit-level Public Use Microdata Sample records from recent ACS years support custom
cross-tabulations and record-level analyses that are not possible from the pre-tabulated summary
tables [19, 20]. These are public-use records rather than the Census Bureau’s confidential source
microdata. Geography is limited to nation, region, division, state, and Public Use Microdata Area
(PUMA)—there is no tract- or county-level PUMS [21]. Variables span demographics (AGEP,
SEX, RAC1P, HISP), education (SCHL, SCH), employment (ESR, COW, WKHP, WKW), income

                                                  37
(PINCP, WAGP, HINCP, PERNP, SEMP), housing (TEN, RNTP, VALP, RMSP, BDSP), and com-
muting (JWTR, JWMNP). Critically for our study, PUMS provides record-level annual homeowner
property-insurance expenditure (INSP) alongside selected monthly owner costs (SMOCP); ACS
detailed table B25141 separately reports binned homeowner-insurance costs, but does not expose
record-level values [22]. The HINS1–HINS7 variables are health-insurance indicators and are not
used as property-insurance measures.
Backend 3 — IPUMS harmonized microdata. Extract-based access to harmonized individual-
level microdata across four survey programs, with variables coded consistently across decades. The
available collections are IPUMS USA (decennial-census and ACS microdata, 1850–present; state,
county, metro, and PUMA geography), IPUMS CPS (Current Population Survey labor-force data,
1962–present; state and metro), IPUMS NHIS (National Health Interview Survey, 1963–present;
region only), and IPUMS MEPS (Medical Expenditure Panel Survey of healthcare costs and uti-
lization, 1996–present; region only). Extracts are requested by sample identifier (e.g., us2023a
for the 2023 ACS 1-year sample) and a chosen variable list, with optional case selection to filter
records. IPUMS USA offers historical depth and cross-decade harmonization that the recent-years
Census PUMS API does not, but the extract did not include the ACS-specific INSP variable [23].
Output format. Every retrieval produces machine-readable estimates plus an accompanying data
dictionary, so that the execution agent receives both the values and the meaning of each variable
code without further lookups. Summary-table downloads are named by table series, geography,
and year; PUMS and IPUMS downloads are per-state or per-extract microdata files.
Realized usage in our run. The 2023 six-state Census PUMS files contain 522,058 housing records
and 1,136,597 person records (1,658,655 records in total); the 2018 Florida housing file adds
103,736 records. These are file-row counts, not survey-weighted population estimates or counts of
distinct individuals across products; they exclude summary-table rows and overlapping IPUMS ex-
tracts. Although the documented access spans several federal survey programs and over a century of
public-use microdata, the economics run drew on a focused subset. From the ACS summary tables
it pulled population, median household income, educational attainment, housing tenure, median
home value, median rent, and geographic-mobility series at the state and county level, concentrated
in 2014, 2023, and 2024 (Supplementary Table 20). From Census PUMS it downloaded the 2023
housing and person files (and the 2018 Florida files) for six states—California, Florida, Louisiana,
Minnesota, Ohio, and Texas—which supplied the standalone INSP measure used to construct the
homeowner-insurance burden metrics. From IPUMS USA it used a single 2023 ACS householder
extract for the individual-level homeownership and migration models. The CPS, NHIS, and MEPS
programs, all pre-2014 microdata, and sub-county geographies were available but not used. Sepa-
rately from the agent’s run, the post hoc premium-trend analyses (Supplementary Note 26) retrieved
owner-occupied housing records from the 1-year PUMS files for 2013–2019 and 2021–2023 directly
from the Census PUMS API.
Exact data-source citations and required attributions. We cite the ACS detailed tables at the ta-
ble and release level, retaining the Census Bureau’s full multiyear labels rather than referring to each
5-year product only by its terminal year. The 2010–2014 ACS 5-Year Estimates used tables B01003,
B15003, B19013, B25003, B25064, B25071, and B25077 [24–30]; an exploratory robustness anal-
ysis additionally used B25034 as a county-level housing-supply proxy [31]. The 2023 ACS 1-Year
Estimates used tables B07001, B07013, B25003, B25038, B25088, and B25103 [32–37]. The

                                                  38
download inventory also contains tables B25089, B25090, B25091, B25094, and B25102, which
were retrieved during exploration but are not marked as reported analysis inputs in Supplementary
Table 20 [38–42]. The 2020–2024 ACS 5-Year Estimates used tables B01003, B15003, B19013,
B25003, B25064, B25071, and B25077 [43–49].
      The microdata inputs comprise the 2023 ACS 1-Year PUMS housing and person files and the
2018 ACS 1-Year PUMS Florida housing file [50,51]. The post hoc trend analyses additionally used
the ACS 1-Year PUMS owner-occupied housing records for Florida and the nation for 2013–2019
and 2021–2023 [19]. We interpreted their variables using the corresponding Census PUMS user
guide, data documentation, and dictionaries [19, 20, 52, 53]. The harmonized householder analysis
used the 2023 ACS sample in IPUMS USA Version 16.0; following the IPUMS terms, we cite the
versioned dataset and separately acknowledge its underlying Census source through the 2023 ACS
PUMS citation [23, 50]. PUMA-based analyses use the Census Bureau’s 2020 PUMA criteria and
the 2020 tract-to-PUMA relationship file, while the 2018 comparison uses the 2010 relationship file
[21, 54, 55]. Florida maps use the 2022 TIGER/Line Florida 2020-PUMA geometry [56]. General
statements about ACS interpretation, sample size, and survey limitations follow the Census Bureau
handbook, sample-size documentation, and 2024 Design and Methodology Report [17, 18, 57].
Survey-data limitations. Because this investigation relies on survey data, its findings inherit the
standard limitations of the ACS and related products [57]. Estimates are subject to sampling uncer-
tainty, coverage and nonresponse error, imputation, and measurement error in self-reported quan-
tities such as income, home value, rent, and insurance costs; margins of error can be especially
consequential for small populations and geographies. Public-use microdata further coarsen geogra-
phy to protect confidentiality and may top-code or otherwise perturb sensitive values, limiting the
resolution of local and distribution-tail analyses. Finally, the ACS provides repeated cross-sections
rather than longitudinal household records, so changes across years may reflect shifts in population
composition as well as changes experienced by households, and estimates drawn from different
survey products, vintages, and geographic levels are not always directly comparable. We therefore
interpret the economics results as population-level descriptive associations rather than household-
level trajectories or causal effects.




                                                39
Supplementary Note 26: Premium-trend analyses and capitalizing insurance into the buy-
versus-rent decision.
      This note specifies the premium and burden analyses behind Figure 5d–g and the price-to-rent
analysis behind Figure 5h. All analyses use owner-occupied housing records (TEN∈ {1, 2}) from
ACS 1-year PUMS files, WGTP-weighted medians, and nominal dollars.
Agent trace and post hoc analyses. The economics trace first tested insurance-to-value (ITV)
as a predictor of homeownership, then evaluated recent-mover, tenure-duration, cross-state, tem-
poral, and county extensions after the initial result proved specification-sensitive. The final clo-
sure records the ITV hypothesis as falsified and a conditional log-median-premium association in
disaster-exposed states; it does not identify a causal insurance effect. The 2018-versus-2023 Florida
premium comparison (Figure 5d) was computed within the trace, in Round 6 (query 7), although
no round report summarizes it. The multi-year trends, the income-quartile burden, and the price-to-
rent capitalization were computed afterward as post hoc reproductions and extensions rather than
as agent-trace findings.
Florida premium comparison, 2018 versus 2023. The reproduction compares the statewide 2018
and 2023 Florida distributions among owners with positive INSP, VALP, HINCP, and WGTP. The
weighted median annual premium rises from $1,400 to $2,200 (+57.1%), matching the trace value.
The comparison is statewide because the two years use 2010- and 2020-vintage PUMAs, which
cannot be matched one-to-one.
Multi-year trends, 2013–2023. Figure 5e and g use the 1-year PUMS files for 2013–2019 and
2021–2023; 2020 is omitted. For Florida and for the nation (records pooled across the four Cen-
sus regions), each series is the weighted median among owners reporting a positive value: annual
insurance premium (INSP), annual property taxes, annual utilities (twelve times monthly electric-
ity and gas plus annual water and other fuel, excluding the flag codes for included or no charge),
selected monthly owner costs (SMOCP), and household income (HINCP). Property taxes use the
dollar-valued TAXAMT from 2018 onward and the midpoints of the coded TAXP categories for ear-
lier years, so the pre-2018 tax series is approximate. Figure 5g reports each series as cumulative
growth relative to 2013.
Income-quartile insurance burden. The insurance-to-income ratio is 100 × INSP/HINCP for
Florida owners with positive INSP, HINCP, and WGTP, computed separately for 2018 and 2023.
Income quartiles are unweighted empirical quartiles of HINCP within each year’s sample, and each
quartile’s burden is its weighted median ratio. The lowest quartile rises from 4.9% to 6.6% and the
highest from 1.0% to 1.2%. Because the sample conditions on positive premiums, owners reporting
zero insurance expenditure are analyzed separately (Supplementary Note 28). These are descriptive
comparisons of repeated cross-sections, not a household panel or causal decomposition.
Price-to-rent data. The price-to-rent analysis uses 2023 Florida PUMS housing records. The
relevant variables are VALP (owner-reported home value), RNTP (monthly contract rent), INSP
(annual homeowners insurance — PUMS is the only ACS product that publishes this as a standalone
item; Supplementary Note 25), TEN (tenure), and WGTP (housing-unit weight). Geography is the
2020-vintage Public Use Microdata Area as delivered in the 2022 TIGER/Line release, giving 168
Florida PUMAs; polygons are clipped to a land mask so that the coastline and the Keys render


                                                 40
correctly.
Per-PUMA estimates. Home value V , monthly rent R, and annual insurance I are weighted me-
dians using WGTP. Owners are households with TEN indicating ownership (with or without a mort-
gage) and both INSP and VALP positive; V and I are their weighted medians. Renters are house-
holds with TEN indicating renting and RNTP above $200, which drops subsidized and nominal
rents; R is their weighted median. A PUMA is retained only with at least 10 qualifying owners and
5 qualifying renters. All 168 Florida PUMAs pass these filters.
Price-to-rent ratios. The raw ratio is P /Rraw = V /(12R). To capitalize insurance into the effec-
tive purchase price we add the perpetuity value of the annual premium stream to the numerator,

                                            V + I/r
                                P /Radj =           ,       r = 0.07,
                                              12R
where r is the assumed discount rate, set equal to the approximate 2023 30-year fixed mortgage rate.
This adjustment is a partial-equilibrium exercise: it capitalizes insurance premiums into the effec-
tive purchase price while holding observed rents fixed. If landlords pass higher property-insurance
costs through to tenants, equilibrium rents may also rise, partially offsetting the increase in the ad-
justed price-to-rent ratio. The threshold-crossing counts therefore quantify the mechanical effect
of owner-side insurance capitalization under fixed rents rather than a long-run equilibrium change
in tenure incentives.
Classification and counts. Given a threshold T , a PUMA crosses when P /Rraw ≤ T but P /Radj >
T , remains below when both ratios are at or below T , and is already above when P /Rraw > T . At
T = 21, the threshold used in Figure 5h, 41 of 168 PUMAs (24.4%) cross, 41 remain below, and
86 are already above. All 41 crossing PUMAs have raw ratios between 18.75 and 20.96, inside the
15–21 interval shaded in the figure. At T = 20, 23 PUMAs (13.7%) cross, 29 remain below, and
116 are already above; the crossing PUMAs have raw ratios between 18.52 and 19.89.
Caveats. (1) The 15 and 20 reference values come from Trulia’s 2012 consumer-market heuristic:
ratios at or below 15 favor buying, ratios at or above 20 favor renting, and intermediate values
depend on tax deductions and personal circumstances [58]. Gallin provides evidence that rent–price
ratios predict subsequent house-price changes [59], while Himmelberg and colleagues show that
valuation depends on the user cost of housing, including interest rates, taxes, maintenance, expected
appreciation, and risk [60]; neither paper establishes universal buy-versus-rent cutoffs of 15 and
20. We use T = 20 and the higher, analysis-specific threshold T = 21 as descriptive sensitivity
benchmarks, applying the strict P /Radj > T crossing rule defined above. Their application to
the insurance-adjusted ratio is a modeling choice, not a validated household decision rule. (2) At
T = 21 the crossing PUMAs span the Panhandle (two PUMAs), interior Central Florida, and
several coastal metropolitan areas, including Jacksonville, Tampa Bay, the southeast metros, and
southwest Florida. (3) Because I/r is a perpetuity, the adjusted ratio is sensitive to r. In a state-
level sensitivity analysis over r ∈ {0.03, 0.05, 0.07, 0.09, 0.11}, the number of Florida PUMAs
crossing T = 21 is 59, 49, 41, 35, and 32 (and crossing T = 20 is 44, 29, 23, 19, and 17), so the
headline count roughly halves between the lowest and highest rates, while the state-level ordering of
adjusted ratios is not invariant (Florida and Ohio exchange ranks between r = 0.05 and r = 0.07).
(4) The crossing PUMAs cluster just below the threshold: 22 of the 41 share an identical raw ratio


                                                  41
of 20.83 (median value $250,000 and median rent $1,000), and 30 of the 41 already exceed the
conventional cutoff of 20, so the T = 21 count is sensitive to threshold placement; the T = 20 row
of Supplementary Table 24 gives the conventional-cutoff count.
      Supplementary Table 22 reports the annual series. For Figure 5e, the Florida premium series
additionally requires positive SMOCP, HINCP, VALP, and WGTP and 0 < INSP/(12 × SMOCP) <
1, whereas the national series pools the four Census regions and requires only positive INSP; the
Figure 5g series uses each variable’s positive-reporting sample. Supplementary Table 23a reports
the 2018–2023 income-quartile burden comparison, with changes computed from unrounded me-
dians; its statewide premium row additionally requires positive VALP. Supplementary Table 24
reports the threshold-crossing counts and the raw-ratio ranges of crossing PUMAs. The increases
in median insurance-to-income burden are 1.7218 percentage points for the lowest income quartile
and 0.2428 for the highest; their ratio, calculated before rounding, is 7.0927, or approximately 7.1
times.

Supplementary Note 27: Stability of zero reported insurance expenditure, 2018 versus 2023.
      This analysis asks whether the 2023 share of Florida owners reporting zero insurance ex-
penditure reflects a recent change. We applied the 2023 sample restrictions, outcome definition
(INSP=0), mortgage definition (MRGP> 0), unweighted HINCP quintiles, and WGTP weighting
(Supplementary Note 28) to the 2018 ACS 1-Year PUMS Florida housing file (Supplementary
Note 25). No 2018 person file was available, so the householder link was omitted; omitting it in
2023 leaves the sample and all estimates unchanged. The 2018 sample contains 59,379 housing-unit
observations.
      The weighted share reporting zero insurance expenditure was 17.3% in 2018 and 17.1% in
2023. Subgroup gradients were similar in both years: 31.5% versus 30.6% in the lowest income
quintile, 7.2% versus 8.1% in the highest, 30.7% versus 28.5% among mortgage-free owners, and
7.0% versus 7.6% among mortgage holders (2018 versus 2023). These are two repeated cross-
sections without survey-design standard errors; they indicate that the 2023 level and its concentra-
tion among lower-income and mortgage-free owners were not new, but do not describe year-to-year
variation between the two endpoints.




                                                43
Supplementary Note 28: Zero reported homeowners-insurance expenditure in Florida.
Data and analytic sample. This is a post hoc descriptive extension, rather than a finding from
the agent’s original economics trace. We analyzed the 2023 American Community Survey 1-year
Public Use Microdata Sample (ACS PUMS) housing and person records for Florida (Supplementary
Note 25). We linked each housing record to its householder using SERIALNO and the householder
indicator SPORDER=1, then retained owner-occupied units (TEN∈ {1, 2}) with positive housing-
unit weight (WGTP), household income (HINCP), and owner-reported home value (VALP). The
resulting analytic sample contains 65,732 housing-unit observations representing approximately
6.0 million Florida owner households after applying WGTP.
Outcome definition. We defined zero reported insurance expenditure as INSP=0, where INSP
records annual fire, hazard, and flood insurance costs for the property. This measure captures an
expenditure report rather than verified policy status: a zero may represent a genuinely uninsured
or self-insuring owner, but may also arise when an escrowed premium is not reported separately or
through other reporting error. Conversely, positive expenditure does not establish that coverage is
comprehensive or adequate. We therefore use “zero reported insurance expenditure” as the primary
description and treat “going bare” only as shorthand for this proxy.
Overall and subgroup estimates. We estimated each rate as the WGTP-weighted share of house-
holds with INSP=0. Overall, 17.1% of eligible Florida owner households reported zero insurance
expenditure in 2023, corresponding to approximately 1.03 million weighted households. The rate
varied sharply with income: it was 30.6% in the lowest income quintile and 8.1% in the highest, a
3.8× difference. The source pipeline defines the five income groups from the unweighted empirical
distribution of HINCP in the analytic sample and then applies WGTP when estimating the rate within
each group. Zero expenditure was also substantially more common among mortgage-free owners
than among mortgage holders (28.5% versus 7.6%), consistent with the role of lender insurance
requirements. Mortgage status is defined by a positive reported first-mortgage payment (MRGP)
rather than by the tenure code; the 1,061 units coded as owned with a mortgage but reporting no
payment are counted as mortgage-free, and defining status by tenure instead gives 29.1% versus
7.7%. These are cross-sectional subgroup comparisons within 2023 and do not measure changes
in coverage over time.
Multivariable analysis. As a descriptive robustness check, we fit a weighted linear probability
model for INSP=0 using WGTP and heteroskedasticity-robust HC1 standard errors. Covariates
included log household income, log home value, mortgage status, age and age squared, race and
ethnicity indicators, bachelor’s-degree attainment, and the Public Use Microdata Area (PUMA)-
level insurance-to-value ratio estimated among owners reporting positive premiums, computed as
the ratio of the weighted mean premium to the weighted mean home value per $1,000; all 65,732
units enter the regression. Mortgage status remained the largest predictor: holding a mortgage
was associated with a 19.9-percentage-point lower probability of zero reported expenditure (β =
−0.199, SE = 0.004, p < 0.001). Higher income (β = −0.034, SE = 0.002, p < 0.001) and home
value (β = −0.063, SE = 0.002, p < 0.001) were also negatively associated with zero expenditure,
while higher local insurance burden was positively associated (β = 0.0070 per additional dollar of
annual premium per 1,000 of home value, SE = 0.0017, p < 0.001).


                                                44
Interpretation and limitations. The estimates identify a substantial population omitted when
premium levels and insurance-to-income ratios are calculated only among owners with INSP> 0.
Because the ACS is a repeated cross-section and neither verifies policy status nor observes policy
terms, these results do not establish that premium increases caused owners to drop coverage and do
not measure underinsurance. The subgroup rates also inherit survey nonresponse, imputation, and
measurement error, while the regression’s HC1 uncertainty is not a full replicate-weight variance
estimate under the ACS survey design. We therefore interpret the results as descriptive evidence
that zero reported insurance expenditure is concentrated among lower-income and mortgage-free
Florida owners.
      Supplementary Table 23b reports the subgroup counts and weighted zero-expenditure rates.

Supplementary Note 29: OpenTargets data-agent access.
       The functional genomics campaign used a specialized data-delivery agent to expose a local
copy of the Open Targets Platform [61]. The agent was registered in the director’s tool catalog only
for this campaign and translated a director query into a sandboxed extraction session. It read the
shared Open Targets source and wrote CSV or parquet extracts to a run-local output directory for
the execution agent to analyze; the specialized agent did not modify the source data.
Data release and access surfaces. The mounted source was Open Targets Platform v25.09, re-
leased September 17, 2025 in parquet format under a CC0 license. A post hoc audit of the mounted
release on September 17, 2026 found 38 datasets comprising 3,508 parquet files and 28.99 GB.
The release contained 78,726 targets, 39,530 diseases or phenotypes, 18,119 drugs or compounds,
30,396,274 evidence strings, and 10,989,518 target–disease associations. The agent could reach
these data through two complementary interfaces. First, its prompt supplied a catalog that exactly
matched all 38 datasets on disk and supported direct table queries. Second, six OpenTargets Model
Context Protocol (MCP) tool groups exposed 58 registered operations (56 distinct operations) over
33 datasets. Direct table access therefore provided complete release coverage, while the MCP in-
terface provided structured genetics, disease, association, target, expression, and drug queries.
Dataset inventory. The 38 catalogued datasets comprised the following groups. Six associ-
ation tables supplied overall, datatype-specific, and datasource-specific scores, each in direct
and ontology-propagated forms: association_overall_direct, association_by_
overall_indirect, association_by_datatype_direct, association_by_
datatype_indirect, association_by_datasource_direct, and association_
by_datasource_indirect. The evidence dataset stored the individual records underly-
ing these associations across 23 sources and seven evidence types: genetic association, somatic
mutation, known drug, affected pathway, literature, RNA expression, and animal model.
      Seven genetics datasets covered variant annotation, fine-mapped credible sets, study meta-
data, locus-to-gene prediction, colocalization, and regulatory intervals: variant, credible_
set, study, l2g_prediction, colocalisation_coloc, colocalisation_
ecaviar, and interval. Three target datasets provided nested gene and protein annotations
(target), DepMap CRISPR essentiality profiles (target_essentiality), and a flat target-
prioritization scorecard (target_prioritisation). Three disease datasets represented the
disease ontology, Human Phenotype Ontology terms, and disease–phenotype links: disease,
disease_hpo, and disease_phenotype.
      Five drug datasets supplied molecules, indications, mechanisms, warnings, and joined
drug–target–disease records: drug_molecule, drug_indication, drug_mechanism_
of_action, drug_warning, and known_drug. Two literature datasets supplied Europe
PMC entity mentions and embeddings (literature and literature_vector); two
interaction datasets supplied summary edges and per-experiment evidence (interaction and
interaction_evidence); and two expression datasets supplied baseline expression across
54 GTEx tissues and a broader tissue and cell-type ontology (expression and biosample).
The remaining datasets covered drug- and target-level FDA adverse-event signals (openfda_
significant_adverse_drug_reactions and openfda_significant_adverse_
target_reactions), mouse phenotypes and pharmacogenomics (mouse_phenotype and


                                                46
pharmacogenomics), and Reactome, Gene Ontology, and Sequence Ontology dictionaries
(reactome, go, and so).
Complete MCP tool inventory. The six MCP tool groups exposed the following operations to the
agent:
       Genetics (10): query GWAS associations; query locus-to-gene predictions; retrieve credible
sets; retrieve QTL colocalizations; convert rsIDs to Open Targets variant identifiers; retrieve variant
annotations; retrieve study metadata; query regulatory regions; query colocalizations; and retrieve
colocalizations by chromosome.
       Disease (6): retrieve disease information; search diseases by name; retrieve disease hierar-
chies; retrieve disease phenotypes; find diseases by therapeutic area; and find diseases by pheno-
type.
       Association (11): query target–disease associations; retrieve associations for a disease; re-
trieve associations for a target; compare direct with ontology-propagated associations; filter asso-
ciations by evidence datatype; filter associations by datasource; query individual evidence records;
retrieve evidence by publication; search the literature; find similar entities; and compute entity sim-
ilarity.
       Target (16): retrieve target information; search targets by name; retrieve target tractability;
retrieve target-prioritization scores; prioritize targets; retrieve target safety profiles; retrieve mouse
phenotypes; retrieve pharmacogenomic evidence; retrieve comprehensive target profiles; retrieve
target hallmarks; retrieve Target Enabling Package information; retrieve chemical probes; retrieve
genetic-constraint metrics; retrieve subcellular locations; retrieve target classes; and retrieve homo-
logues.
       Expression (6): list available tissues; query expression by gene; query expression by tissue;
compare expression across tissues; find tissue-specific genes; and search the biosample ontology.
       Drug (9): search drugs; retrieve drug information; retrieve target tractability; retrieve drug in-
dications; retrieve drug warnings; retrieve drug adverse events; retrieve drug mechanisms of action;
search known drugs; and retrieve pharmacogenomic evidence.
       Target tractability and pharmacogenomic evidence were each registered in two relevant tool
groups, producing 58 registrations for 56 distinct operations. Together with these data-specific tools,
the extraction session could search and read the complete mounted catalog and write requested
tabular extracts for downstream analysis.
       Five datasets were available only through direct parquet queries:                         target_
essentiality, interaction_evidence, reactome, go, and so. Thus, MCP-
only access would omit per-cell-line DepMap gene-effect scores, per-experiment interaction
support, the Reactome hierarchy, Gene Ontology term names, and Sequence Ontology labels,
although related identifiers and summary fields remained present in other datasets.
Identifiers and query scale. Targets are keyed by Ensembl gene identifier and diseases by Ex-
perimental Factor Ontology identifier across the association, evidence, expression, drug, and ge-
netics tables, allowing joins without an intermediate identifier map. Drugs use ChEMBL identi-
fiers; variants use Open Targets variant identifiers with rsID cross-references; tissues and cell types
use UBERON, EFO, or Cell Ontology identifiers; phenotypes use HPO; pathways use Reactome;
adverse events use MedDRA; and publications use PubMed identifiers. Seven datasets exceeded
1 GB—evidence, both colocalization tables, variant, interval, credible_set, and


                                                   47
literature—and therefore required selecting only relevant columns and records rather than
loading them in full. In particular, evidence occupied 8.37 GB but was partitioned by source,
allowing source-restricted queries to read only the relevant files.
Data handling and outputs. The Open Targets release was mounted read-only, and each invo-
cation wrote only derived CSV or parquet extracts to a separate run-local output directory. This
separation preserved the source release while making requested data available to the execution agent
for downstream analysis.




                                                48
Supplementary Note 30: Relay gene standardization.
      Because DISCOVERYAGENT uses varying definitions for relay genes in the internal experimen-
tation, we perform a standardization of the lists of genes in the topology groups, which we define
here.
Three definitions appear across the campaign. Numbers computed under the three are not in-
terchangeable. (i) An absolute-threshold definition pools degrees across the three conditions and
calls a gene relay when in-degree ≥ 265 and out-degree ≥ 30, giving 659 relays over a 9,474-
gene classifiable background; this underlies the exploratory GO-enrichment, network-density, and
functional-list analyses and the 149-gene relay∩autoimmune-GWAS set. (ii) A per-condition upper-
quartile definition computes in- and out-degree cutoffs within each condition; this is the standard
adopted here. (iii) A two-tier variant used when extending to other cell lines labels q75 genes relay
and q90 genes “very strong” relay.
Adopted definition. Throughout the main text a gene is a relay in a given condition when both
its in-degree and its out-degree are at or above the 75th percentile (q75 ) of that condition, which is
the upper-quartile definition used in the main text. Percentiles are taken over the classifiable subset
— genes with a defined out-degree, i.e. genes that were themselves perturbed and passed quality
control — not over all measured genes. The empirical cutoffs and resulting class sizes are:

            Condition   q75 in   q75 out   Relay   Terminal   Upstream   Other   Measured-only
            Rest           60        12     344      1,205       1,182   3,321           4,230
            Stim8hr        82        13     399      1,207       1,206   3,467           4,003
            Stim48hr       71        12     415      1,206       1,187   3,588           3,886

Each condition covers 10,282 genes; the classifiable background at Stim8hr is 6,279.
Degree construction. A directed edge from perturbation i to measured gene j exists when knock-
down of i significantly changes j’s expression at Benjamini–Hochberg adjusted p < 0.10; missing
statistics are treated as non-significant. Self-edges are removed before counting. Out-degree is the
row sum of the resulting significance mask and in-degree the column sum; where a gene appears
under more than one perturbation row or duplicated measured-gene symbol, the maximum is taken.
Upstream-regulator counts additionally require the perturbing row to be on-target significant with
no off-target flag. The 0.10 edge-FDR is held fixed across the campaign for comparability.
Class definitions. Over the classifiable background, writing hiin and hiout for being at or above
the respective q75 cutoff: relay = hiin ∧ hiout (heavily regulated and broadly regulating); terminal
= hiin ∧ ¬hiout (effector sinks); upstream = ¬hiin ∧ hiout (drivers); other = neither. Genes measured
but never perturbed have undefined out-degree and are labelled measured-only; they are excluded
from the enrichment foregrounds and backgrounds but retained in the functional-characterization
background.
Per-condition versus collapsed sets. Some analyses need one label per gene rather than one per
condition. Unless otherwise specified, collapsing uses the priority rule relay > upstream > terminal:
a gene is relay if it is relay in any condition, otherwise upstream if ever upstream, otherwise terminal;
genes only ever “other” or measured-only are dropped. Which set each analysis uses:


                                                     49
            Analysis                                          Set                          Relay n
            Topology enrichment, Figure 6i (Suppl. Note 32)   per-condition, Stim8hr       399
            Cell-type specificity (Suppl. Note 33)            per-condition, Stim8hr       399
            Essentiality (Suppl. Note 36)                     collapsed, any condition     762
            Druggability and annotation-confound analyses     collapsed, any condition     762
            Filtration funnel (Suppl. Note 37)                collapsed, stimulated only   639

The filtration funnel excludes Rest, on the grounds that relay status under stimulation is the disease-
relevant signal; its 639-gene set is a strict subset of the 762-gene any-condition set, with no genes
added.
Cell-line variants. The default 0.10 edge-FDR is used for CD4+ T cells, K562, and HEK293T. For
HCT116 a tightened edge-FDR of 0.01 is used (1,326 relay genes rather than 998 at 0.10) because
the HCT116 network is far denser than the CD4+ network at the default threshold: at edge-FDR
0.10 the mean out-degree of a classifiable HCT116 gene is ∼1,590 (upper-quartile cutoffs of 1,166
incoming and 450 outgoing edges), against 88 for CD4+ Stim8hr; at 0.01 it is ∼68 (cutoffs 73 and
23), comparable to CD4+ . The enrichment recipe is otherwise identical, and the immune-specificity
conclusion holds at either threshold (Supplementary Note 33).




                                                     50
Supplementary Note 31: Validation of the STAT3, VAV1, and IFNG exemplar nodes.
      Figure 6g illustrates one gene from each of three network-topology quadrants in the Stim8hr
condition. We revalidated the examples directly against the standardized per-condition topology
table rather than inferring totals from the panel artwork.
Authoritative counts and classification. We use the standardized per-condition topology table as
the source of record. At Stim8hr the upper-quartile thresholds are in-degree 82 and out-degree 13.
STAT3 has in-degree 137 and out-degree 1,369, placing it above both thresholds (relay). VAV1 has
in-degree 78 and out-degree 4,897, placing it four incoming edges below the in-degree threshold but
far above the out-degree threshold (upstream). IFNG has in-degree 136 and out-degree 0, placing
it above the in-degree threshold and below the out-degree threshold (terminal). Supplementary
Table 29 gives the arithmetic used by the panel.
Displayed neighbors. Every named edge in the panel is present in the corresponding Stim8hr
example-gene list: VAV1, ZAP70, and LAT are listed upstream of STAT3; IL10, LCK, and MAF
downstream of STAT3; CD247, LCK, and LCP2 upstream of VAV1; SLC3A2, BCL10, and
CORO1A downstream of VAV1; and STAT3, VAV1, and ZAP70 upstream of IFNG. These named
genes are representative examples rather than a complete neighborhood.




                                                51
Supplementary Note 32: Topology × genetic-evidence enrichment (Figure 6i).
      Figure 6i asks whether the relay topology role is over-represented among genes carrying
independent genetic evidence of disease, and whether this is specific to the immune cell type.
For two perturbation-screen cell types — CD4+ T cells (8-hour stimulation, “Stim8hr”) and
K562 erythroleukemia cells [62] — we cross every topology class (Relay / Terminal / Upstream
/ Other) against six genetic-evidence gene sets (autoimmune GWAS, primary immunodeficiency,
erythrocyte- and lymphocyte-trait GWAS sets, and cardiovascular- and eye-disease GWAS
controls) and plot the per-cell enrichment as a forest of log(OR) values. The full numeric results
— odds ratios, confidence intervals, overlap counts and one- and two-sided p-values for all 48 tests
— are given in Supplementary Table 30.
Topology classification. Within each cell type we build a directed gene–gene network from the
Perturb-seq differential-expression statistics: a perturbation of gene i is connected to a measured
gene j when the knockdown significantly changes j’s expression (Benjamini–Hochberg adjusted
p < 0.10), self-edges removed. Each gene’s out-degree (number of downstream genes it signifi-
cantly perturbs) and in-degree (number of perturbations that significantly affect it) are computed,
taking the maximum across any duplicate contrast labels. Genes are then labelled relative to the
upper-quartile (q75 ) in- and out-degree cutoffs computed over all classifiable genes (those with a de-
fined out-degree): relay (in- and out-degree ≥ q75 ), terminal (high in-, low out-degree), upstream
(low in-, high out-degree), and other (both below q75 ). The classifiable genes form the background
for all tests: n = 6,279 for CD4+ Stim8hr (Relay 399, Terminal 1,207, Upstream 1,206, Other
3,467) and n = 2,751 for K562 (Relay 201, Terminal 619, Upstream 528, Other 1,403). Note that
this panel uses the per-condition Stim8hr classification, so the relay set (399 genes) differs from the
any-condition collapsed relay set used for the druggability (762 genes) and filtration-funnel (639
genes) analyses (Supplementary Note 30); the Rest and Stim48hr conditions are shown in Supple-
mentary Figure 6.
Genetic-evidence gene sets. Four original GWAS sets are fixed prior to topology testing and de-
rived from the Open Targets [61] v25.09 locus-to-gene release by retaining the top-ranked gene per
locus at L2G score > 0.5: Autoimmune GWAS (union over autoimmune diseases; 1,368 genes),
Erythrocyte (union of the MCH, RDW and IRF red-blood-cell indices; 3,244 genes), and the Car-
diovascular (736) and Eye (476) control sets. The Lymphocyte count set (1,765 genes) uses the
current Open Targets gwas_credible_sets release at score > 0.5. PID is a curated primary-
immunodeficiency gene panel (69 genes) capturing rare monogenic immune disorders. Each set is
intersected with the cell-type background before testing; the resulting in-background gene counts
are listed per row in Supplementary Table 30.
Enrichment test. For each (evidence set × topology class) pair we form a category-vs-rest 2 × 2
contingency table over the background: rows are membership in the focal class versus all other
classified genes, columns are membership in the evidence set versus not. The point estimate is the
cross-product odds ratio after applying a Haldane–Anscombe 0.5 correction to all four cells, and the
√ confidence interval is the corresponding Woolf log interval, OR · exp(±1.96 SE), where SE =
95%
   1/(a + 0.5) + 1/(b + 0.5) + 1/(c + 0.5) + 1/(d + 0.5). Exact one-sided enrichment (pone ) and
two-sided (ptwo ) p-values are computed from the uncorrected counts. We deliberately keep the panel
free of multiple-testing correction so the per-cell pattern is visible directly; the significance stars

                                                  52
reflect the uncorrected one-sided p-value (∗ p < 0.05, ∗ ∗ p < 0.01, ∗ ∗ ∗ p < 0.001).
Plot description. Each row draws a marker at log(OR) with whiskers spanning the 95% CI on the
log scale; the dashed reference line at log(OR) = 0 is no enrichment. In CD4+ T cells the Relay
class is strongly enriched for autoimmune GWAS genes (OR = 2.44, p = 7.0 × 10−9 ) and very
strongly for PID genes (OR = 11.66, p = 3.3 × 10−13 ), with Terminal genes showing a weaker
autoimmune enrichment and Upstream/Other genes showing none or depletion; the cardiovascular
and eye controls are non-significant in every class. In K562 the immune signals are null (autoim-
mune Relay OR = 1.11; PID Relay OR = 1.02) while the erythrocyte set is weakly enriched in
K562 Relay genes (OR = 1.40, one-sided p = 0.033, two-sided p = 0.058).
Expression control. In-degree tracks a gene’s own expression (Spearman ρ = 0.57 with Stim8hr
baseline expression), so DISCOVERYAGENT tested expression as a confounder in Round 5. It showed
that in-degree remained associated with autoimmune GWAS status after adjusting for mean baseline
expression (OR 2.00 per unit log(1 + in-degree), p = 6.0 × 10−11 ; expression p = 0.48), and top-
quartile in-degree was enriched within each expression tertile (OR 1.67, 2.01 and 2.44 from low
to high). Adjusting the reference-set analysis above for each gene’s Stim8hr baseline expression
attenuated but did not remove relay enrichment for autoimmune GWAS genes (logistic OR 2.21,
95% CI 1.64–2.97, p = 1.6 × 10−7 ; Mantel–Haenszel OR across expression deciles 2.14) or PID
genes (OR 6.53, 95% CI 3.42–12.47, p = 1.3 × 10−8 ; Mantel–Haenszel OR 6.50).
Analysis implementation. We rebuild the native-background statistics directly from the current
CD4+ and K562 topology classifications. The per-disease breakdown in Supplementary Figure 7
applies the same corrected-OR and exact-test recipe to each autoimmune disease’s L2G > 0.5 gene
list against the CD4+ relay set. This breakdown uses a separately retrieved eight-disease gene list
(1,212 unique genes), also used for candidate filtration, rather than the 1,368-gene v25.09 reference
list used in the main enrichment analysis. On the same 6,279-gene Stim8hr background and 399-
gene relay set, these lists yield 471 background/66 relay hits and 519 background/67 relay hits,
respectively.




                                                 53
Supplementary Note 33: Cell-type specificity of relay-gene genetic enrichment.
      CD4+ relay genes are strongly enriched for autoimmune GWAS and primary-immuno-
deficiency (PID) genes, supporting the relay principle in an immune-relevant cellular context. We
extended the CD4+ T-cell and K562 comparison to HCT116 and HEK293T and tested evidence-set
overlap and degree bias (Supplementary Figure 9; Supplementary Table 30).
Analysis setup. We used the upper-quartile in-/out-degree classification and enrichment tests
described in Supplementary Note 32. Relay/background sizes were 399/6,279 for CD4+ Stim8hr,
201/2,751 for K562 [62], 1,326/18,293 for HCT116, and 1,008/18,311 for HEK293T [63].
HCT116 used edge-FDR 0.01 rather than 0.10 to obtain a network density comparable to CD4+
(mean out-degree ∼68 versus ∼1,590 at 0.10); autoimmune and PID relay enrichment remained
null at either threshold (Supplementary Note 30). Alongside the original evidence sets, we included
lymphocyte-count/percentage GWAS genes (1,765; EFO_0004587/EFO_0007993) and CD8+
T-cell-count genes (24; EFO_0010107), using the current Open Targets [61] gwas_credible_
sets release at score > 0.5. The original four GWAS sets use v25.09 L2G > 0.5, and PID is a
fixed curated panel. No credible-set evidence was available for the dedicated CD4+ -count trait.
Immune enrichment and relay identity. In native backgrounds, CD4+ relay genes were enriched
for PID, autoimmune, and lymphocyte-count genes (OR 11.66, 2.44, and 2.04, respectively; all
one-sided p < 10−6 ), whereas none of these sets was significantly enriched in the other three lines.
Cardiovascular and eye control sets were not enriched in relay genes in any line. Autoimmune log-
odds-ratio comparisons using two-sample Woolf z-tests gave p = 1.8 × 10−7 versus HCT116 and
p = 2.3 × 10−6 versus HEK293T. Only 24 (6%), 60 (15%), and 11 (3%) of the 399 CD4+ relay
genes were also relays in K562, HCT116, and HEK293T, respectively; 310 (78%) were relay in
none of these lines, although differences in gene coverage contribute to this non-overlap.
Overlap and degree controls. Autoimmune and erythrocyte gene sets share 499 genes (overlap
OR 3.1, p ≈ 2 × 10−72 ). Excluding these genes reduced CD4+ relay erythrocyte enrichment
from OR 1.71 to 1.41; the residual signal survived Benjamini–Hochberg correction (q ≈ 0.006–
0.018), but reached p < 0.05 in only ∼55% of downsampling trials matched to the autoimmune
set size. Using the same 399 Stim8hr relay genes and autoimmune GWAS reference list as the
main enrichment analysis, we observed 67 autoimmune-associated relay genes versus 53.2 expected
under a null matched exactly on in-degree (one-sided exact p = 0.0090), supporting enrichment
after controlling for incoming connectivity. Within each exact in-degree stratum of the 6,279-gene
classifiable background, the null samples without replacement the observed number of relay genes
from all genes in that stratum, holding autoimmune labels fixed. We computed the probability of at
least 67 total hits by convolving the stratum-specific hypergeometric hit-count distributions. This
control matches in-degree only, testing whether the high-out-degree subset is enriched among genes
with the same incoming connectivity.
Limits of lineage specificity. K562 has an independently supported erythroid identity [64], but
its relay erythrocyte enrichment was present but weak (OR 1.40, one-sided p = 0.033, two-sided
p = 0.058; BH q ≈ 0.58). Erythrocyte enrichment also appeared in CD4+ relays (OR 1.71) and
HCT116 relays (OR 1.45, one-sided p = 1.0 × 10−7 ) and terminal genes (OR 1.60), consistent with
shared loci and proliferation programs rather than strict lineage specificity. LDSC identified no well-


                                                 54
matched positive-control trait for HCT116 or HEK293T, and small targeted perturbation screens
lack the broad background needed for this topology classification. Lymphocyte count includes B
and NK cells, while the CD8+ set had only 1–2 in-background hits and is reported only in the table.
Together, the native-background comparisons and in-degree-matched sensitivity analysis support
autoimmune GWAS enrichment in CD4+ relay genes and its immune-context dependence.




                                                55
Supplementary Note 34: Relay-gene tractability and annotation-depth control.
      We independently retested the Round-12 druggability claim using fixed tractability annota-
tions from Minikel et al. [65], then asked whether any enrichment is explained by study depth.
Supplementary Figure 10 shows the per-condition tractability analysis, Supplementary Figure 11
shows the annotation-depth control, and Supplementary Table 32 reports the key numerical results.
Fixed tractability analysis. For each condition, the background comprises every gene assigned Re-
lay, Terminal, Upstream, or Other by the standardized upper-quartile topology definition; measured-
only genes are excluded. We intersect this background with nine fixed Minikel tractability lists: an-
tibody tractable, small-molecule tractable, catalytic receptors, nuclear receptors, kinases, enzymes,
transporters, ion channels, and rhodopsin GPCRs. For each condition, role, and list, a one-sided
category-versus-rest Fisher exact test evaluates enrichment. Benjamini–Hochberg correction is ap-
plied across the nine lists separately within each condition–role family. Relay genes are small-
molecule tractable at similar rates in Rest (154/344 = 44.8%, OR 1.94, q = 4.3 × 10−8 ), Stim8hr
(178/399 = 44.6%, OR 1.90, q = 1.0 × 10−8 ), and Stim48hr (183/415 = 44.1%, OR 1.89,
q = 6.0 × 10−9 ).
Pooled annotation-depth control. We next collapsed topology across conditions using the
analysis-specific priority rule Relay > Terminal > Upstream, yielding a fixed universe of 7,181
classified genes, including 762 relay genes. We retain the 2,994 genes whose only classifiable role
is Other, yielding a non-relay background of 6,419 genes. This priority choice changes only the
partition among non-relay roles and cannot alter any relay-versus-rest result. Annotation depth is
the alias-aware PubMed count per gene. Of the 7,181 genes, 3,766 counts were inherited from
existing annotation tables and 3,415 were fetched for this analysis, with no missing genes. To limit
spurious inflation from ambiguous aliases, the 25 genes with counts above 200,000 are winsorized
at 200,000 before transforming annotation depth as log10 (PubMed + 1).
The confounding pathway is present. Relay genes have a median of 261 PubMed papers versus
74 among all non-relay genes (one-sided Mann–Whitney p = 2.7 × 10−46 ). Tractable genes are
also much more studied: medians are 273 versus 50 for small-molecule tractability (p = 6.9 ×
10−216 ), 2,250 versus 79 for approved-drug targets (p = 5.9×10−63 ), and 114 versus 64 for antibody
tractability (p = 4.6 × 10−33 ). Study depth is therefore associated with both relay status and the
