probability of carrying a curated tractability annotation, establishing the proposed confounding
pathway.
Regression and matched-stratum results. For each binary tractability outcome we fit a crude lo-
gistic model, outcome ∼ relay, and an adjusted model, outcome ∼ relay + log10 (PubMed + 1). For
small-molecule tractability the relay coeﬀicient falls from log2 OR = 0.848 (95% CI 0.627–1.070,
p = 6.3 × 10−14 ) to 0.330 (0.092–0.569, p = 0.0066), a 61% attenuation. As a model-independent
check, we stratify genes into PubMed-depth quintiles and combine the five relay-versus-rest 2 × 2
tables with a Cochran–Mantel–Haenszel estimator; the matched OR is 1.25 (95% CI 1.06–1.47,
p = 0.0086). The approved-drug coeﬀicient attenuates by 70% and becomes non-significant after
adjustment (log2 OR = 0.320, 95% CI −0.290–0.930, p = 0.30; matched OR 1.14, p = 0.54). An-
tibody tractability is not enriched in the pooled set even in the crude model (p = 0.23; matched OR
0.95, p = 0.51), although in the per-condition analysis Stim8hr relay genes are antibody-enriched


                                                 56
after BH correction across the nine tested tractability lists (205/399, OR 1.34, q = 0.0087; Supple-
mentary Figure 10). Thus annotation depth explains most of the crude small-molecule enrichment,
but a modest residual remains; the approved-drug association is no longer statistically significant
after adjustment.




                                                57
Supplementary Note 35: Role-specific functional enrichment and the role simplex.
      This note documents the analysis behind the claim that the three topology roles occupy sepa-
rable functional compartments rather than three views of one correlated gene program. The figure
and all counts below are for the Stim8hr condition.
Behavior across stimulation conditions. The same contrast run at Rest and Stim48hr shows that
terminal and upstream biology is stable across the time course while relay biology is not. Terminal
genes are enriched for response-to-stimulus, immune and defense response, and cytokine-response
programs in every condition (IFN-γ response OR 3.9–4.7; IL2–STAT5 OR 4.6–4.9), and upstream
genes for nucleic-acid metabolism, RNA processing, DNA repair, and chromatin terms in every
condition, with only 55–66 significant terms (25–31 clusters) at any timepoint. Relay genes instead
change both in number of significant terms (172 → 293 → 149 from Rest to Stim8hr to Stim48hr)
and in content: a poised, generically lymphoid regulatory program at Rest (lymphocyte differenti-
ation OR 4.8, Myc targets v1 OR 5.5, negative regulation of biosynthetic process OR 2.9), acute
activation hubs at Stim8hr (TNFα–NFκB OR 7.2, αβ T-cell activation OR 8.0, allograft rejection
OR 7.5), and committed lineage and viral/checkpoint regulators at Stim48hr (Th17 cell differentia-
tion OR 7.6, Measles OR 6.4, Epstein–Barr virus infection OR 5.1, mTORC1 signaling OR 4.2).
Enrichment testing. Each topology role is tested against all other measured genes in the same
condition rather than against the other two roles, so that genes falling in neither the role tiers nor
the classifiable set still contribute to the null. Foregrounds are the per-condition role assignments
(Stim8hr: relay 399, terminal 1,207, upstream 1,206 genes) and the background is the full mea-
sured transcriptome for that condition (10,282 genes: relay ∪ terminal ∪ upstream ∪ “other” ∪
“measured-only”). Two engines are run against this identical background and merged. GO:BP,
GO:MF, and KEGG terms come from g:Profiler with g:SCS-adjusted p ≤ 0.05 (symbol mapping
expands g:Profiler’s effective domain to 10,342; a small number of foreground symbols fail to map,
giving query sizes of 397, 1,199, and 1,199). MSigDB hallmark sets (50 terms) are tested locally
by hypergeometric test against the same background with Benjamini–Hochberg FDR ≤ 0.05. Odds
ratios are computed from the 2 × 2 table with a 0.5 continuity correction in every cell, which is
the effect-size metric used throughout; because the two engines use different multiple-testing pro-
cedures, terms are ranked by odds ratio rather than by p-value whenever sources are mixed. This
yields 293, 298, and 65 significant terms for relay, terminal, and upstream genes respectively.
The role simplex. Taking the union of the three significance tables over (source, term ID) gives
486 distinct enriched terms. For each term and role we define the excess odds ratio max(OR − 1, 0),
which is zero wherever a term is not significant in that role, and place the term in a simplex plot
(or ternary plot) at its three-way share of excess odds ratio across roles. A term therefore sits at a
corner when its enrichment is confined to one role, on an edge when two roles share it, and in the
interior only when all three carry it. Points are colored by dominant role (largest excess odds ratio),
sized by peak odds ratio across roles, and given a small deterministic Gaussian jitter so that terms
stacked exactly on a corner remain individually visible. Of the 486 terms, 267 are relay-dominant,
180 terminal-dominant, and 39 upstream-dominant, of which 123 (46%), 157 (87%), and 36 (92%)
respectively are significant in that role alone. The overlap is concentrated predominantly on one
edge: 141 terms are shared by relay and terminal genes and 29 by relay and upstream genes, while
no term is shared between terminal and upstream genes and no term is significant in all three roles.

                                                  58
This is the quantitative content of the visual claim that the simplex interior is empty.
Ranked term lists. The marginal panels rank each role’s terms by odds ratio under two filters that
keep the lists interpretable. First, a term-specificity window restricts to terms annotating 10–500
genes in the background, which removes umbrella annotations such as “immune system process”
(1,621 genes) and “response to stimulus” (4,731 genes) whose enrichment is broad by construction;
the window is equivalent to an information-content threshold of − log2 (500/10,282) ≈ 4.4 bits
and follows the standard gene-set-size bounds used in GSEA-style analyses. Second, within that
window we take each role’s 30 most significant terms (deduplicated by name, keeping the smallest p-
value) and report the 10 with the largest odds ratios, so that the displayed terms are both statistically
well-supported and large in effect. The odds ratios plotted on these axes are the role-versus-all-
other-measured-genes values defined above; axis ranges are scaled per role because the three roles
differ by roughly an order of magnitude in effect size.
Highlighted terms. Terms called out on the simplex are chosen for illustrative purposes, and every
highlighted term also appears in the corresponding ranked list. Three groups are role-exclusive:
relay genes (regulation of CD4+ , αβ T-cell activation, OR = 12.4; CD4+ , αβ T-cell activation,
OR = 9.0), terminal genes (viral protein interaction with cytokine and cytokine receptor, OR = 7.2;
cytokine–cytokine receptor interaction, OR = 4.5), and upstream genes (positive regulation of
double-strand break repair, OR = 4.4; positive regulation of DNA repair, OR = 3.8; positive reg-
ulation of DNA metabolic process, OR = 2.6). A fourth group, drawn in purple, is significant in
both relay and terminal genes (TNFα–NFκB signaling, IFN-γ response, and IL2–STAT5 signal-
ing). Of the 10 strongest terminal terms, 8 are also enriched in relay genes, whereas all 10 of the
strongest upstream terms are upstream-specific — the asymmetry that motivates describing relay
and terminal genes as two layers of a shared activation program and upstream genes as functionally
orthogonal to both.
Caveats. (1) Terms are not redundancy-reduced. The simplex is built from the full significance
tables, so GO parent and child terms are counted separately and the per-role term counts reflect
annotation granularity as much as biological breadth; families such as “T cell activation /
lymphocyte activation / leukocyte activation” inflate the relay and terminal totals in particular.
The pipeline’s Jaccard-clustered representatives (161, 200, and 31 clusters respectively) give the
redundancy-reduced counts, and the corner-versus-interior structure is unchanged when they are
used instead. (2) “Exclusive” is significance-thresholded, not effect-size-thresholded. A term
counts as role-exclusive when the other roles fall below the significance cutoff, which is not the
same as their odds ratios being 1; because terminal and upstream foregrounds are roughly three
times larger than the relay foreground, they have more power to detect weak enrichment, making
relay-exclusivity the more conservative of the two directions. (3) Statistical dependence between
the plotted quantities. The simplex position and the marginal odds ratios derive from the same
tests, so the panels are two views of one analysis rather than independent confirmations. (4) Single
condition. Finally, all numbers here are Stim8hr; relay biology in particular shifts across the
stimulation time course, so these values should not be read as condition-independent properties of
the role.
       Supplementary Table 33 reports the ten displayed terms per topology role, including the
source-specific adjusted p-values and role-gene overlaps.



                                                  59
Supplementary Note 36: Essentiality of relay, upstream, and terminal genes.
      We asked whether relay genes are more essential than the other topology roles in the func-
tional genomics network, which would offer an orthogonal, perturbation-based explanation for their
disease enrichment. We compared genome-wide CRISPR essentiality across the three groups (Sup-
plementary Figure 13).
Group assignment. Each gene is labelled per condition (Rest, Stim8hr, Stim48hr) by its in-/out-
degree relative to the upper-quartile thresholds: relay (in- and out-degree both above the q75 cutoffs),
upstream (high out-degree, low in-degree), and terminal (high in-degree, low out-degree). Because
a gene’s role can differ across conditions, we collapse to a single label per gene using the priority rule
relay > upstream > terminal: a gene is relay if it is a relay gene in any condition, otherwise upstream
if ever upstream, otherwise terminal. This is the same 762-gene any-condition relay set used in the
druggability analysis (Supplementary Note 30). Genes only ever classed “other”/“measured-only”
are excluded. This yields 762 relay, 1,783 upstream, and 1,642 terminal genes.
Essentiality data. We pulled DepMap CRISPR essentiality [66, 67] from Open Targets [61] via a
single batched targets(ensemblIds: [...]) GraphQL query (gene symbols mapped to
Ensembl IDs in batch), retrieving for each gene both (i) the isEssential “common-essential”
flag and (ii) the continuous per-cell-line Chronos gene-effect scores (∼1,183 lines; gene-effect ≈ 0
for a non-essential gene and ≈ −1 at the median of common-essential genes, so more negative
indicates greater essentiality). Of the 4,187 assigned genes, 3,991 (95.3%) had a DepMap record.
For each gene we summarised the continuous readout as the mean gene-effect across cell lines.
Statistics. For the continuous gene-effect we used a Kruskal–Wallis test across the three groups
followed by pairwise two-sided Mann–Whitney U tests, reporting the rank-biserial effect size and
the Hodges–Lehmann median shift. For the binary common-essential rate we used a 3 × 2 χ2 test
followed by pairwise Fisher exact tests of relay against each other group.
Result: out-degree, not relay status, tracks essentiality. The three groups differ overall (Kruskal–
Wallis H = 256.2, p = 2.3 × 10−56 ), but the structure is driven by out-degree rather than by
relay status. Relay and upstream genes — the two high-out-degree groups — are statistically in-
distinguishable in mean gene-effect (median −0.104 vs. −0.103; Mann–Whitney p = 0.89; rank-
biserial −0.004), whereas both are markedly more essential than terminal genes (relay vs. terminal
p = 1.3×10−28 , rank-biserial 0.29; upstream vs. terminal p = 2.9×10−51 , rank-biserial 0.31; termi-
nal median −0.027). The binary common-essential rate mirrors this exactly: relay 12.0% (88/733)
≈ upstream 9.8% (165/1,690) ≫ terminal 3.1% (48/1,568); 3 × 2 χ2 p = 1.1 × 10−17 ; Fisher relay
vs. terminal odds ratio 4.32 (p = 5.3 × 10−16 ), relay vs. upstream odds ratio 1.26 (p = 0.11, n.s.).
Thus relay genes are substantially more essential than terminal (high-in/low-out “sink”) genes, but
no more essential than upstream genes; the essentiality signal tracks broad downstream regulatory
influence (out-degree), the feature relay and upstream genes share.
Caveat. DepMap essentiality is measured in cancer cell lines and therefore reflects generic cellular
fitness rather than CD4+ T-cell–specific dependency; it should be read as a cell-type-agnostic fitness
readout.




                                                   60
Supplementary Note 37: The relay-to-target filtration funnel.
      We ask whether relay topology can prioritize genes that are implicated in autoimmune disease
but lack an established mechanistic hypothesis. We operationalize this as a four-stage subtractive
funnel that begins from a relay∩GWAS gene set and, at each stage, removes a class of genes that are
already well-characterized or non-specific, so that survivors are genetics-supported, non-essential,
mechanistically under-annotated, and supported by colocalization with a disease GWAS signal. The
funnel reduces 82 genes to 4 (82 → 80 → 11 → 4); colocalization analysis of the survivors is
described in Supplementary Note 38 and the prioritized genes in Supplementary Note 39.
Step 1: relay topology intersected with autoimmune GWAS. We define a gene as a relay
if its network-topology classification is relay in at least one stimulated condition (Stim8hr or
Stim48hr); the Rest condition is excluded because relay status under T-cell stimulation is the
disease-relevant signal. This yields 639 stimulated relays (399 in Stim8hr, 415 in Stim48hr). We
define the autoimmune-GWAS reference set as genes with an Open Targets [61] Locus-to-Gene
(L2G) score > 0.5 for at least one of eight autoimmune diseases—celiac disease, Crohn’s disease,
inflammatory bowel disease (IBD), multiple sclerosis (MS), rheumatoid arthritis (RA), systemic
lupus erythematosus (SLE), type 1 diabetes (T1D), and ulcerative colitis (UC)—giving 1,212
unique genes. Their intersection is the 82-gene Step-1 set; 29 are relays in both stimulated
conditions and 53 in exactly one.
Step 2: essentiality removal. Pan-essential genes are poor selective targets, so we remove any
Step-1 gene essential under either of two standard definitions: Hart core-essential (membership in
the Hart et al. CEG/CEG1 constitutive core-essential list [68]) or DepMap common-essential (the
Open Targets target.isEssential flag, mirroring DepMap’s pan-cancer CRISPR designa-
tion; the same essentiality source characterized in Supplementary Note 36). No Step-1 gene is Hart
core-essential (0/82)—expected given the depletion of purifying-selection genes among GWAS
hits—and two are DepMap common-essential: ARPC2 (isEssential=true; mean CRISPR
gene-effect ≈ −0.54 across 1,183 lines) and MYC. Removing these leaves 80 survivors.
Step 3: Open Targets non-genetic annotation removal. To prioritize genetics-supported genes
with limited non-genetic annotation, we remove, from the 80 essentiality survivors, genes that meet
any of the specified Open Targets drug-count, pathway, animal-model, or literature removal criteria
below. We never filter on the genetic_association datatype—the axis the pipeline already
selected on—so the removal is not circular. Rather than the broad “autoimmune disease” parent, we
score against the same eight diseases used above, querying Open Targets per disease and taking, for
each datatype, the maximum score across the eight (a gene meets a datatype removal criterion if its
score reaches the threshold for at least one disease): Crohn’s (EFO_0000384), UC (EFO_0000729),
IBD (EFO_0003767), RA (EFO_0000685), SLE (MONDO_0007915), MS (MONDO_0005301),
T1D (MONDO_0005147), and celiac (EFO_0001060). Gene symbols are resolved to Ensembl
IDs through Open Targets’ own mapIds endpoint (preferring exact symbol matches) to avoid
alias/scaffold mismatches; datatype scores are read from the disease.associatedTargets
field and drug evidence from the target-level drugAndClinicalCandidates count; we as-
sign score 0 for filtering when no association scores are returned for any of the eight diseases. This
convention and the subthreshold scores alone do not establish the absence of all non-genetic ev-
idence or prior mechanistic knowledge, but this is a strong proxy. We queried the Open Targets

                                                 61
Platform v4 GraphQL API on 2026-06-18.
      We apply four removal gates: one target-level drug/clinical-candidate count gate (> 0),
without restricting the count to the eight diseases, and three disease-association datatype score
gates at threshold τ = 0.1 on the 0–1 scale. Each gene exits at the first gate it trips, and
gates are ordered by attribution specificity (most-specific first, most-ambiguous last) so each
removed gene is attributed to its most informative evidence type: (1) existing drug target
(drugAndClinicalCandidates > 0); (2) pathway-corroborated (affected_pathway
≥ τ ); (3) animal-model-corroborated (animal_model ≥ τ ); (4) literature-corroborated
(literature ≥ τ , applied last because it is the least specific). The gates remove 20, 5, 22, and
22 genes respectively, leaving 11 survivors of the specified annotation filter: ANKRD10, ATXN2L,
CALHM6, COPRS, FOSL2, GCN1, NABP1, RTRAF, STARD10, TUFM, and ZBTB21. The
removed genes are canonical autoimmune genes correctly recovered by the relay∩GWAS axis and
removed by design (Supplementary Note 39): e.g. JAK2, IL2RA, ITGAL, ICOS, CD28 (drug);
IRF4, PTPN1 (pathway); ZAP70, CBLB, TNFAIP3, CARD11, BACH2 (animal model); GATA3,
FOXO1, CSK, EGR2, RBPJ (literature).




                                               62
Supplementary Note 38: Colocalization of candidate-gene molecular QTLs with autoimmune
GWAS signals.
      Human genetic association localizes a signal to a locus, not to a gene, e.g., a relay∩GWAS
gene may lie near, but not mediate, its associated variant. The final filter asks whether each candi-
date’s autoimmune GWAS signal and the gene’s own molecular-QTL signal are consistent with a
single shared causal variant, measured by the colocalization posterior probability H4 . A high H4
supports the nominated gene as a plausible effector of the association but does not establish that the
disease association acts through that gene; linkage disequilibrium, pleiotropic variants, and QTLs
for neighboring genes can also produce colocalization.
Method. For each of the 11 annotation-filter survivors (Supplementary Note 37) we retrieve all
Open Targets [61] credible sets (target.credibleSets, SuSiE fine-mapping) and their pair-
wise colocalizations (colocalisation, with per-pair H4 , H3 , clpp, and method). We retain
colocalizations in which (i) the gene’s own credible set is a molecular QTL (eqtl, pqtl, sqtl,
or tuqtl) and (ii) the partner credible set is a GWAS whose disease matches the gene’s relay
autoimmune disease by a prespecified per-gene keyword set (e.g. TUFM → {ulcerative colitis, col-
itis, inflammatory bowel}; ATXN2L → {crohn, inflammatory bowel}). H4 is the posterior prob-
ability of a single shared causal variant between the two credible sets. For each gene we take
the maximum H4 across all qualifying colocalizations and, separately, the maximum restricted to
immune cell types (biosample name matching an immune keyword list spanning T/B/NK cells,
monocytes, macrophages, dendritic cells, blood, PBMC, spleen, and thymus). We assign PASS at
max H4 ≥ 0.80, MODERATE at 0.50 ≤ H4 < 0.80, and FAIL otherwise. Six survivors reuse
credible-set records cached from an earlier iteration of the funnel, and five (CALHM6, FOSL2,
GCN1, RTRAF, TUFM) were fetched live. We queried the Open Targets Platform v4 GraphQL
API on 2026-06-18, paginating credible sets in batches of 50.
Result. Four of eleven genes PASS, none are MODERATE, and seven FAIL; each survivor colo-
calizes with an IBD-spectrum GWAS in an immune-relevant cell type.

    gene       relay disease        max H4   n(H4 ≥ 0.8)   best cell (max H4 )   best immune-cell H4
    TUFM       ulcerative colitis    1.000           60    classical monocyte    1.000 (classical monocyte)
    CALHM6     ulcerative colitis    0.995           16    macrophage            0.995 (macrophage)
    ATXN2L     Crohn’s disease       0.988           97    ovary                 0.949 (macrophage)
    FOSL2      Crohn’s disease       0.968           17    breast epithelium     0.963 (blood)

       For ATXN2L and FOSL2 the single highest-H4 colocalization is in a non-immune biosam-
ple (ovary, breast epithelium), but both retain a high-confidence immune-cell colocalization
(macrophage H4 = 0.949; blood H4 = 0.963), consistent with an immune mechanism; TUFM’s
best cell is a CD14+ CD16− classical monocyte. The seven FAIL genes (ZBTB21, COPRS, GCN1,
STARD10, RTRAF, ANKRD10, NABP1) have no qualifying QTL–GWAS colocalization with
their relay disease at H4 ≥ 0.50. Molecular-QTL evidence derives from Open Targets’ aggregated
QTL studies, including GTEx and immune-cell eQTL/tuQTL resources [69–74]. We retain every
tested colocalization with its cell type, QTL project, GWAS study ID, lead variant, H4 /H3 , and
method.


                                                    63
Supplementary Note 39: Description of the prioritized genes.
       Four genes survive the full funnel (Supplementary Note 37): TUFM (UC), ATXN2L (Crohn’s),
FOSL2 (Crohn’s), and CALHM6 (UC). All four are genetics-supported and non-essential, fall below
the 0.1 Open Targets score threshold for pathway, animal-model, and literature evidence across the
eight tested autoimmune diseases, have a target-level drug/clinical-candidate count of zero, and
colocalize (H4 ≥ 0.8) with an IBD-spectrum GWAS signal through their own molecular QTL
(Supplementary Note 38). To characterize their mechanisms in the relevant cell type, we analyzed
each in the Zhu et al. Perturb-seq atlas [75] across the Rest, Stim8hr, and Stim48hr conditions,
restricted to on-target-significant perturbations without an off-target flag (the QC pool) at FDR
10%. For each gene we define its downstream neighborhood (genes differentially expressed upon
its knockdown), its upstream regulators (perturbations whose knockdown moves the gene, FDR
< 0.1), and its signature phenocopies (perturbations whose knockdown signature, taken over the
gene’s own differentially expressed set, is Pearson-correlated with the gene’s, ranked across the QC
pool). All directional statements are relative to knockdown of the named gene.
TUFM (ulcerative colitis). TUFM is the mitochondrial elongation factor Tu. Its top upstream reg-
ulators are proximal T-cell-receptor (TCR) signaling components—CD3E, PLCG1, LAT, VAV1,
LCP2, ZAP70, CD247—whose knockdown lowers TUFM (productive TCR signaling raises it).
Its signature phenocopies form a mitochondrial-translation and oxidative-phosphorylation cluster
(TARS2, TFAM, LARS2, VARS2, EARS2, LRPPRC, POLG, TWNK, UQCRB/UQCRC2/COX6C,
PDSS1/2, COQ6), confirming that TUFM reads out as its housekeeping role. Upon TUFM knock-
down, an immune-activation program is upregulated (TNF-α/NF-κB, IL-2/STAT5, IFN-γ) and a
growth/biosynthesis program downregulated (Myc and E2F targets, mTORC1). This is a generic
activation-versus-biosynthesis axis, not the autophagy/RIG-I–MAVS mechanism proposed for the
UC locus, i.e., MAVS, NLRX1, PINK1, and ATG16L1 are neither differentially expressed upon
TUFM knockdown nor among its phenocopies.
ATXN2L (Crohn’s disease). ATXN2L is a paralog of ATXN2 that passes the specified annotation
filter, including a literature score below 0.1 across the eight tested autoimmune diseases. Its top
signature phenocopies are RNA-metabolism/stress-granule factors—NUFIP2 (rank 1, r = 0.51),
G3BP2, and its own paralog ATXN2—consistent with its annotated stress-granule role. Its up-
stream regulators are an AP-1 and mitochondrial/metabolic set (BATF3, PMVK, MBTPS1, TFAM,
OXA1L, SLC25A26) whose knockdown raises ATXN2L (they repress it). Upon ATXN2L knock-
down, mitochondrial respiration is upregulated (OXPHOS, respiratory electron transport, TCA cy-
cle; q ≈ 10−7 –10−8 ) and cell-cycle/mitosis modules downregulated. ATXN2L thus resolves to
an RNA-metabolism/stress-granule identity with a mitochondrial-respiration downstream arm—a
concrete, testable hypothesis for a Crohn’s-locus gene with limited non-genetic annotation under
the specified filter.
TUFM and ATXN2L at 16p11.2. TUFM and ATXN2L are immediate neighbors on chromosome
16p11.2 (∼4 kb apart), raising the question of whether their colocalizations reflect one shared locus
or two independent signals. We find that their genetic signals are not independent, but their knock-
down phenotypes differ. First, their colocalizing GWAS lead variants are distinct and ∼10.5 kb
apart, each inside its own gene, but they are in strong linkage disequilibrium (r2 = 0.94 in 1000
Genomes European samples) and so tag a single haplotype that colocalization alone cannot resolve

                                                 64
to one gene. Second, their Perturb-seq downstream neighborhoods barely overlap (Jaccard 0.008 at
Rest, 0.012 at Stim8hr, 0.014 at Stim48hr; 9 shared genes of 652 for TUFM versus 120 for ATXN2L
at Stim8hr) and their knockdown signatures are uncorrelated to slightly anti-correlated (r = −0.11).
Third, they occupy distinct functional programs with negligible, at most one-directional cross-
regulation. These differences support distinct knockdown phenotypes, not independent causal sig-
nals, i.e., given the linkage disequilibrium between the lead variants, the two colocalizations likely
reflect one shared haplotype.
FOSL2 (Crohn’s) and CALHM6 (UC). FOSL2 is an AP-1 transcription factor. Its strongest up-
stream regulator is STAT3, whose knockdown lowers FOSL2 (z = −8.4), consistent with STAT3
occupancy at the FOSL2 promoter during human Th17 differentiation [76]. FOSL2 knockdown
upregulates an IFN-γ/cytotoxic program at Stim48hr. CALHM6 is an immune ion channel with a
small trans-footprint in T cells (8–33 differentially expressed genes per condition); its downstream
program is enriched for cation/ion-channel transport, membrane depolarization, and protein secre-
tion, consistent with its annotated identity, but its colocalization cell type is macrophage, so its
disease mechanism is best followed up in myeloid cells. Together the four genes span the range the
funnel recovers, from a housekeeping mitochondrial factor tuned by TCR signaling to ATXN2L,
for which the Perturb-seq analysis supports an RNA-metabolism/stress-granule hypothesis despite
limited non-genetic annotation under the specified filter.
      For the knockdown-response programs in Supplementary Table 34, we split genes passing
adjusted p < 0.10 by whether expression increased or decreased after knockdown, then tested
each list for over-representation against Enrichr’s default background using the MSigDB Hallmark
2020 and Reactome 2022 libraries. Enrichr returned up to 40 terms per library; q is its Benjamini–
Hochberg adjusted p-value.




                                                 65
Supplementary Note 40: Functional genomics hypothesis-generation comparison.
       Inputs and baseline. We tested whether a coding agent given the initial functional genomics
data and scientific task would independently propose the relay-gene hypothesis. On 3 November
2026, we ran five fresh sessions of Claude Opus 4.6 (claude-opus-4-6) using the default
Claude Code system-prompt preset through claude_agent_sdk version 0.1.69 and Claude
Code CLI version 2.1.121. Each session received the original dataset README and task descrip-
tion, asking whether gene-level knockouts could help interpret non-coding variation. Its data di-
rectory contained the original differential-expression AnnData file (GWCD4i.DE_stats.h5ad),
the README, and an empty downloads directory. The data included the precomputed numbers
of downstream affected genes and upstream regulators. We excluded all derived files from the DIS-
COVERYAGENT investigation, its background research report, and scientist feedback. The paper PDF
mentioned in the README was unavailable in the original sandbox and was also absent from this
comparison.
       Generation protocol. We instructed each session to explore the dataset for about 15 min-
utes, inspect its structure, compute a few summary statistics, and then generate exactly 20 distinct,
testable hypotheses addressing the supplied task. We requested a title, a one-to-three-sentence hy-
pothesis, a rationale grounded in the exploration, and a proposed test identifying any additional data
needed, saved in both Markdown and JSON. The prompt instructed the agent to work autonomously,
avoid long analyses or rigorous hypothesis tests, use only local files, and leave the input data un-
changed. Sessions ran independently with five CPU cores and 128 GB of memory. We imposed
a 45-minute timeout on the initial exploration-and-generation phase, after which the runner could
request final hypotheses in the same session; we imposed no turn limit. All five sessions completed
successfully, each producing 20 valid hypotheses, yielding 100 proposals in total.
       Access controls. Each session used a separate working directory and had access only to
the Read, Write, Edit, Bash, Glob, and Grep tools. We disabled web tools, subagents, account
connectors, automatic memory, and loading of user or project settings. Tool hooks restricted access
to the working directory and source data and screened for paths to DISCOVERYAGENT traces and
other investigation artifacts. We retained the full message and tool logs and audited tool inputs and
outputs for access to excluded artifacts. Across 100 tool calls, the saved audits reported no flags
or live guard denials. These checks determine that there was no observable access to investigation
artifacts.




                                                 66
Supplementary Note 41: Model assignments and run budgets.
     DISCOVERYAGENT assigns a model independently to each agentic component. This note sum-
marizes assignments and budgets from checkpoint configurations, launch files, and runtime logs.
Components. The director agent, hypothesis-generation agent, review agent, review designer, and
the three empirical-memory agents each take their own model setting. In automatic mode, the
hypothesis-generation agent produced a single hypothesis per round, which was passed directly to
the director without a judge; candidate lists for scientist selection are generated in the steering inter-
face (Supplementary Note 45). The execution agent takes its own model, and its documentation sub-
agents inherit from it. The deep-research agent is fixed to Perplexity’s sonar-deep-research
for our experiments, but could support other models in principle. The web-search agent has its
own model and a cap of three web searches per call. A report-summarization model exists but was
disabled in every experiment reported here.
      Supplementary Table 35 lists the per-campaign assignments and budgets.
Budget semantics. The per-round query cap bounds how deeply one hypothesis can be pursued,
whereas the reported rounds count completed research rounds across initial launches and resumes;
DA-Steer’s post-research aggregation step is reported separately. The caps are ten queries for
StarEmbed and both ProteinGym campaigns, increasing to fifteen for DA-Steer Round 10 and its
aggregation step; the latter completed 14 queries. The caps are ten for extragalactic astronomy ex-
cept twenty in Round 9, and 25 for economics and functional genomics. The functional genomics
execution-agent turn cap increases from 30 in Rounds 4–9 to 50 in Rounds 10–12. Compaction,
where enabled, is configured to trigger at 150,000 estimated input tokens. The event logs distinguish
this configuration from execution: compaction actually occurred only in DA-Steer (seven events),
economics (12), and functional genomics (15). Event counts include launches and resumes and are
not counts of distinct completed rounds.
Model configuration. DA-Auto operated automatically and used Sonnet 4.6 throughout the ten-
round campaign. DA-Steer’s per-round configurations record Opus 4.6 for Rounds 0–7 and Son-
net 4.6 for Rounds 8–10. The separate aggregation step used Sonnet 4.6. The extragalactic astron-
omy and economics assignments are supported by checkpoint configurations and runtime model-
resolution events. Functional genomics used Sonnet 4.5 for Rounds 0–2 and Opus 4.6 for Rounds
3–12. The OpenTargets agent used Claude Opus 4.6 with a maximum of 20 turns per invocation.




                                                   67
Supplementary Note 42: Director context compaction.
       Long rounds can drive the director’s conversation past the underlying model’s context limit.
When enabled and supported, the director compacts its own context in place rather than truncating
or failing.
Configuration and observed use. Director context compaction was enabled for StarEmbed, both
ProteinGym campaigns, and all three case studies, with a threshold of 150,000 estimated input
tokens; StarEmbed recorded no completed compaction events. Runtime logs record actual com-
paction only in DA-Steer (seven events), economics (12), and functional genomics (15). DA-Auto
and extragalactic astronomy sessions issued requests with compaction enabled but record no com-
pleted compaction event. Compaction uses Anthropic’s native context-management API and is
gated on an Anthropic Claude Opus 4.6 director and the per-run enable setting.
Triggers. When enabled in the experiments reported here, the configured threshold is 150,000 esti-
mated input tokens. The system also handles a context-overflow error when compaction is disabled:
for a supported director, it enables compaction at the minimum 50,000-token threshold and retries
the request.
What the summary preserves. Compaction replaces earlier conversation turns with a model-
generated summary instructed to retain (i) the original hypothesis or research question, (ii) all
experimental results and statistical findings, (iii) key methodology decisions, (iv) the current in-
vestigation state and outstanding next steps, and (v) any data file paths or column names, while
discarding redundant tool outputs and repeated instructions. This keeps the information needed to
continue the round and to write up its methods and results, while bounding context growth.




                                                68
Supplementary Note 43: Computational resources, budgets, and tool usage.
      This note records the compute allocated to each DISCOVERYAGENT campaign and to each
locally executed baseline, the realized execution budgets, and tool usage. Model assignments and
round or query budgets are separate and are given in Supplementary Note 41 and Supplementary
Table 35.
Execution environment. All runs, both DISCOVERYAGENT campaigns and the coding-agent and
AI-Scientist-v2 baselines, ran on Stanford University’s Sherlock system inside the same Singularity
container image, with one CPU core per job and, where a GPU was requested, a single GPU. Supple-
mentary Table 36 lists the per-job allocations. Two campaigns, economics and functional genomics,
requested no GPU at all; their analyses are tabular and run on CPU. As with DISCOVERYAGENT, all
baseline agents were run with internet access.
Wall-clock and resubmission. Campaigns are long-lived and were resumed from checkpoints
across multiple submissions rather than run to completion in a single job: the elapsed span from
the first to the last round was roughly two days for StarEmbed, twelve days for DA-Steer and for
economics, two months for DA-Auto (which includes the rollback and re-execution described in
Supplementary Note 17), ten weeks for functional genomics, and three months for the extragalac-
tic astronomy campaign. Several individual jobs were terminated at their Slurm time limit and
resumed from the most recent query checkpoint, which is the intended behaviour of the resume
machinery (Supplementary Note 44). Because the calendar span includes scientist deliberation
between rounds, it is an upper bound on compute time and not a measure of it.
Baseline resource control. For the locally executed baselines we matched the class of resource.
Each coding-agent and AI-Scientist-v2 run received a single GPU on the same partition and con-
tainer as DISCOVERYAGENT, with memory and time limits of the same order. Realized execution
budgets differ by design and by outcome. The StarEmbed coding-agent loop ran four continuations
to 625 messages in roughly 1.5 hours; the single-session StarEmbed run was capped at 500 turns and
used 93. On ProteinGym, the Opus 4.6 and Sonnet 4.6 coding-agent loops each received a 48-hour
run ceiling and requested ten continuations; Opus timed out during continuation Round 2, whereas
Sonnet timed out during Round 4. The single-session Opus 4.6 Coding agent@1 run also received
a 48-hour ceiling and unlimited turns; its surviving job log recorded 85 messages. AI-Scientist-v2
caps, by default, each tree-search node at one hour internally. Kosmos and AutoDiscovery were
accessed through hosted interfaces and executed on infrastructure we neither provisioned nor ob-
served, so no hardware, wall-clock, or budget record exists for them and the resource-parity state-
ment does not extend to those two systems. Different CPU memory sizes were used across agents
based on available resources at the time of running (Supplementary Table 36), but we observed no
out-of-memory errors that would indicate that the allocation constrained the agent’s strategy.
Tool usage. Which tool agents were available is fully determined by the configurations: the census
agent was enabled only for economics, the OpenTargets agent only for functional genomics, and
BioMCP was added as an execution-agent skill from Round 4 of the functional genomics campaign,
where its invocations were recorded in Rounds 4, 10, and 12. Reviewer invocations are recorded
per campaign in the action logs: 9 for StarEmbed, 42 for DA-Steer, 78 for economics, and 132
for functional genomics, with none recorded for DA-Auto or the extragalactic astronomy campaign


                                                69
despite a configured review interval. A round-0 deep-research background report was generated for
every campaign, so deep research contributed to hypothesis seeding throughout; statements about
deep research being used in a single campaign refer to mid-round dispatches by the director, not to
background-report generation.




                                                70
Supplementary Note 44: Checkpointing and version control.
      DISCOVERYAGENT records two parallel provenance trails: a checkpoint tree capturing the state
of the agents, and a version-control history capturing the state of the code and data.
Checkpoint store. Checkpoints are organized by round and query. Each per-query checkpoint con-
tains a structured record of the workflow state—dataset and task descriptions, the round and query
indices, intermediate results, the running report, the active reviewer prompt, and run-configuration
flags—together with the serialized state of the director, the hypothesis-generation agent, and the
registered tool agents (each with an integrity hash), and a human-readable rendering of the query
and its result. Each round additionally records its starting configuration, the round report before
memory integration, the accumulated memory report after integration, the round log, any generated
reviewer prompt, and, where applicable, the selected hypothesis and the candidate list used to seed
the round.
Version control. When git tracking is enabled, the execution agent’s working directory is initialized
as a git repository and its changes are committed as the run proceeds. Commits stage the working-
directory contents (skipping files above a size cap) and carry a message tagged by round, producing
a navigable, diffable history of the code, data, and figures generated during the run. Keeping this
history separate from the checkpoint tree lets an auditor reconstruct both what the agents were
reasoning over and what was on disk at any step.




                                                 71
Supplementary Note 45: Hypothesis-steering interface and workflow handoff.
    The hypothesis-steering interface is a checkpoint-aware web application that connects the
completed state of one research round to the hypothesis-directed execution of the next.
Round context and candidate generation. The application initializes from a completed round
directory and reads the run configuration, dataset and task descriptions, latest round report, and
accumulated memory report. When available, it also loads the deep-research background, report
summaries, research paper, and supplied background documents. The scientist can inspect the
background and latest-round reports before generation and provide initial guidance that is appended
verbatim to the hypothesis-generation context. The number of candidates is configurable. In the
default path, the hypothesis-generation agent produces all candidates in one model call, with in-
structions to vary the primary scientific factor across candidates and to return a title, one-sentence
hypothesis, two-to-three-paragraph justification, and an optional analysis plan.
Inspection and revision. The interface provides separate operations for asking questions without
changing a hypothesis, requesting an agent-written revision, manually editing the text, regenerating
one candidate, and regenerating the full candidate set. Candidate and report chats maintain conver-
sational state within the session, and compact summaries can be imported between chat contexts.
Evidence can be inspected through rendered round and background reports, report-grounded chat, a
read-only code assistant, and a read-only checkpoint browser. These mechanisms give the scientist
access to both the empirical record and the implementation underlying it while keeping hypothesis
revision distinct from code execution.
Finalization and provenance. The scientist finalizes either a generated candidate or custom text.
The application records the selected content in the round directory and retains a timestamped copy;
the artifact contains the hypothesis and justification, optional plan, incorporated expert feedback,
and optional overall research feedback. Overall guidance is recorded separately for the next round.
When a scientist explicitly submits reviewed final text, that exact text is treated as the authoritative
selection. The final hypothesis object contains the hypothesis text along with the optional guidance.
Workflow handoff. In human-selection mode, the next DISCOVERYAGENT round loads the selected-
hypothesis artifact from the preceding completed round. The director workflow disables exploratory
execution when a selection is present and inserts the full text into a human-selection prompt on the
first query. Because the selected artifact includes both the focal hypothesis and any overall research
feedback, both remain in the director’s conversational context as it dispatches subsequent queries.
The corresponding round and query checkpoint contents are described in Supplementary Note 44.
User interface implementation. We hosted the web-based user interface (UI) on a remote server
during the experiments so that participant scientists did not need to host the agent locally. The
remote deployment introduced latency for audit and hypothesis-generation agents, so we have also
released a local version.




                                                  72
Supplementary Note 46: StarEmbed round-by-round research trajectory.
    The reported trace comprises exploratory Round 0 and three hypothesis-driven rounds.
Round 0 — preprocessing and model choice. The agent characterized the seven-class imbalance
and the period, amplitude, and color distributions, then adopted balanced accuracy as the primary
metric. It engineered Fourier features from phase-folded light curves. Its first Random Forest
reached 14.27% balanced accuracy, at chance for seven classes, and a period-only model performed
equally poorly (14.43%). Our post hoc inspection traced this failure to a pipeline error: merging
the Fourier features introduced 52 duplicated rows, and truncating the merged table misaligned
features and labels for 98% of objects. The agent’s next script carried labels through the merge,
restoring alignment, and reached 83.68%. The agent attributed this gain to log transformation,
outlier clipping, and standardization; because Random Forests are invariant to monotonic feature
transforms, the improvement instead reflects the corrected alignment. Switching to LightGBM
provided a stronger baseline for subsequent rounds.
Round 1 — phase-resolved colour. The agent tested whether phase-folded g − r and r − i tra-
jectories added class information beyond single-band morphology. Combining these trajectories
with per-band and Fourier features produced the final 46-feature representation and 84.67% cross-
validated balanced accuracy. The incremental gain over the single-band model was not statistically
detectable, however, and several comparisons worsened performance on the rare RRc class, moti-
vating a more targeted analysis of that failure mode.
Round 2 — hierarchical classification. The agent grouped variable stars into broad physical
classes and tested whether routing them to specialist classifiers would improve the diﬀicult
RRab/RRc distinction. It also added rise-time and Fourier-phase asymmetry features intended to
separate these subclasses. Although specialists performed better under oracle routing, errors in the
learned router erased that benefit, and the flat LightGBM remained stronger overall.
Round 3 — RRc error analysis. The agent tested whether persistent RRc errors reflected label
contamination rather than model limitations. Cross-matching ten targeted examples against VizieR
found agreement with the source labels, and removing examples flagged by multiple contamina-
tion indicators reduced balanced accuracy from 83.16% to 79.68%. A Bailey-diagram analysis
instead found that 90.5% of RRc objects lay within the RRab convex hull, supporting the main-text
conclusion that overlap in the available feature space limits separation.
      The trace therefore selected the flat LightGBM with all 46 features for held-out evaluation.
We applied its recommendation for validation-fold early stopping when training the final model
(Supplementary Note 6).




                                                73
Supplementary Note 47: DA-Auto ProteinGym round-by-round research trajectory.
      DA-Auto completed ten automatically selected rounds (0–9). We report the leakage-free tra-
jectory; after a superseded Round 7 accessed published ProteinGym scores, we restored the end-
of-Round-5 checkpoint and reran the remaining work under hardened containment (Supplementary
Note 17).
Round 0 — transferable baselines. The agent compared substitution matrices, multiple-sequence-
alignment (MSA) profiles, AlphaFold confidence, physicochemical features, trained feature combi-
nations, and ESM-2 masked-marginal scores. ESM-2 was the strongest transferable starting point,
whereas trained cross-protein combinations and naive position-specific scoring matrices did not
generalize to held-out proteins.
Round 1 — sequence-model ensemble. The agent tested whether scaling ESM-2 from 650M to
3B parameters improved mutation scoring and whether ESM-1v added a complementary sequence
signal. The larger ESM-2 model performed worse in the controlled comparison, whereas averaging
ESM-2 and ESM-1v improved the broader stratified benchmark.
Round 2 — inverse folding. The agent added ESM-IF1 to test whether a structure-conditioned
score complemented the two sequence models. The equal-weight three-model ensemble improved
eight of ten proteins and raised mean Spearman correlation from 0.423 for ESM-2 alone to 0.516,
establishing the multimodal ensembling strategy used in later rounds.
Round 3 — assay-scale inference. The agent scaled the sequence–structure ensemble from small
comparisons to the full collection of assays. This round established long-sequence windowing,
fallbacks for unavailable structures, and row-order checks needed to produce complete and aligned
mutation scores.
Round 4 — ensemble weights. With complete ESM-1v and ESM-IF1 caches, the agent compared
231 weighting schemes for the three-model ensemble. Mean validation Spearman reached 0.5192,
but the broad plateau around equal weighting indicated that the gain came from complementary
modalities rather than precise weight tuning.
Round 5 — ProteinMPNN. The agent introduced ProteinMPNN as a second inverse-folding fam-
ily and tested checkpoint ensembling, score normalization, and long-sequence handling. Protein-
MPNN added a modest ∼0.005 mean-Spearman gain, whereas extra checkpoints and normalization
added little after blending.
Round 6 — MSA Transformer. The agent tested whether direct MSA-conditioned masked-
marginal scores could add evolutionary information not captured by the sequence models. After
correcting the treatment of lowercase insertions in A2M alignments, MSA Transformer improved
several diﬀicult viral assays and modestly strengthened the ensemble, although some mutations
still lacked mapped scores.
Round 7 — adaptive combination. The agent tested nonlinear fusion and routing weights based
on assay metadata. Generic nonlinear rules degraded performance, but assigning more structural
weight to stability assays improved the internal benchmark; sequence length, taxon, and MSA depth
did not provide useful routing rules.
Round 8 — additional pretrained models. The agent evaluated SaProt’s joint sequence–structure

                                               74
tokens and screened several additional protein language models. Rank-normalized SaProt scores
added complementary information, whereas the other accessible models did not improve the en-
semble suﬀiciently to justify broad inclusion.
Round 9 — final blending. The agent tested assay-category-specific weights and a repaired Tran-
ception integration on a subset of proteins, then consolidated the components that improved internal
validation. The final zero-shot predictor combined sequence, alignment, and structure-conditioned
scores as described in Supplementary Note 10. This progression underlies the main-text contrast
between DA-Auto’s pretrained-score ensemble and DA-Steer’s supervised feature interactions.




                                                75
Supplementary Note 48: DA-Steer ProteinGym round-by-round research trajectory.
      DA-Steer completed 11 rounds (0–10), including one exploratory round and ten scientist-
intervention rounds, followed by a separate model-aggregation step.
Round 0 — supervised multimodal baseline. The agent built a LightGBM baseline that learned
from mutation-level DMS labels using ESM-2 scores, amino-acid and physicochemical changes,
sequence position, and coarse structural features. Naive alignment profiles performed poorly and
were omitted until their construction could be corrected in a later round.
Round 1 — evolutionary couplings. The agent tested whether Potts-model couplings, filtered by
spatial proximity in AlphaFold structures, could capture epistatic constraints. The features were
unstable and did not improve the supervised baseline, so the campaign shifted toward pretrained
structure-conditioned scores.
Round 2 — inverse folding. The agent added ProteinMPNN log-likelihood ratios to test whether
structure-conditioned mutation scores complemented ESM-2. Mean Spearman increased from
0.5251 to 0.5363, a modest but detectable gain that established structural information as useful
when represented compactly.
Round 3 — dense sequence embeddings. The agent replaced the scalar ESM-2 likelihood score
with dense hidden-state differences at the mutated residue. These embeddings helped when the
same proteins appeared in training and validation but failed to transfer across proteins, exposing
the central generalization constraint of the supervised setting.
Round 4 — contrastive sequence–structure learning. The agent tested whether contrastively
aligning sequence and local structural environments would produce more transferable mutation
features. The learned representation underperformed scalar ESM-2 scores on held-out proteins
and was not retained.
Round 5 — residue-contact graphs. Guided toward richer structural modeling, the agent repre-
sented each protein as a residue-contact graph and learned mutation-centered embeddings with a
graph neural network. These embeddings improved over four hand-engineered structural features
in the controlled comparison, motivating the graph-attention branch retained in the final model.
Round 6 — structural descriptors. The agent tested whether explicit secondary-structure, ge-
ometry, hydrogen-bonding, packing, and solvent-exposure features would especially help stability
assays. The added descriptors reduced mean Spearman both overall and within stability assays,
reinforcing the use of learned graph features rather than a larger hand-engineered structural set.
Round 7 — broader training data. The agent tested whether increasing the supervised training
set from 121 to all 217 assays improved cross-protein transfer. Mean Spearman rose from 0.5191
to 0.5397, making data scale one of the clearest gains in the campaign. The expansion also exposed
an organism-name matching bug affecting approximately 30% of the data, which the agent repaired
before later rounds.
Round 8 — corrected alignment profiles. The agent revisited MSA features with a repaired
A2M parser and weighted position-specific log odds, entropy, and gap statistics. These corrected
evolutionary summaries supplied complementary signal and became part of the final feature set.


                                               76
Round 9 — graph and covariation features. The agent jointly tested richer contact-graph embed-
dings and pairwise MSA covariation intended to capture epistasis. The graph branch added value,
whereas pseudo-direct-coupling features did not consistently improve transfer and were not central
to the final model.
Round 10 — neural feature fusion. The agent tested whether a neural mixture-of-experts model
could learn interactions among dense sequence, alignment, and structure representations more ef-
fectively than a tree model. On the same 24-assay comparison, the neural model reached 0.603
mean Spearman versus 0.619 for LightGBM, supporting the simpler supervised learner used in the
final predictor.
Final aggregation. The final step trained and compared the selected candidates across all 217 as-
says under a common evaluation setup. It selected the supervised 98-feature LightGBM used in the
main text, which combines scalar sequence scores, physicochemical changes, corrected alignment
summaries, and graph-derived structure features (Supplementary Notes 9 and 8). This model em-
bodies the campaign’s main finding that compact, complementary features transferred better than
dense end-to-end representations.




                                               77
Supplementary Note 49: Human audit error matching and adjudication.
Inputs and reference catalog. We evaluate the eight participants, four per cohort, using 54 orig-
inal reported findings. We fix finding boundaries before judging, retaining the submitted wording
and removing empty response templates, time summaries, and appended checklists or interface in-
structions outside the finding blocks. We use the original findings rather than paraphrases in the
historical assignment table, which can change the substance of a criticism. The applied-error log
records the original and corrupted wording for 37 numbered entries. We exclude error 13, which
was fully superseded by error 18, and restrict error 15 to its retained sub-percentage changes; its
superseded main coverage value belongs to error 27. This yields 36 active catalog identifiers. Com-
pound entries, such as error 17, which changes both vocabulary size and softmax dimension, remain
single catalog units.
Matching rubric and blinding. To prevent human bias in matching errors, we use an indepen-
dent LLM-as-a-judge system to match submitted errors to our ground-truth catalog. We provide
the judge with one participant’s findings and the complete active catalog, withholding participant
names, cohort labels, historical assignments, and other participants’ judgments. The judge must
identify the specific altered claim challenged by each finding, rather than match on shared termi-
nology, a nearby location, or incidental quotation of a corrupted value. A concrete contradiction
or paraphrase can suﬀice without an exact corrected value, but the judge may not infer an unstated
criticism or expand the participant’s allegation. Criticisms of unplanted methodological, presenta-
tion, or code issues receive no match regardless of their potential validity. One finding may match
multiple distinct catalog entries, and separate findings may match the same entry; identifying a
specific component of a compound error can match that entry once. The judge records genuinely
ambiguous candidates separately, and we do not count these as positive matches. We require a
supporting quotation and a concise explanation for each positive assignment, and an explanation
for each finding-level decision. The rubric contains synthetic examples unrelated to this study and
is fixed before examining the repeated judgments.
Independent repetitions and validation. We make five independent OpenAI Responses API calls
per participant using gpt-5.6-sol, medium reasoning effort, and a maximum output allowance
of 16,000 tokens, with no tools or shared conversation state. Each call includes all findings from
one participant. We deterministically shuffle the catalog once per participant and retain the same
ordering and prompt across that participant’s five calls. Structured JSON outputs identify each find-
ing, positive catalog matches, uncertain candidates, and supporting evidence. We check complete
finding coverage, valid catalog identifiers, and absence of duplicate or contradictory identifiers
within each finding. We preserve input hashes, exact requests, model and response identifiers, raw
responses, and parsed decisions.
Robustness results. The positive catalog-ID set is identical across all five repetitions for 53 of
54 findings (98.1%). Comparing the complete match set for each finding across the ten pairs of
repetitions gives 536 agreements in 540 comparisons (99.3%). These measures include agreement
on empty match sets. For the one participant with variable matches, the mean positive-set Jaccard
similarity across repetitions is 0.90 and pooled positive agreement is 0.9375, where positive agree-
ment is twice the summed intersection size divided by the summed sizes of the two sets across


                                                 78
repetition pairs. Both measures equal 1 for the other three participants with positive matches; they
are undefined for the four participants with no positive matches. All 40 API responses complete
successfully, but five fail the initial strict quotation check. Across 86 positive assignments, 57 quo-
tations are contiguous verbatim excerpts, 24 use ellipses whose fragments we verify in source order,
and five are non-verbatim and remain flagged for review. We retain all original judgments without
resampling or rewriting them, auditing quotation fidelity separately from matching stability.
Manual adjudication. We compare the repeated assignments with the historical table and man-
ually adjudicate the two findings with variable matches or substantive disagreement. One author
makes these decisions after inspecting the findings, corresponding catalog entries, and matching
results; this adjudication is not blinded. For participant R01, finding 9, one of five runs matches
error 6, whereas four return no match. We assign no match because the finding criticizes the justifi-
cation of the 0.7/0.3 objective weighting without identifying its replacement by 0.5/0.5 in the paper.
For participant R04, finding 4, all five runs match both error 8 and error 4, whereas the historical
assignment includes only error 8. We retain only error 8: the finding identifies the context-window
alteration from 1,022 to 512 tokens, but does not match the catalog’s protein-count alteration from
16 to 12; the participant instead proposes a count of three. We record the explicit decisions and their
input hashes separately from model outputs, apply these two overrides, and retain the unanimous
five-run match sets for every other finding.
Scoring units and interpretation. In addition to the total number of reported findings, we distin-
guish three counts for each participant: the number of reported findings with at least one positive
match, the number of finding–catalog matching assignments, and the number of unique catalog
identifiers recovered. We deduplicate identifiers across a participant’s findings for unique-error
recovery and use 36 as its denominator. Assignment counts can exceed the number of matched
findings because a finding can identify multiple errors, and can exceed unique-error counts because
several findings can identify the same error. We summarize participant-level counts within cohorts;
repeated API calls are not additional human participants. The adjudicated tables contain all three
scoring units, while original model outputs and historical assignments are documented separately.
      Supplementary Table 11 reports the manually adjudicated participant counts and cohort sum-
maries.




                                                 79
Supplementary Note 50: Economics round-by-round research trajectory.
      The economist-steered campaign completed seven rounds (0–6). We summarize the trace
findings here; Supplementary Notes 26 and 28 describe the post hoc extensions emphasized in the
main text.
Round 0 — broad ACS exploration. The agent surveyed several questions spanning education,
housing affordability, demographic sorting, and population growth in the American Community
Survey. Among these directions, rising housing costs offered the clearest path to a focused de-
scriptive investigation, and the economist steered the next round toward homeowners insurance in
disaster-exposed markets.
Round 1 — hurricane exposure and insurance burden. The agent compared insurance-to-
income ratios across three hurricane-exposed states and three less-exposed states using 2023 ACS
housing microdata. Burden was higher and more dispersed in hurricane-exposed states, with
Louisiana and Florida roughly twice the income share observed in Minnesota and Ohio. A parallel
test did not support the proposed link between premium changes and out-migration, so later rounds
focused on affordability rather than migration.
Round 2 — homeownership rates. The agent tested whether higher insurance-to-value ratios
were associated with lower homeownership, particularly among middle-income households near the
ownership margin. None of 17 Public Use Microdata Area (PUMA)-level specifications produced
the predicted robust negative relationship, weakening a direct homeownership-rate interpretation.
Round 3 — recent movers. The agent refined the hypothesis by asking whether insurance costs
act as an entry barrier specifically for recent movers even when aggregate homeownership effects
are weak. The apparent mover-specific relationship disappeared after controlling for migration
intensity, so the data did not support this proposed mechanism.
Round 4 — population growth and tenure. The agent tested whether the association between in-
surance burden and population growth survived richer demographic, economic, and housing-market
controls and whether affected areas shifted toward renting. Housing-supply controls removed the
growth association, while the renter composition of movers still shifted. This mixed result mo-
tivated a closer examination of tenure and model specification rather than a causal claim about
regional growth.
Round 5 — tenure duration and specification sensitivity. The agent tested whether insurance
effects attenuated with length of residence and replicated across states, years, and county-level
changes. The proposed tenure gradient failed, and the coeﬀicient’s sign depended on whether bur-
den was measured as insurance-to-value, insurance-to-income, or log premium. These results led
the economist to frame the robust contribution as a descriptive change in insurance burden rather
than an estimated causal effect on tenure.
Round 6 — robust descriptive framing and Florida trends. The agent tested whether insurance-
to-income produced a stable negative homeownership association across tenure bands, geographies,
and demographic groups; it did not, and log premiums with income entered separately provided the
least mechanically coupled descriptive specification. Narrowing to Florida then revealed the central
temporal result: the weighted-median annual premium increased from $1,400 in 2018 to $2,200 in


                                                80
2023, with the distribution shifting broadly upward. This finding motivated the main-text follow-up
analyses showing a worsening and regressive burden and examining its implications for buy-versus-
rent and coverage decisions.
      Supplementary Table 21 reports the Round 1 PUMA-level distributions across the six states.
Each PUMA retains owner-occupied housing units with positive insurance expenditure, and its
insurance-to-income ratio is the WGTP-weighted mean of INSP divided by the WGTP-weighted
mean of HINCP. The displayed percentiles and medians are unweighted across PUMAs, and “in-
sured owners” sums the PUMA-level WGTP-weighted household counts.




                                                81
Supplementary Note 51: Functional genomics round-by-round research trajectory.
      The campaign ran 13 rounds (0–12) on a genome-scale CRISPRi Perturb-seq screen of pri-
mary human CD4+ T cells [75] across Rest, 8-hour stimulation, and 48-hour stimulation. The
screen covered 12,748 perturbations over four donors and 10,282 measured genes. The trajectory
below focuses on the tests that led to the relay-gene hypothesis and the qualifications emphasized
in the main text.
Round 0 — network exploration. The agent characterized how perturbation effects changed across
the resting and stimulated CD4+ T-cell states and examined whether disease-associated genes occu-
pied distinctive positions in the inferred regulatory network. The exploratory results linked network
topology with autoimmune GWAS annotations, motivating later rounds to test whether regulatory
position was more informative than perturbation magnitude alone.
Round 1 — effect-size metric. The agent compared raw log fold-changes with z-scores as the
common measure of perturbation effect across guides and conditions. Because standard errors
varied substantially across perturbations, z-scores reduced false positives and variance inflation
and were adopted for subsequent network analyses.
Round 2 — perturbation magnitude. The agent tested whether putative causal genes at au-
toimmune genome-wide association study (GWAS) loci produced broader or stronger expression
changes than neighboring genes. The hypothesis was not supported within loci, and removing
an on-target self-counting artifact weakened the apparent association further. This negative result
shifted the investigation from the magnitude of a perturbation to the gene’s structural position in
the regulatory network.
Round 3 — regulatory breadth and paralogs. The agent tested whether paralog compensation
explained weak effects among GWAS genes and whether disease-associated genes differed in reg-
ulatory breadth. The proposed compensation mechanism was not supported, but GWAS genes had
greater genome-wide out-degree than the background set, motivating the focus on regulatory hubs.
(We exclude the paralog conclusions because a post hoc audit found that the analysis partly grouped
genes by symbol prefix; the out-degree result does not depend on that definition.)
Round 4 — condition specificity. The agent tested whether autoimmune GWAS genes were more
condition-specific than primary-immunodeficiency (PID) genes across Rest, Stim8hr, and Stim48hr.
The predicted difference was absent, but genes belonging to both disease sets were among the
broadest regulators. Together with the out-degree result from Round 3, this finding motivated the
joint in-degree–out-degree definition tested in Round 5.
Round 5 — relay-gene hypothesis. The agent defined “relay genes” as genes in the upper quartiles
of both in-degree and out-degree and tested whether they were enriched for autoimmune GWAS
genes. At Stim8hr, relay genes had a 19.0% autoimmune GWAS hit rate (76/399), compared with
7.5% among all non-relay genes (440/5,880; OR 2.91, BH-adjusted q = 7.2 × 10−12 ). In-degree
explained most of the association, but the combined relay indicator contributed additional signal,
establishing the central hypothesis developed in the main text.
Round 6 — feedback loops. The agent tested whether reciprocal regulatory feedback, rather than
joint in- and out-degree, explained the disease enrichment. Feedback-loop membership added no


                                                 82
predictive value beyond relay status, and degree-preserving randomization was also null. This result
narrowed the relevant topology to the relay definition.
Round 7 — regulatory directionality. The agent next tested whether GWAS genes preferentially
activated or repressed their targets and whether this directionality varied with feedback complexity.
Directional asymmetry changed with the number of feedback interactions but did not distinguish
GWAS from non-GWAS genes. The original index includes the on-target transcript; excluding it
removes the feedback-associated trend without revealing a GWAS/non-GWAS difference. Together,
Rounds 6 and 7 strengthened the relay hypothesis by eliminating two alternative topological expla-
nations.
Round 8 — rare-disease genetics. The agent tested whether PID genes, which are implicated by
rare Mendelian variants, were primarily upstream regulators as initially predicted. Instead, they
were depleted from the upstream-only class and were even more strongly enriched among relay
genes than autoimmune GWAS genes (reported OR 8.91). This result connected common- and
rare-variant disease evidence through the same network topology.
Round 9 — selective constraint. The agent asked whether genes with relay topology were also
subject to stronger evolutionary constraint. Relay genes had 4.4× higher median shet than back-
ground genes, and out-degree retained a positive relationship with constraint after accounting for
expression. This established the constraint result highlighted in the main text while showing that
topology explains only part of the variation among genes.
Round 10 — cross-cell-type generalization. The agent tested the relay hypothesis in a K562
Perturb-seq network to separate general topological properties from immune-context-specific as-
sociations. The relationship between relay status and constraint replicated, whereas autoimmune
GWAS enrichment did not, suggesting that disease enrichment depends on the relevant cell type.
One reported finding did not survive standardized reproduction: only 6% of CD4+ relay genes
were also relays in K562, rather than the broad overlap reported during the trace (Supplementary
Note 33).
Round 11 — within-locus comparison. The agent returned to the original question of whether
network topology distinguishes candidate genes within the same GWAS locus. Relay enrichment
weakened and reversed under within-locus comparisons, while in-degree was more informative than
out-degree.
Round 12 — druggability. The agent tested whether the regulatory position of relay genes corre-
sponded to therapeutic tractability in Open Targets. Relay genes were enriched for small-molecule
tractability (reported OR 2.21), motivating the main-text reproduction with an independent tractabil-
ity set. In later reproductions, the reproduced association remained but was smaller after controlling
for publication depth, whereas the apparent enrichment for genes with approved drugs disappeared
(Supplementary Note 34).
Trace versus reproduction. The round-level values use the gene sets available during the trace.
Post hoc reproduction with fixed reference sets yielded OR 2.44 for autoimmune GWAS enrichment
and OR 11.66 for PID enrichment; the main text uses these reproduced values for validated claims
and the original values only when describing the research trajectory.
Detailed trace tables. Supplementary Table 25 reports the Round 5 degree-binned GWAS hit rates


                                                 83
for 6,279 Stim8hr genes, including 516 autoimmune L2G genes at score > 0.5. In-degree deciles
contain equal numbers of genes with row order breaking ties; out-degree bins retain tied degrees
together, with degrees 0, 1, and 2 separate and higher cut points obtained by rounding the original
decile boundaries down. Supplementary Table 26 reports the Round 7 directional asymmetry index,
(ndown − nup )/(ndown + nup ), where a feedback partner has significant effects in both directions at
adjusted p < 0.10; the displayed within-bin GWAS-versus-non-GWAS Mann–Whitney tests were
computed for the table rather than during the agent run. Supplementary Table 27 reports GeneBayes
shet for the Round 9-specific role assignment over 10,153 genes, where in-degree ≥ 74 and out-
degree ≥ 14 define the 485 relays; these roles differ from the standardized 399-relay Stim8hr set.
Its per-role contrasts use one-sided Mann–Whitney tests with the alternative that relay values are
greater, and the four-role comparison uses Kruskal–Wallis. Supplementary Table 28 reports the
Round 12 Open Targets tractability flags for the 6,279 classified Stim8hr genes; the 52 genes absent
from that annotation table count as non-tractable, “Other clinical” is the other-clinical-modalities
flag, and the displayed Fisher tests compare relay with other genes rather than all non-relay genes.

Supplementary Figure 1: The DISCOVERYAGENT inner loop adaptively coordinates tool use and review within a
hypothesis round. The director agent selects one toolbox agent—execution, deep research, web search, or an optional
domain-specific agent—and sends it a natural-language query derived from the current hypothesis and accumulated
evidence. The selected agent returns a query report that informs the director’s next decision and is also available to
the review agent. At configurable intervals, the review agent evaluates the cumulative report and returns a mid-round
critique to the director, which can revise the next query in response. This cycle continues until the director terminates
the round or exhausts its query budget.

Supplementary Figure 6: Topology × genetic-evidence enrichment across CD4+ T-cell conditions. Columns
show Rest, Stim8hr, and Stim48hr, with relay/classifiable-background sizes of 344/6,052, 399/6,279, and 415/6,396,
respectively. The six row blocks show autoimmune GWAS, primary immunodeficiency (PID), erythrocyte GWAS,
lymphocyte-count GWAS, cardiovascular-control, and eye-control evidence sets; rows within each block show Relay,
Terminal, Upstream, and Other topology classes. Markers show log(OR), whiskers show 95% Woolf intervals, and the
dashed line marks no enrichment. Tests and interval estimates follow Supplementary Note 32; stars report unadjusted
one-sided Fisher enrichment tests (∗ p < 0.05, ∗ ∗ p < 0.01, ∗ ∗ ∗ p < 0.001). Autoimmune GWAS and PID genes are
enriched among relay genes in all three conditions. Full results are in Supplementary Table 31.

Supplementary Figure 7: Autoimmune GWAS genes are enriched among CD4+ T-cell relay genes across seven
of eight diseases. Each disease row tests whether genes linked by Open Targets Locus-to-Gene (L2G) scores > 0.5 are
over-represented in the Stim8hr CD4+ T-cell relay set (399 genes; upper-quartile definition, Supplementary Note 30),
relative to all other genes in the 6,279-gene classifiable background. Points show odds ratios (Haldane–Anscombe 0.5
correction), horizontal lines show 95% Woolf log confidence intervals, and the dashed line marks an odds ratio of 1.
Right-hand annotations give relay hits divided by total in-background GWAS hits; the pooled row uses the union of
all eight disease gene sets. Stars report unadjusted one-sided Fisher exact enrichment tests (∗ p < 0.05, ∗ ∗ p < 0.01,
∗ ∗ ∗ p < 0.001). Abbreviations: MS, multiple sclerosis; IBD, inflammatory bowel disease; RA, rheumatoid arthritis;
UC, ulcerative colitis; SLE, systemic lupus erythematosus; T1D, type 1 diabetes.




Supplementary Figure 8: Relay-gene enrichment is robust to the L2G threshold. Sensitivity of relay-versus-
rest genetic-evidence enrichment to the Open Targets Locus-to-Gene (L2G) score cutoff. The three panels report
autoimmune GWAS genes in CD4+ T cells (Stim8hr), erythrocyte GWAS genes in CD4+ T cells (Stim8hr), and
erythrocyte GWAS genes in K562. Points show odds ratios from category-versus-rest 2 × 2 Fisher tests across score
cutoffs 0.1–0.9; shaded bands show 95% confidence intervals, the dashed horizontal line marks an odds ratio of 1, and
the vertical line marks the main-text cutoff (> 0.5). Slanted labels give the L2G reference-set size at each cutoff. Stars
denote one-sided Fisher enrichment tests (∗ p < 0.05, ∗ ∗ p < 0.01, ∗ ∗ ∗ p < 0.001).

Supplementary Figure 9: Topology × genetic-evidence enrichment across four cell lines. Forest plot of relay-gene
genetic-evidence enrichment, extending the two-cell main-text panel (Figure 6i) to four perturbation-screen cell lines.
Columns: CD4+ T cells (8-hour stimulation), K562 (erythroleukemia) [62], HCT116 (colon), and HEK293T (kid-
ney). Row blocks (right labels): six genetic-evidence gene sets — autoimmune GWAS, primary immunodeficiency
(PID), erythrocyte (MCH+RDW+IRF) GWAS, lymphocyte-count GWAS, and cardiovascular and eye GWAS controls.
Within each block, the four network-topology classes (relay, terminal, upstream, other) are tested for over-representation
of the evidence set against the rest of the classifiable background (category-vs-rest 2 × 2 Fisher’s exact test). Markers
show log(OR), whiskers the 95% Woolf confidence interval, and the dashed line marks log(OR) = 0 (no enrich-
ment). Stars give the one-sided (enrichment) Fisher p-value, coloured by topology class (∗ p < 0.05, ∗ ∗ p < 0.01,
∗ ∗ ∗ p < 0.001); no multiple-testing correction is applied. Classification and enrichment follow the recipe of Supple-
mentary Note 32. See Supplementary Note 33 for interpretation and confound analyses and Supplementary Table 30
for the full numeric results.
