Supplementary Figure 10: Tractability enrichment by CD4+ T-cell topology role and condition. Each cell
compares one topology role with all other classifiable genes in the same condition for overlap with a fixed Minikel
et al. [65] tractability list. Color gives the category-versus-rest log2 odds ratio; text gives the percentage and overlap
count within the topology role. Significance comes from a one-sided Fisher exact test followed by Benjamini–Hochberg
correction across the nine tractability lists separately within each condition–role family (∗ q < 0.05, ∗ ∗ q < 0.01,
∗∗∗ q < 10−4 ). Relay small-molecule enrichment is consistent across Rest, Stim8hr, and Stim48hr. See Supplementary
Note 34 and Supplementary Table 32 for the numerical results and annotation-depth control.

Supplementary Figure 11: Annotation depth explains most, but not all, relay-gene small-molecule tractability
enrichment. a, Alias-aware PubMed counts are higher both for small-molecule-tractable genes than for non-tractable
genes and for relay genes than for the remaining topology background; boxes show medians and interquartile ranges,
with whiskers at 1.5× the interquartile range and outliers omitted. b, The small-molecule-tractable rate for relay and
non-relay genes within quintiles of PubMed annotation depth. c, Relay log2 odds-ratio coeﬀicients and 95% confidence
intervals from crude logistic models and models adjusted for log10 (PubMed + 1). The small-molecule coeﬀicient
attenuates by 61% but remains significant (p = 0.0066); approved-drug enrichment attenuates by 70% and becomes
non-significant (p = 0.30); antibody tractability is not enriched in either model. The annotation-matched Cochran–
Mantel–Haenszel results agree (small molecule OR 1.25, p = 0.0086; approved drug OR 1.14, p = 0.54; antibody
OR 0.95, p = 0.51). PubMed counts above 200,000 are winsorized before regression and binning. See Supplementary
Note 34 and Supplementary Table 32.

Supplementary Figure 12: Relay-gene pathway enrichment across the stimulation time course. Every signifi-
cantly enriched term for CD4+ T-cell relay genes, ranked by adjusted p-value, in each of the three stimulation condi-
tions (Rest, Stim8hr, Stim48hr; one panel per condition). Each point is one enriched term plotted at its rank (x) against
− log10 (padj ) (y), with the eight most significant terms labelled. Enrichment is the same role-versus-all-other-measured-
genes contrast used in Figure 6k and described in Supplementary Note 35: GO:BP, GO:MF, and KEGG terms from
g:Profiler (g:SCS-adjusted p ≤ 0.05) merged with MSigDB hallmark sets (hypergeometric test, Benjamini–Hochberg
FDR ≤ 0.05), tested against the full measured transcriptome for that condition. Both the number of significant terms
(172, 293, and 149) and the identity of the top terms shift across the time course, from a poised, generically lymphoid
regulatory program at Rest, through acute activation hubs at Stim8hr, to committed lineage and viral/checkpoint regu-
lators at Stim48hr. Note that the y-axis scale differs between panels and that p-values are not strictly comparable across
the two enrichment engines, so terms are compared within panels rather than across them.

Supplementary Figure 13: DepMap essentiality across CD4+ T-cell topology roles. Genome-wide CRISPR essen-
tiality (DepMap, via Open Targets [61]) for the three functional genomics topology groups, assigned by the priority
rule relay > upstream > terminal (a gene is relay if it is a relay gene in any of the Rest/Stim8hr/Stim48hr conditions;
otherwise upstream if ever upstream; otherwise terminal), giving the same 762-gene any-condition relay set used in
the druggability analysis. Left: mean CRISPR gene-effect score (Chronos) per gene across ∼1,183 cell lines (more
negative = more essential; y-axis inverted so essential genes are at top); dashed/dotted guides mark the non-essential
(0) and common-essential (−1) reference levels. Right: percentage of genes classed DepMap common-essential per
group, with binomial standard-error bars and counts. Relay and upstream genes (both high out-degree) are statistically
indistinguishable from each other and both are markedly more essential than terminal genes; n per group reflects genes
with a DepMap record (relay 733, upstream 1,690, terminal 1,568; 3,991/4,187 assigned genes). See Supplementary
Note 36 for statistics.

Supplementary Prompt Note 1: StarEmbed dataset seed prompt for DISCOVERYAGENT.
# StarEmbed Classification Dataset - Train/Test Splits

This directory contains the training and test datasets for classification
tasks using multi-band light curve data.

## Dataset Overview

```
DatasetDict({
    train: Dataset({
        features: ['sourceid', 'bands_data', 'item_id', 'start', 'freq',
        'period', 'objid', 'csdr1_id', 'class_str'],
        num_rows: 29047
    })
    validation: Dataset({
        features: ['sourceid', 'bands_data', 'item_id', 'start', 'freq',
        'period', 'objid', 'csdr1_id', 'class_str'],
        num_rows: 4150
    })
})
```

**Note:** There is also a validation split available in the
`star_embed-validation.arrow` file.

---

## Dataset Structure

### Top-Level Features

| Feature | Type | Description |
|---------|------|-------------|
| `sourceid` | int64 | Unique source identifier |
| `bands_data` | dict | Multi-band photometric time series data (see below) |
| `item_id` | string | Item identifier |
| `start` | timestamp[us] | Start date/time of observations |
| `freq` | string | Sampling frequency (e.g., "1D" for daily) |
| `period` | float64 | Catalog period of the variable star |
| `objid` | string | Object identifier (cross-matched to Pan-STARRS1) |
| `csdr1_id` | string | Catalina Sky Survey Data Release 1 identifier |
| `class_str` | string | Classification label for the astronomical object |

### Multi-Band Data Structure (`bands_data`)

                                               117
The `bands_data` field contains photometric data for three bands: **g**,
**i**, and **r**. Each band has the following structure:

#### Per-Band Fields

| Field | Type | Description |
|-------|------|-------------|
| `target` | sequence of float64 | Magnitude measurements (brightness in AB
system) |
| `mjd` | sequence of float64 | Modified Julian Date for each observation |
| `past_feat_dynamic_real` | sequence of float64 | Magnitude error (1-sigma
uncertainty) |
| `feat_dynamic_real` | sequence of float64 | Time gap (delta_t) between
consecutive observations |
| `length` | int64 | Number of observations in this band |

### Complete Feature Schema

```json
{
  "features": {
    "sourceid": {
       "dtype": "int64",
      "_type": "Value"
    },
    "bands_data": {
       "g": {
         "target": {
           "feature": {"dtype": "float64", "_type": "Value"},
           "_type": "Sequence"
         },
         "mjd": {
           "feature": {"dtype": "float64", "_type": "Value"},
           "_type": "Sequence"
         },
         "past_feat_dynamic_real": {
           "feature": {"dtype": "float64", "_type": "Value"},
           "_type": "Sequence"
         },
         "feat_dynamic_real": {
           "feature": {"dtype": "float64", "_type": "Value"},
           "_type": "Sequence"
         },
         "length": {
           "dtype": "int64",
           "_type": "Value"
         }
      },
       "i": { /* same structure as g */ },
      "r": { /* same structure as g */ }
    },
    "item_id": {"dtype": "string", "_type": "Value"},
    "start": {"dtype": "timestamp[us]", "_type": "Value"},


                                      118
      "freq": {"dtype": "string", "_type": "Value"},
      "period": {"dtype": "float64", "_type": "Value"},
      "objid": {"dtype": "string", "_type": "Value"},
      "csdr1_id": {"dtype": "string", "_type": "Value"},
      "class_str": {"dtype": "string", "_type": "Value"}
  }
}
```

---

## Files in This Directory

- `star_embed-train-00000-of-00002.arrow` - Training data (part 1)
- `star_embed-train-00001-of-00002.arrow` - Training data (part 2)
- `star_embed-validation.arrow` - Validation data
- `README.txt` - This file

---

## Usage Notes

1. **Multi-band data**: Each object has observations in up to three
photometric bands (g, i, r). Not all objects may have data in all bands.

2. **Time series structure**: The data is organized as irregular time series,
with observations at different times captured in the `mjd` field.

3. **Classification labels**: The `class_str` field contains the astronomical
classification for each source (e.g., variable star types).

4. **Data loading**: Use the Hugging Face `datasets` library to load this
data:
   ```python
   from datasets import load_from_disk
   dataset = load_dataset("path/to/data")
   ```

Supplementary Prompt Note 2: ProteinGym dataset seed prompt for DISCOVERYAGENT.
# ProteinGym Deep Mutational Scanning Dataset

ProteinGym is an extensive set of Deep Mutational Scanning (DMS) assays
curated to enable thorough comparisons of various mutation effect predictors
indifferent regimes.
It contains a substitution benchmark which consists of the experimental
characterisation of missense variants in human proteins across various DMS
assays.

## Task and evaluation

You are to create a machine learning model to predict the DMS scores from the
protein sequence, structure, and/or MSA.
You will be evaluated on a held-out test set that you do not have access to.
This held-out test set may contain proteins not found in this set as well as
MSAs and structures that are only provided at test time.
You should build a model that outperforms other baseline methods on:
    1) Spearman correlation coefficient to ground-truth DMS scores
    2) Normalized discounted cumulative gain (NDCG) - a ranking metric that
    measures the correspondence of your ranked list to the list of ranked
    variants predicted for a protein in a given DMS screen

## File tree
Directory path: {datapath}
msa/:
    - Contains multiple sequence alignments for all proteins
    - The naming of the files contains information about what they contain
    - Example: DNJA1_HUMAN_2023-08-07_b07.a2m
        - Protein: DNJA1
        - Organism: Human
        - The other bits of the name are Metadata
pdb/:
    - Contains protein structures (predicted by AlphaFold 2) of all the
    proteins
    - Structures encoded in PDB files
    - Naming also carries meaning
    - Example: CD19_HUMAN.pdb
        - Protein: CD19
train/substitutions/:
    - Main sample files containing DMS scores on (protein, screen) pairs
    - Naming of files convey properties:
    - Example: DLG4_HUMAN_Faure_2021.csv
        - Protein: DLG4
        - Screen/study: Faure_2021
DMS_substitutions.csv:
    - This contains information on all the data across the substitions/
    screens + proteins
    - See below for a description of all the columns:

The meaning of each column in the ProteinGym reference files is provided
below:



                                       120
- DMS_id (str): Uniquely identifies each DMS assay in ProteinGym. It is
obtained as the concatenation of the UniProt ID of the mutated protein, the
first author name and the year of publication. If there are several datasets
with the same characteristics, another defining attribute of the assay is
added to preserve unicity.
- DMS_filename (str): Name of the processed DMS file.
- target_seq (str): Sequence of the target protein (reference sequence mutated
in the assay).
- seq_len (int): Length of the target protein sequence.
- includes_multiple_mutants (bool): Indicates whether the DMS contains
mutations that are multiple mutants. Substitution benchmark only.
- DMS_total_number_mutants (int): Number of rows of the DMS in ProteinGym.
- DMS_number_single_mutants (int): Number of single amino acid substitutions
in the DMS. Substitution benchmark only.
- DMS_number_multiple_mutants (int): Number of multiple amino acid
substitutions in the DMS. Substitution benchmark only.
- DMS_binarization_cutoff_ProteinGym (float): Cutoff used to divide fitness
scores into binary labels.
- DMS_binarization_method (str): Method used to decide the binarization cutoff
(manual or median).
- region_mutated (str): Region of the target protein that is mutated in the
DMS.
- MSA_filename (str): Name of the MSA file generated based on the reference
sequence mutated during the DMS experiment. Note that different reference
sequences may be used in different DMS experiments for the same protein. For
example, Giacomelli et al. (2018) and Kotler et al. (2018) used slightly
different reference sequences in their respective DMS experiments for the P53
protein. We generated different MSAs accordingly.
- MSA_start (int): Locates the beginning of the first sequence in the MSA with
respect to the target sequence. For example, if the MSA covers from position
10 to position 60 of the target sequence, then MSA_start is 10.
- MSA_end (int): Locates the end of the first sequence in the MSA with respect
to the target sequence. For example, if the MSA covers from position 10 to
position 60 of the target sequence, then MSA_end is 60.
- MSA_bitscore (float): Bitscore threshold used to generate the alignment
divided by the length of the target protein.
- MSA_theta (float): Hamming distance cutoff for sequence re-weighting.
- MSA_num_seqs (int): Number of sequences in the Multiple Sequence Alignment
(MSA) used in this work for this DMS.
- MSA_perc_cov (float): Percentage of positions of the MSA that had a coverage
higher than 70% (less than 30% gaps).
- MSA_num_cov (int): Number of positions of the MSA that had a coverage higher
than 70% (less than 30% gaps).
- MSA_N_eff (float): The effective number of sequences in the MSA defined as
the sum of the different sequence weights.
- MSA_N_eff_L (float): Neff / num_cov.
- MSA_num_significant (int): Number of evolutionary couplings that are
considered significant. Significance is defined by having more than 90%
probability of belonging to the log-normal distribution in a Gaussian Mixture
Model of normal and log-normal distributions.
- MSA_num_significant_L (float): MSA_num_significant / num_cov.
- raw_DMS_filename (str): Name of the raw DMS file.
- raw_DMS_phenotype_name (str): Name of the column in the raw DMS that we used
as fitness score.


                                     121
- raw_DMS_directionality (int): Sign of the correlation between the
DMS_phenotype column values and protein fitness in the raw DMS files. In any
given DMS, the directionality is 1 if higher values of the measurement are
associated with higher fitness, and -1 otherwise. For simplicity, we adjusted
directionality in the final ProteinGym benchmarks so that a higher value of
DMS_score is always associated with higher fitness. Consequently, correlations
between model scores and the final DMS_score values should always be positive
(unless the predictions from the considered model are worse than random for
that DMS).
- raw_DMS_mutant_column (str): Name of the column in the raw DMS that
indicates which mutants were assayed.

Supplementary Prompt Note 3: Extragalactic astronomy task seed prompt for DISCOVERYA-
GENT.
Your task is to predict galaxy redshift (distance) directly from resolved
imaging (photometric redshift estimation, focused on low redshift (z < 0.03)
where morphology/size/structure carry distance information beyond integrated
magnitudes/colors).
DO NOT use any metadata that is provided in the data, only the images.




                                        123
Supplementary Prompt Note 4: Extragalactic astronomy dataset seed prompt for DISCOV-
ERYAGENT.
# The Image-Based Redshift Estimate Challenge:

This repository describes the challenge and dataset for estimating redshifts
based on images.

## Context
A critical task for most extragalactic, astronomical studies is to estimate
the redshift to a galaxy. Redshifts are typically measured using spectroscopic
observations. However, it is not possible to obtain spectroscopy for every
galaxy due to the large observation time requirement, particularly for the
faintest galaxies. Instead, there has been substantial effort to develop
methodology to estimate redshifts based on broadband imaging of galaxies,
which is much easier to obtain for large samples. We will call redshifts
measured from broadband imaging “photometric redshifts” or “photo-zs”, and
those from spectroscopy “spectroscopic redshifts” or “spec-zs”.

Traditionally, photo-z methods rely on integrated photometric quantities.
Using either apertures (e.g., ellipses) or model fits to galaxy images, total
magnitudes and colors are extracted (see, e.g., the photutils tool for
examples of how this is done). There are correlations between the total
magnitudes, colors, and redshifts, albeit with large uncertainties and,
sometimes, strong degeneracies. These methods are generally not developed with
the faintest or lowest mass galaxies in mind.

Methodology to produce precise and accurate photometric redshifts for low mass
galaxies is essential to many areas of astrophysics. We are particularly
interested in low mass galaxies with Mstar < 10^9.25 M_sun. We are
particularly interested in two questions:

* **Transient astronomy**: with the rapid growth of high cadence, sensitive,
wide-field optical imaging, we now have the ability to study populations of
transients in real-time. Dwarf galaxies host a wide range of fascinating, and
rare, transients, including superluminous supernovae, gamma-ray bursts,
luminous fast blue optical transients (LFBOTs), and potentially Ca-rich
transients. Of particular interest, they may host intermediate mass black
holes, which can produce flaring through tidal disruption events. The rate of
tidal disruption events in dwarf galaxies may depend on stellar population,
and could potentially be biased towards dwarfs with less star formation. The
luminosities of these transients (rather than their fluxes) also contains
substantial information about their origins. Determining the transients of
most interest that are worthy of using telescope time to follow-up, as well as
measuring the rates of transient populations are fundamental questions in
transient astronomy. With a catalog of nearby dwarf galaxies, where we
understand the completeness and selection, we can begin to answer some of
these questions.




                                        124
* **Galaxy evolution**: A key constraint on models of galaxy evolution is the
quenched fraction of galaxies as a function of stellar mass and environment.
Measuring this requires reliable distance or redshift estimates for large
samples of low-mass galaxies. However, spectroscopic redshifts are expensive
to obtain and are particularly incomplete for quenched dwarf galaxies, which
are fainter at fixed stellar mass and therefore harder to target
spectroscopically. As a result, current measurements of quenched fractions and
environmental trends are limited by selection effects and incompleteness.
Galaxy images contain substantial information about distance through surface
brightness, angular size, morphology, color gradients, and internal structure.
If a model could take a galaxy image cutout as input and predict a redshift,
this information could be propagated directly into downstream analyses. These
redshift estimates can be folded into measurements of quenched fractions,
stellar mass functions, and environmental dependencies. More broadly, applying
such a model to wide-field imaging surveys would enable the construction of a
three-dimensional map of the nearby Universe using imaging data alone. A model
that produces accurate and well-characterized redshift estimates with high
completeness and purity would therefore be a powerful tool for studies of
low-mass galaxy evolution.

For nearby galaxies (z < 0.03), galaxies are often (but not always) spatially
resolved in imaging data. The spatially resolved features contain rich
distance information that integrated photometric quantities alone cannot
recover:

Angular size: Larger apparent sizes typically indicate closer galaxies
Internal structure: Spiral arms, star-forming regions, and morphological
features are resolved
Surface brightness fluctuations: The degree of smoothness versus clumpiness
encodes distance-dependent information.

These are not the only properties, and may not be the most important. All
aspects of the images should be explored. In addition, the integrated
photometric properties, which do contain additional information beyond these
spatially resolved features, are extracted directly from the image data. This
means that the full image is a strictly richer data representation for
redshift inference, especially at low redshift. This challenge focuses on
leveraging images to estimate redshifts, rather than relying on handcrafted
photometric features.

## The problem
We want to develop a methodology that can produce a complete and pure catalog
of galaxies within z < 0.03 with photo-z estimates, where completeness and
purity are with respect to the spectroscopic redshift catalog. Our main
criteria is that the model should be at least 95% complete and as pure as
possible. While you will evaluate the metrics using all galaxies, we are
particularly concerned about dwarf galaxies, and so you will test that the
performance for dwarf galaxies (identified by the IS_DWARF flag) is also good.
The photo-z estimates should be accurate (as defined by the metrics below). We
also want to understand the completeness of the photo-z catalog the model will
produce as a function of galaxy color, magnitude, and redshift (see required
results section below).




                                     125
### Training dataset overview
Directory: {datapath}
The dataset is here: galaxy_images_128_v2_train.h5 and a validation set is
given here: galaxy_images_128_v2_val.h5.

The dataset contains galaxies from the Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument
(DESI) Data Release (DR1) with r-band magnitude of r < 21.2 .

The sample is based on the Darragh-Ford z < 0.03 photometric cuts. The
resulting training sample is
* A population of dwarf galaxies with Mstar < 10^9.25 M_sun, with stellar mass
roughly estimated using prescription from de los Reyes et al. 2025. These will
be galaxies that tend to be fainter and at low redshifts.
* Galaxies satisfying Darragh-Ford et al. 2023 “z < 0.03 complete”
photometric cuts. These photometric cuts were designed to identify galaxies at
z < 0.03 with low purity with many higher redshift galaxies ( z ≲ 0.15 )
included in this sample as well.

Basic quality and cleaning cuts were applied to the source catalogs prior to
image construction.
The training sample is not necessarily balanced, and you should explore
methods of weighting/balancing the dataset without using changing the dataset
manually.

#### Image Cutouts
For each galaxy, an image cutout is provided with the following properties:
* Image shape: 3 × 128 × 128
* Each of the 3 layers corresponds to different photometric filters: g, r, z
* Pixel scale: 0.262 arcsec/pixel
* Centered on the galaxy coordinates.
* These are coadded images are from Legacy Surveys imaging DR9.
* The images are oriented with North towards the top, however, distance is
(and should be in your result) distance invariant.

Note that there will be other background galaxies, stars, image artifacts in
the image cutouts, however, as noted before, the cutouts are centered
(although the centering algorithm may be imperfect) on the galaxy whose
redshift we know.

Each galaxy has a cutout of size 128x128, however, the same performance (and
better memory usage) could be achieved with a smaller cutout like 64x64.
However, we are not sure which cutout size is better.

The image cutouts are already fully reduced, flux calibrated and science
ready, so when training the model you can either use them directly, or convert
them to rgb images using the color transformation provided in sdss_rgb
function below. Many previous ML methods on galaxy images transform these
images to RGB images where the different filters are combined using the color
transformation presented in the sdss_rgb function. Use raw g/r/z channels for
training by default; sdss_rgb is for visualization unless you justify
otherwise.
```
def sdss_rgb(imgs, bands=["g","r","z"], scales=dict(g=(2,6.0), r=(1,3.4),
z=(0,2.2)),m = 0.03):


                                     126
      '''
      Function to convert the 3x128x128 images into RGB images using a color
      transformation
      '''
      rgbscales = {'u': (2,1.5), #1.0,
                   'g': (2,2.5),
                   'r': (1,1.5),
                   'i': (0,1.0),
                   'z': (0,0.4), #0.3
                   }

      if scales is not None:
          rgbscales.update(scales)

      I = 0
      for img,band in zip(imgs, bands):
          plane,scale = rgbscales[band]
          img = np.maximum(0, img * scale + m)
          I = I + img
      I /= len(bands)

      # b,g,r = [rimg * rgbscales[b] for rimg,b in zip(imgs, bands)]
      # r = np.maximum(0, r + m)
      # g = np.maximum(0, g + m)
      # b = np.maximum(0, b + m)
      # I = (r+g+b)/3.
      Q = 20
      fI = np.arcsinh(Q * I) / np.sqrt(Q)
      I += (I == 0.) * 1e-6
      H,W = I.shape
      rgb = np.zeros((H,W,3), np.float32)
      for img,band in zip(imgs, bands):
          plane,scale = rgbscales[band]
          rgb[:,:,plane] = (img * scale + m) * fI / I

      # R = fI * r / I
      # G = fI * g / I
      # B = fI * b / I
      # # maxrgb = reduce(np.maximum, [R,G,B])
      # # J = (maxrgb > 1.)
      # # R[J] = R[J]/maxrgb[J]
      # # G[J] = G[J]/maxrgb[J]
      # # B[J] = B[J]/maxrgb[J]
      # rgb = np.dstack((R,G,B))
      rgb = np.clip(rgb, 0, 1)

      return rgb
```

## Metadata

Each image is associated with catalog-level metadata including:
* DESI object identifier TARGETID (int64)
* Right ascension (RA) in degrees (float64)


                                       127
* Declination (DEC) in degrees (float64)
* Spectroscopic redshift (Z) (float32)
* Magnitude of galaxy in the g-band (MAG_G) (float32)
* Magnitude of galaxy in the r-band (MAG_R) (float32)
* Magnitude of galaxy in the z-band (MAG_Z) (float32)
* Boolean flag indicated whether the galaxy is a dwarf galaxy or not
(IS_DWARF) (boolean)

Do not use the IS_DWARF or Z metadata as an input feature, these are only for
metric evaluation and producing the required results. Do not use
TARGETID,RA,DEC as input features in the training. You may use MAG_G, MAG_R,
MAG_Z as input features.

## Task Metrics:

The goal of this challenge is to predict point estimates of galaxy redshift
from imaging data. We do not consider full redshift probability distribution
functions (PDFs); each model must output a single photometric redshift
estimate per galaxy. We are most interested in identifying galaxies within z <
0.03. Model performance is evaluated by comparing the predicted photometric
redshift to the true spectroscopic redshift.
* Prediction bias defined as $\langle \frac{\Delta z}{1 + z_{\rm spec}}
\rangle, ;$, i.e. the average value of the prediction error.
* Normalized Median Absolute Deviation ($\sigma_{\rm NMAD}$) defined below.
This is a robust measure of the spread of prediction errors.
$$1.4826 \times \mathrm{Median}\left(\left| \frac{\Delta z}{1 +
z_{\text{spec}}} - \text{Median}\left(\frac{\Delta z}{1 +
z_{\text{spec}}}\right) \right|\right)$$
* Fraction of Outliers ($f_{\rm outlier}$) defined as the fraction of photo-z
predictions for which $\left| \frac{\Delta z}{1 + z_{\text{spec}}} \right| >
0.05$, i.e. the fraction of cases where the prediction error is very high. We
chose the threshold of 0.05 to easily compare our results with other similar
works.
* The completeness of the sample of galaxies with photo z < 0.03 (defined as
the fraction of all galaxies with zspec < 0.03 in training dataset that have
zphot < 0.03 in the final catalog) should be at least 95%. The purity (defined
as the fraction of galaxies with zphot < 0.03 that also have zspec < 0.03 in
the training dataset) should be as high as possible while ensuring
completeness.

## Required results:

We require that you produce certain key plots and test results

* Make a completeness vs. purity curve (like an ROC plot) for selecting z<0.03
galaxies where completeness and purity are defined above in the task metrics
section. Make this plot for all the variations we suggest below (split by
magnitude ranges, split by color like g-r < 0.3 and g-r > 0.3,and split by is
dwarf or not.)
* Test the performance as a function of magnitude range. Evaluate the metrics
in each of these bins separately:
| Bin | Range | Rationale |
|-----|-------|-----------|
| A | r < 19 | How model does on bright galaxies |


                                     128
| B | 19 < r < 20.5 | How model does on slightly fainter galaxies |
| C | 20.5 < r < 21.2 | Faint end of current DESI targeting |
* Test if there is any variation within the bin.
* Test the performance as a function of galaxy color g-r = MAG_G - MAG_R.
Evaluate the metrics in color bins of size 0.1 separately to search for
variations for redder vs bluer galaxies.
* Test the performance for dwarf galaxies versus not dwarfs, identified by the
IS_DWARF flag.
* Report Purity at Fixed Completeness and Vice Versa: report two standardized
metrics, both for the entire sample and per magnitude bin
**Purity at completeness = 95%**: How clean is the sample when we insist on
finding nearly all low-z galaxies?
**Completeness at purity = 90%**: How many low-z galaxies do we recover when
we insist on a clean sample?
Perform these tests for both dwarfs and the full sample

## Important points to remember:
Follow standard astronomical practices as much as possible, and use standard
astronomical codes to compute any needed values rather than writing your own
We have provided magnitude values, but these may be incorrect for the most
highly resolved objects (the most nearby objects, but not always). Do not use
this information about bad fluxes in the ML method as a discriminatory tool.
However for the more compact sources, the provided photometry and color
information is accurate. Do not recompute fluxes or the flux calibration
between sources on your own.
The image centering may also be wrong for highly resolved objects, so your
algorithm should be robust to centering errors.
Be careful about normalizing images. Normalizing individual images will remove
flux and color information, which are very important for photometric
redshifts. Best practice is to do channel/filter-wise normalization across
your entire dataset, as this will preserve the relative filter differences for
each galaxy. Do not normalize each image to have zero mean and unit variance
independently. Do not rescale each channel of each image independently.
Relative colors between filters encode physical information and per-image
normalization will degrade model performance.
Make plots as much as possible. As one example (but you should extend this as
much as possible), do not just quote the prediction bias, but make a plot
showing the prediction bias as a function of redshift (including for different
subsets of the training set, like described in required results).
Try to make apples-to-apples comparisons to past work on similar problems as
much as possible, in particular Darragh-Ford et al. 2023 and the other works
listed below
All completeness/purity metrics in this task are computed with respect to the
training dataset provided, and do not attempt to infer completeness relative
to the underlying photometric population.

Background Material




                                     129
"Target Selection and Sample Characterization for the DESI LOW-Z Secondary
Target Program" This paper introduces the DESI LOW-Z Secondary Target Survey,
which combines the wide-area capabilities of the Dark Energy Spectroscopic
Instrument (DESI) with an efficient, low-redshift target selection method.
Their selection consists of a set of color and surface brightness cuts,
combined with modern machine learning methods, to target low-redshift dwarf
galaxies (z < 0.03) between 19 < r < 21 with high completeness. We employ a
convolutional neural network (CNN) to select high-priority targets.
"Extending the SAGA Survey (xSAGA). I. Satellite Radial Profiles as a Function
of Host-galaxy Properties" Using spectroscopic redshift catalogs from the SAGA
Survey as a training data set, they optimized a convolutional neural network
(CNN) to identify z < 0.03 galaxies from more-distant objects using image
cutouts from the DESI Legacy Imaging Surveys.
"Photometric redshifts from SDSS images with an interpretable deep capsule
network" This work uses a capsule network to train a neural network that is
better suited for identifying morphological features of the input images than
traditional CNNs. They use a deep capsule network trained on ugriz images,
spectroscopic redshifts, and Galaxy Zoo spiral/elliptical classifications of
∼400 000 Sloan Digital Sky Survey galaxies to do photometric redshift
estimation. We achieve a photometric redshift prediction accuracy and a
fraction of catastrophic outliers that are comparable to or better than
current methods for SDSS main galaxy sample-like data sets ( r < 17.8 and z ≲
0.4 ) while requiring less data and fewer trainable parameters. Code:
https://biprateep.de/encapZulate-1/
“Self Supervised similarity search for large scientific datasets ” This paper
uses 42 million galaxy images from the latest data release of the Dark Energy
Spectroscopic Instrument (DESI) Legacy Imaging Surveys, to train a
self-supervised model to distill low-dimensional representations that are
robust to symmetries, uncertainties, and noise in each image. This model is
very useful for similarity searches. Code:
https://github.com/georgestein/ssl-legacysurvey
“DeepDISC-photoz: Deep Learning-Based Photometric Redshift Estimation for
Rubin LSST” This paper trains a photo-z estimator on simulated LSST data from
the second Data Challenge (DC2). They produce probabilistic photometric
redshifts directly from the simulated, multi-band images. They find that their
image-based estimator outperforms feature-based estimators, both for point
estimates and probabilistic metrics. They find that the signal-to-noise of the
images is the primary limiting factor.
Code: https://github.com/LSSTDESC/rail_deepdisc

                    CENSUS & SURVEY DATA — REFERENCE GUIDE
============================================================================= ⌋
===

This document describes all data accessible through the acs_agent package,
which provides three backends: the Census Bureau ACS API, the Census Bureau
PUMS API, and IPUMS microdata via ipumspy.

Downloaded files are stored in the acs_downloads/ directory.
NOTE: Not all data may be available locally — some queries require live API
calls or IPUMS extract submissions.

============================================================================= ⌋
===
 1. OVERVIEW
============================================================================= ⌋
===

The American Community Survey (ACS) is an ongoing, nationwide survey conducted
by the U.S. Census Bureau to provide detailed, up-to-date information about
the
social, economic, housing, and demographic characteristics of the U.S.
population. Unlike the decennial census, which counts every resident once per
decade, the ACS is administered continuously to a sample of households each
year, producing timely estimates that reflect changes in communities over
time.

The ACS collects data on age, sex, race and ethnicity, education, income,
employment, commuting patterns, health insurance coverage, housing
characteristics, and housing costs. Data are released as one-year and
five-year
estimates, with the latter providing greater statistical reliability for
smaller
geographic areas (census tracts, towns, rural communities).

Beyond the ACS, the acs_agent package also provides access to labor force
surveys (CPS) and health surveys (NHIS, MEPS) through IPUMS, as well as
historical census microdata back to 1850.

===
 2. DATA BACKENDS

Census Bureau ACS API
  - Real-time access to published ACS summary tables (pre-tabulated).
  - Coverage: 2005-present.
  - Geography: nation, state, county, tract, place, metro, zip code (ZCTA).
  - Instant responses.

Census Bureau PUMS API
  - Real-time access to Public Use Microdata Sample (individual records).
  - Coverage: Recent ACS years.
  - Instant responses for queries and tabulations.

IPUMS (via ipumspy)
  - Extract-based access to harmonized historical microdata.
  - Four collections: USA, CPS, NHIS, MEPS.
  - Responses range from instant (metadata) to minutes (extracts).

 4. DATA SOURCES — PUMS MICRODATA (Census Bureau PUMS API)

The Public Use Microdata Sample provides individual- and household-level

Coverage: Recent ACS years (real-time API)
Geography: Public Use Microdata Areas (PUMAs), states

  Demographics        AGEP (age), SEX, RAC1P (race), HISP (Hispanic origin)

                      RMSP (rooms), BDSP (bedrooms), MHP (mobile home costs)
  Insurance           HINS1-HINS7 (insurance coverage types)
  Commuting           JWTR (transportation mode), JWMNP (travel time)

 5. DATA SOURCES — IPUMS MICRODATA COLLECTIONS

IPUMS provides harmonized individual-level microdata from multiple survey
programs. Variables are coded consistently across years and decades.

Available collections:   usa, cps, nhis, meps

 5.1 IPUMS USA (collection: usa)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Harmonized individual and household records from every U.S. Census


                                      134
(1850-2010) and the American Community Survey (2000-present). Variables are
coded consistently across decades, so "EDUC" means the same thing in 1940 as
in 2022.

Topics:   Demographics, income, education, employment, housing, migration,
          disability, health insurance, ancestry, commuting, fertility

Time span:    1850-present
Geography:    State, county, metro, PUMA

Why use IPUMS USA over the direct Census PUMS API?
  - Historical data back to 1850 (PUMS API only covers recent years)
  - Harmonized variables with consistent coding across decades
  - Full-count data available for some historical censuses

Decision flow:

  Is the data from the current ACS (2009+)?

             YES -> Census API (search_tables, download_table,
             get_quick_stats)
             NO -> Need individual records?
                      YES -> Census PUMS API (pums_get_records, pums_tabulate)
                      NO -> Census API (download_variables)
    NO -> Is it individual-level records?
             YES -> IPUMS microdata (ipums_get_microdata) -- 2-30 min
             NO -> Not currently supported (aggregate historical data
                    would require NHGIS, which is not enabled)

Supplementary Prompt Note 8: Functional genomics dataset seed prompt for DISCOVERYA-
GENT.
# Data overview

This dataset provides a high-resolution, genome-scale map of how expressed
genes regulate the behavior of primary human immune cells. Unlike studies that
rely on immortalized model cell lines (for example, K562 or Jurkat), this
atlas was built using **primary cells** isolated from four healthy human
donors. Using primary cells preserves the biological relevance needed to study
complex immune traits and autoimmune disease susceptibility.
The paper describing this study is in data/gwcd4.pdf.

---

### Experimental Design and Generation

The data was generated using a scalable, probe-based **perturb-seq platform**.
Researchers employed **CRISPR interference (CRISPRi)** to systematically knock
down **12,748 genes**, comprising nearly all expressed genes in T cells plus
all known human transcription factors. To capture immune dynamics, cells were
profiled across three physiological states:

- **Resting state (Rest):** Cells expanded ex vivo and allowed to reach a
quiescent state without active stimulation.
- **Early activation (Stim8hr):** Cells 8 hours post-restimulation, capturing
immediate-early regulatory circuits of the T cell receptor (TCR).
- **Late activation (Stim48hr):** Cells 48 hours post-restimulation,
reflecting transition to metabolic expansion and effector functions.

The team leveraged **10x Genomics Chromium Flex** technology to "superload"
and profile approximately **22 million cells** with high sensitivity, even for
low-abundance transcripts.

---

### Data Filtering and Statistical Rigor

Processing involved multiple filters to ensure results reflect true biological
"trans-effects" (the effect of a regulator on downstream genes) rather than
technical noise:

- **Single-cell quality control:** Cells were filtered by mitochondrial
content and genes detected to retain high-quality transcriptomes. Only cells
with a single, confidently assigned guide RNA were used to avoid confounding
effects from multiple perturbations.
- **On-target verification:** Measured "cis-effects" to confirm CRISPRi
reduced expression of the intended target gene.
- **Off-target safeguards:** Flagged potential off-target effects by checking
whether a perturbation silenced neighboring genes within 10 kb of the target.
- **Pseudobulk aggregation:** Grouped cells by donor, condition, and guide to
increase statistical power and enable robust differential expression analysis
with **DESeq2**, accounting for donor-level variability.



                                        141
---

### Biological Context

This dataset is designed as a foundational resource for decoding human immune
function. It identifies clusters of regulators governing core cellular
processes (for example, citrate metabolism, mTORC1 signaling, and vesicle
trafficking) and reveals how these networks rewire during an immune response.
By comparing perturbations to natural genetic variation in large populations
(such as the UK Biobank), the data helps explain how specific genetic regions
contribute to traits like lymphocyte counts or autoimmune disease risk.

## File tree:
Directory path: {datapath}
Filename: GWCD4i.DE_stats.h5ad

This AnnData object contains genome-wide differential expression results from
a perturb-seq experiment in CD4+ T cells. Each observation represents a single
perturbation (perturbed gene) tested in a specific culture condition (n_obs =
33,983). Each variable is a measured gene in the transcriptome (n_vars =
10,282).

**Observation Metadata (.obs)**
Annotations for each perturbation-condition pair:
`target_contrast_gene_name`: Name of the perturbed gene
`culture_condition`: culture condition (Rest, Stim8hr, Stim48hr)
`target_contrast`: Unique identifier for the perturbed gene
`chunk`: differential expression processing group identifier
`n_cells_target`: Number of cells with targeting guide for the perturbed gene
`n_up_genes`: Count of significantly upregulated genes (10% FDR)
`n_down_genes`: Count of significantly downregulated genes (10% FDR)
`n_total_de_genes`: Total number of significantly differentially expressed
genes (10% FDR)
`ontarget_effect_size`: Effect size of the perturbation on its intended target
gene
`ontarget_significant`: Boolean indicating whether on-target knockdown was
significant (10% FDR)
`target_baseMean`: Mean baseline expression of the target gene
`offtarget_flag`: Flag indicating potential off-target effects (TSS within 10
kb with significant down-regulation)
`n_total_genes_category`: Category based on number of trans-effects
`ontarget_effect_category`: Category based on on-target / off-target effects
`n_downstream`: Number of genes significantly affected by this perturbation,
excluding on-target effect (incoming trans-effects)

**Variable Metadata (.var)**
Annotations for each measured gene:
`gene_ids`: Gene identifiers (e.g., Ensembl IDs)
`gene_name`: Gene symbols

**Variable Matrices (.varm)**
Summary statistics for measured genes across conditions:




                                     142
`measured_genes_stats_Stim8hr`: Gene-level statistics for 8-hour stimulation
condition
`measured_genes_stats_Stim48hr`: Gene-level statistics for 48-hour stimulation
condition
`measured_genes_stats_Rest`: Gene-level statistics for resting/unstimulated
condition

**Data Layers (.layers)**
Differential expression statistics for each perturbation-gene pair (from
DESeq2):

`log_fc`: Log2 fold change
`p_value`: Raw p-values from differential expression testing
`adj_p_value`: FDR-adjusted p-values
`baseMean`: Mean normalized expression of the gene across cells
`lfcSE`: Standard error of log fold change
`zscore`: Z-scores for differential expression (logFC / lfcSE)
